And as the devil draws those who obey him from the heavens to earthly things and to sins, where there is no foundation, because evil is without substance, and always casts them down into worse things: so the evil hearer, whether he be initiated in the mysteries of Christ, or wholly a stranger, cast down as it were into a well without a bottom, finds not where to hold himself, but when he has come into the depth of evils, he despises.

VERS. 48.-- *He digged deep.* He digs deep who by the precepts of humility roots out utterly from the hearts of his own all earthly things, that he may not serve God for the sake of anything fleeting, that he may have in them an unshaken dwelling. *He laid a foundation.* Foundations in the plural, the doctors; in the singular, the foundation, the doctor of doctors [0267B] Christ.

Morally. The foundations of the house are the intentions of a good manner of life, which through Christian humility, the rubbish of superfluous thoughts having been drawn off, the perfect hearer sets within himself, doing spiritually in himself what Christ does generally in the whole Church.

*A flood.* This flood he elsewhere calls the gates of hell. *And the gates of hell shall not prevail against it [n: (Matth. XVI)]*. This flood happens in three ways, because each man is tempted by his own concupiscence, or by the wickedness of false brethren, or by the open assault of those without. *Beaten upon.* By the rush of the river can be understood the crisis of the last judgment, when each Church has been consummated. *Everyone that exalteth himself [0267C] shall be humbled, and he that humbleth himself shall be exalted [n: (Luc. XIV)]*. *That house.* Our several houses are daily beaten by the unquiet of unclean spirits, or of wicked men, or of one's own mind, or of the flesh. And in so far as they trust in their own strength, they are bowed down: in so far as they cleave to the most unconquered rock, they cannot be shaken.


## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

VERS. 49.-- *Upon the earth,* etc. The devil builds upon the earth the world which is set in wickedness, because he draws it to the love of earthly things. He builds without a foundation, because sin does not subsist in its own nature, since evil is not a substance, which nevertheless, wherever it happens, grows together in a good nature, or without a foundation, that is, without a bottom. As therefore he who is plunged into a well is held at the bottom of the well, so [0267D] the fallen soul stands as it were in a certain place of the bottom, if it holds itself within some measure of sin. But since it cannot be content with the sin into which it falls, while it is daily led to worse things, as in a well into which it has fallen it does not find a bottom whereon it may be fixed. *It fell down, and there was made a ruin,* etc. [var: Vulg. Lc 6:49 reads *et continuo cecidit: et facta est ruina domus illius magna*, "and immediately it fell, and the ruin of that house was great."] Every conscience which does not remain with its hope fixed in God is not able to persist in temptations, and is the more driven the more it is disjoined from things above in those things which are of the world. And truly all the evil, or the feignedly good, become worse when any temptation presses in.



## CHAPTER VII.

VERS. 1.-- *When he had fulfilled.* Beautifully, where he has completed his precepts, [0268A] he teaches the form of carrying out his precepts. For at once the servant of a Gentile centurion is offered to the Lord to be healed, in whom the Gentiles, who were held sick in worldly servitude, are set forth as those to be saved by the benefit of God. *He entered into Capharnaum. Of a centurion,* etc. He did not enter before he had brought his words to an end, although he did not enter immediately after these words. For before he entered, in that very interval the leper was cleansed, as Matthew sets it down.

VERS. 3.-- *He sent to him the elders,* etc. By divine providence the elders of the Jews are sent, and in their presence the sick man is healed, that they may be without excuse if they have refused to believe.

The centurion sends friends, lest by his own presence he should seem to burden the Lord's modesty, and to have challenged [0268B] service with service. But it signifies that we who from among the Gentiles believe in the Lord cannot ourselves come to him whom we cannot see in the flesh, but to him who sits at the Father's right hand. The Gentile people, like a centurion, is thronged about with the soldiery of the virtues and with the perfection of spiritual things, which is signified in the hundred. Lofty, seeking nothing earthly from the Lord for himself and for his own, but only the joys of eternal salvation: and these make supplication to the Lord for servants, that is, for those who are still pressed down in fear by the spirit of servitude, that, as they are little by little advanced to higher things, *perfect love may cast out fear [n: (I Joan. IV)]*.

Matthew says that the centurion came near, which is to be understood of faith. For by faith one truly draws near to God, and that faith is commended in the centurion by the Lord: [0268C] *I have not found so great faith in Israel.* But Luke follows out the order in which it was done, that we might be compelled to understand in what manner Matthew says he came near.

VERS. 5.-- *Our nation.* Mystically. The Gentile people (whose figure the centurion bears) has Jesus not far from the house, although it does not dare to invite him under the roof, because, *near to them that fear him is his salvation.* He who rightly uses the natural law, so as to work the good things he knows, draws near thereby to him who is truly good. But to those whom the crimes of Gentile error had bound, that can be fitted which is said elsewhere: *for some of these came from afar [n: (Marc. VIII)]*.

VERS. 6.-- *And Jesus went.* Being about to heal powerfully by his word alone, he deigns, for the commendation of humility, to visit [0268D] the sick man; and he who would not go to heal the ruler's son, lest he should seem to have honoured riches, goes to a servant, lest he should be thought to have despised the servile condition. Faith shines out in his works while he heals, but humility works more in the affections while he goes. *I am not worthy.* Because of the conscience of his Gentile life, he thought himself to be burdened rather than helped by the Lord's condescension, and that he could not have Christ as a guest, since, although endowed with faith, he was nevertheless not yet steeped in the sacraments. But because divine grace knows how to give what human weakness does not presume, as a figure of the Gentile people he deserved, not yet catechized, both that his faith should be praised by the Lord and that his servant should be healed.

VERS. 8.-- *For I also,* etc. If I, a man set under another, [0269A] can command those beneath me, how much more thou, who, having angels serving thee over all things, canst through their ministries, without the presence of thy body, say to sickness that it depart, and it will depart, and to health that it come, and it will come.

VERS. 9.-- *Not even in Israel.* This man's faith is set even before the more chosen and those who see God. Even in this one man the faith of the Gentiles is preferred to Israel.

VERS. 10.-- *And they returned.* Matthew explains it more plainly, that when the Lord said: *As thou hast believed, so be it done to thee; let the boy be healed [n: (Matth. VIII)]* [var: Vulg. Matt 8:13 "Et sanatus est puer in illa hora," and the boy was healed in that hour; the printed reading has the subjunctive]. But it is the custom of blessed Luke to abridge what has been plainly set out by others, or even of set purpose to pass over what has been briefly said by others, or to elucidate more skilfully what has been omitted. *They found the servant.* The master believing, the servant is healed, because the merit of the master [0269B] can avail even for servants, not only by the merit of faith, but also by zeal for discipline.

VERS. 11.-- *And it came to pass,* etc. After the sick man healed he raises the dead, because sickness goes before death.

*Which is called Naim.* Naim is a city of Galilee at the second milestone from Thabor toward the south, next to Endor, which is a large village. And Naim is interpreted *a wave* or *a commotion.*

VERS. 12.-- *And when he drew near,* etc. [n: (BEDA.)] When the Word made flesh, the Gentile people through the gates of faith, etc., *as far as* which he commands both meanwhile in the few of the Jews converted, and at last in the fulness.

[0269C] *Behold a dead man was carried out,* etc. The dead man, who is carried out before many outside the gate, signifies him who sins criminally, and who does not cover his sin in the chamber of the heart, but by the evidence of deed or of speech publishes it abroad to others, as it were through the doors of his own city. Whom mother Church bewails as an only son, for although she is gathered out of many persons, yet mother Church is one virgin, while the sons are single. The gate by which he is carried out is some one of the senses, by which a man falls into sin. As he who looks in order to lust, and he who [lends himself] to hearing idle or filthy things, and he who lends his tongue to quarrels.

*The only son to his mother. [n: (AMBR.)]* Adam is carried from the earth by the four elements, of which man consists. The bier is touched, etc., *as far as* when he thinks upon avarice, or when for excess of fever [0269D] he cannot be touched.

VERS. 13.-- *And a crowd.* Many with the Lord, many with the widow, so that, the miracle being seen, many may become praisers and witnesses of God.

VERS. 15.-- *And he sat up.* The Lord raised three dead: the daughter of the ruler of the synagogue in the house, that is, in thought; the only son of his mother at the gate, that is, in word; Lazarus in the tomb, that is, in deed.

VERS. 16.-- *He hath visited.* Since he both appointed the Word to be embodied once, and daily sends the Holy Spirit into the hearts of men, that they may be raised up.

VERS. 18.-- *And they told. [n: (AMBR.)]* Not with a simple heart, but at the prompting of envy, etc., *as far as* [0270A] and, being corrected, they may believe in Jesus and, at their master's questioning, say for themselves: *Thou art he that is to come.*

[n: (BED., GREG.)] He does not say, who hast come, etc., *as far as* because a lapse of love does not hinder faith, for such a lapse is religious.

VERS. 22.-- *And answering he said to them: Go and relate to John what you have seen,* etc. [var: Vulg. Luke 7:22 "quae audistis et vidistis," what you have heard and seen] Jesus, knowing that no one can fully believe without the Gospel, because as faith begins from the Old Testament, so it is fulfilled in the New. Being questioned about himself, he signified what he was, not by any word, but by his deeds, answering not to the things about which he was questioned, but to the scandal of the messengers.

VERS. 23.-- *Blessed.* As if he should say: I do indeed marvellous things, but I do not disdain to suffer abject ones, and men must beware lest they despise death in me, who [0270B] venerate the signs.

*He that shall not. [n: (GREG.)]* He who shall not have doubted of the power of the godhead. This is not against John, whom he presently commends so highly, but he calls back from faithlessness those who believe not, and he sets forth to John what he was asking, because *God of saving; and of the Lord, of the Lord are the issues from death [n: (Psal. LXVII)]*. So many signs and mighty works having been seen, the faithful man could not doubt concerning his death, but only wonder; but the mind of the unbelieving bore a scandal in him, when it saw him dying even after the miracles.

VERS. 24.-- *What.* When he had admonished John's disciples that they must believe in the Lord's cross, turning to the crowds he summons the poor to virtue, lest, lifted up in heart, unstable in mind, weak in counsel, they should prefer specious and fleeting things to things eternally useful, but that they should rather take up the cross [0270C] than the world's trappings. And therefore he praises the person of John, who, setting aside the love of life, did not change the form even of justice through fear of death. The world is compared to a desert, because it is still untilled and barren, into which one must not so go forth as to think that men puffed up with the mind of the flesh and empty of virtue, and boasting of the glory of a frail world, are to be imitated.

*A reed by the wind. [n: (BED.)]* By the reed is meant the carnal mind, which, when it is touched by favour or by detraction, is bent to whatever side. But John is not a reed shaken by the wind, because neither did favour make him soft nor any man's anger make him harsh, nor did prosperity lift him up, nor did adversity bow him down.

VERS. 25.-- *Clothed in soft garments.* By the [0270D] garment the human body is understood. Therefore by the prophetic example he exhorts to the virtue of a virtue to be undergone. Behold they who are in costly apparel. Those whose limbs are loosed and made flabby by riches, strangers to the heavenly kingdom, grow old under the sway of demons (who are the kings of darkness) within the dwellings of this world.

VERS. 26.-- *More than a prophet.* Because he is an angel: not by nature, but by office, who is sent to announce the judge from on high.

VERS. 28.-- *But I say.* In this he is not preferred before the patriarchs and all men, but is shown equal to the rest of the saints. *But he that.* Every saint who is already with God is greater than he who is still in the world. Or: I, whom the opinion of many makes the lesser, [0271A] am in the Church of the saints set before him. Or, the least angel of heaven is better than any man whatsoever.

VERS. 29.-- *And all the people.* If this is understood to have been said by the Lord, the people is understood to be designated as hearing John. If it is said to have been interposed by the evangelist, it remains to be understood as hearing the Lord himself, discoursing upon John's greatness.

VERS. 31.-- *To whom then,* etc. John being praised, he passes to the rebuking of those who neither were moved by John's preaching, or who despise the preaching of Christ himself.

VERS. 32.-- *They are like to children.* The children are teachers humble in spirit. The marketplace is the synagogue, or Jerusalem itself, in which the ordinances of God's precepts were established, because they were wont to reproach their fellow tribesmen that neither, [0271B] drawn by psalms, did they rise up to the praises of God with a devout heart and with agility of limbs (which the dancing denotes), nor by threnodies (that is, by the lamentations of the prophets over the destructions already wrought or yet to be wrought) were they turned to penance.

VERS. 34.-- *The Son of man is come eating and drinking.* He comes as then, so now also; you spurn both ways of salvation. We have lamented to John, we have sung to Christ.

VERS. 35.-- *Wisdom is justified.* He shows that the sons of wisdom understand that justice lies neither in abstaining nor in eating, but in the evenness of mind that bears want and temperance, and does not corrupt itself through abundance. *The kingdom of God is not meat and drink [n: (Rom. XIV)]*. Of which not the use but the [0271C] concupiscence is reproved. *Justified,* etc. Whatever you think me, I nevertheless, the power of God and the wisdom of God, am understood to act justly by the apostles, to whom the Father revealed the things he hid from the prudent.

VERS. 36.-- *He asked.* As he had set it forth in words, so in deeds also he establishes that wisdom is justified by all her sons, because she is justified even by Mary repenting after her fault.

VERS. 37.-- *In the house.* In whosesoever inner house thou shalt have known wisdom and justice reclining, run back to the feet, that is, to the furthest part of wisdom; seek with tears, confess thy sins, spread out thy hair, that is, strew before him all the dignities of thy body; kiss, that is, thou shalt speak nothing but wisdom. *She brought.* As many delights as she had in herself, [0271D] so many holocausts she found out of herself.

[n: (HIER.)] The alabaster box of ointment, that is, the body with faith, etc., *as far as* that is, the sins of the Jews.

VERS. 38.-- *She began to water.* Mary the sister of Lazarus performed the same office twice: once in Galilee, when she first draws near with humility and tears, where she received the remission of sins. The second time, in Bethania, no longer named a sinner, but chaste, and therefore devout: there she anoints the feet, here the head.



## CHAPTER VII. (cont.)

VERS. 43.-- *He to whom more.* According to men, he perhaps offended more who owed more. But by the Lord's mercy the case is changed, so that he who owed more should love more, and yet obtain grace. *Thou hast judged rightly.* The Pharisee is convicted by his own sentence, [0272A] because, like a frenzied man, he carries the rope by which he is to be bound.

VERS. 44.-- *I entered into the house.* I entered, flesh being assumed, into the people of the Jews.

VERS. 46.-- *My head with oil thou didst not anoint.* And although that people confessed that it believed in God and not in a man, yet it neglected to proclaim with worthy praise that very power of the godhead which appeared through the miracles. But the Gentile world, while it believes the mystery of the incarnation, proclaims with the highest praise even his weak things.

VERS. 47.-- *Her sins are forgiven her.* In the house of the Pharisee, that is, in the keeping of the law and the prophets, it is not the unbelieving Pharisee but the repentant sinful woman who is glorified, because the ardour of charity burned away in her the rust [0272B] of her offences.

VERS. 49.-- *And they that were together began.* The sick woman being healed, they sicken over her salvation, to others.

VERS. 50.-- *Thy faith hath made thee safe.* Faith made her safe, because she did not doubt that she could receive what she asked, since she had already received hope from him from whom she was seeking salvation.



## CHAPTER VIII.

VERS. 1.-- *And it came to pass.* After the manner of an eagle stirring up her young to fly, the Lord little by little clothes his own disciples in the nest of faith with the feathers of the virtues, by which afterwards they may be able to fly more loftily and to instruct others. First he teaches in the synagogues, works miracles, spreads his fame everywhere, receives the crowds, heals, instructs; here he makes disciples, out of whom he chose twelve, [0272C] whom at first he teaches with the crowd present, and bestows on the wretched his accustomed benefits; but now he keeps them alone with him, that they may hear him more familiarly; to them alone he expounds the more hidden mysteries; and so at last, when they have been strengthened by the showing of his powers as by the protection of wings, he gives them the power of healing, and sends them to preach the kingdom of God.

VERS. 2.-- *Mary who is called Magdalen.* When Mary was making the journey with the Lord, and is recorded to minister to him, he calls her by the celebrated name of Magdalen: where he describes the same woman as a sinner, he reverently calls her by the general name of woman, lest the mark of her former error should darken a name of such renown as we venerate today. *Mary,* a bitter sea, because of the ingrafted roaring of penitence, by which she herself, or we, bewail our several [0272D] evils. *Seven demons.* That same Church of the Gentiles, cleansed from the filth of vices, which Mary signifies, Joanna also signifies: formerly given over to the worship of idols, but now redeemed by the mercy of Christ and filled with grace, and which was before as it were the wife of the devil.

VERS. 3.-- *And Joanna,* etc. Joanna, the Lord her grace, or the Lord merciful. whose is all that we live. *Of Herod's steward.* When any malignant spirit labours to deceive on behalf of the devil's kingdom, he stands as it were the steward of the most impious Herod. *Of their substance,* etc. It was an ancient custom of the Jews, and it was not accounted a fault, that women should feed and clothe their teachers out of their own substance. Here, because this could give [0273A] scandal among the Gentiles, Paul records that he abstained from it. They ministered therefore carnal things to the Lord, whose spiritual things they reaped, that he might give a form to preachers, that they ought to be content with the food and clothing they receive from the peoples.

VERS. 4.-- *And when a crowd,* etc. As often as the word "crowd" is inserted in the Gospel, just as a diversity of men, so also a diversity of wills is intimated. For not all followed Christ with the same will, but for different causes: some for good, some for evil; yet to all the Lord bestows his benefits, by teaching, by feeding, by healing. *The sower went out,* etc. The Son of God went out from the bosom of the Father, to which there was no access for a creature, that he might sow his own seed; he came into the world, that he might give testimony [0273B] to the truth. Whence, according to the other evangelists, when about to speak this parable he is recorded to have gone out of the house, to have gone to the sea, and to have gone up into a ship, insinuating by the posture of his body the very thing he insinuated by the course of his discourse.

VERS. 5.-- *By the wayside.* The way is the heart worn down and dried up by the constant passage of evil thoughts, so that it cannot receive the seed of the word nor make it germinate, but it is trodden down by the worst thoughts, and snatched away by the demons. Who are therefore called the birds of the air, because they are of a heavenly and spiritual nature, or because they course through the air.

VERS. 6.-- *Upon the rock.* The rock is the untamed heart, pierced by no ploughshare of true faith, in which there is not true love nor the virtue of perseverance.

[0273C]VERS. 8.-- *A hundredfold,* etc. Since the number ten is wont to be taken for perfection (because the keeping of the law is contained in its ten precepts), the number a hundred, which arises from ten multiplied, is set for great perfection: therefore that heart bears fruit a hundredfold which by the love of its neighbour attains the active life, and by the love of God the contemplative, and is adorned with the perfection of spiritual virtues.

VERS. 9.-- *This parable.* Note that this is the first parable which is set down with its own interpretation. And one must beware, wherever the Lord, being asked by the disciples privately, expounds his own discourses, lest we should wish to understand either something else, or more, or less, than what has been set forth by him. And therefore he deigns to show this parable by himself, that he might make known that he speaks figuratively, [0273D] and might teach that the significations of things are to be sought even in those which he was unwilling to expound.

VERS. 10.-- *Seeing.* The teachers of the Jews, who, although they have been blinded by the desert of their own perversity, falsely think themselves to be seeing.

VERS. 12.-- *But that which by the wayside.* They are sown by the wayside who deign to receive it with no faith, with no understanding, not even with any occasion of profit tempting them. But they are sown upon the rock and among thorns who do indeed hear, and approve the profit of the word, and taste the desire of it; but, lest they should come to that which they long for, they hold them back either by terrifying through adverse things or by flattering with prosperous ones. By these three kinds [0274A] are designated all who do not do the word they have heard.

VERS. 13.-- *For a while they believe.* Many, hearing a discourse against avarice or luxury, say that despisers of the world and chaste men are blessed: but when desirable forms are set before their gaze, presently whatever they had rightly thought departs from them.

VERS. 14.-- *They are choked.* Because pleasures and riches, by their importunate thoughts, strangle the throat of the mind, as it were the entrance of the vital breath, so that a good desire cannot enter to the heart.

VERS. 15.-- *On the good ground,* etc. Upon good hearers who take pains to hear the word, and lay it up in the belly of memory, and so in due time bring forth the fruit [0274B] of working, being patient amid the prosperity and adversity of the world. And the good ground renders fruit with patience, because it does good things, and bears with an even mind the evils of its neighbours; and while they humbly look up to the scourges, after the scourges they are received into rest.

VERS. 16.-- *And no man.* These words are set down according to the likeness of human custom.

VERS. 17.-- *For there is not,* etc. As if he should say. Let not the fear of carnal men hold you back, nor yet the evil opinion which is now held of you among unbelievers; because although you appear vile, one day your brightness will appear, when *the Lord will bring to light the hidden things of darkness,* then you also will be crowned, and your adversaries will be punished.

[0274C]VERS. 18.-- *For he that hath,* etc. These things seem to be said specially to the apostles, to whom out of charity it is given to know the mysteries, and to the faithless Jews (who through parables hear what they do not understand). It can also be taken generally, because both the clever man, by being negligent, deprives himself of true wisdom, and the simple diligent man, by labouring, tastes it. Because to him who has the love of the word, the sense also of understanding what he loves will be given. But he who has not the love of hearing, even though by natural talent or by studious labour he thinks that he understands, will yet taste no sweetness of true knowledge. *And whosoever hath not.* Therefore the slothful man often receives talent, that he may be the more justly punished for his negligence, who scorned to know what he could have attained without labour; and sometimes the studious man is pressed by slowness of understanding, that he may find the greater [0274D] rewards the more studiously he labours.

VERS. 24.-- *And it ceased, and there was a calm.* Note that every creature perceives its Creator. He commands, he rebukes, and they perceive him commanding; not that, according to the heresy of certain men, they are living things, but that things which with us are insensible are sensible to the majesty of their maker.

VERS. 25.-- *They wondered one to another.* Not the disciples, but the sailors and the others who were in the ship wonder. Whence Matthew: *But those men wondered, saying: What manner of man is this? [n: (Matth. VIII.)]* But if we say that the disciples wondered, they too are rightly called men.

*To the country of the Gerasens.* Gerasa, a city of Arabia [0275A] beside Mount Galaad in the tribe of Manasses, not far from the lake of Tiberias, signifies the Gentiles whom after his passion and resurrection the Lord visited through his preachers; whence Gerasa, or Gergesi, casting out the settler, that is, the devil by whom it was formerly inhabited, or, a stranger drawing near, who was far off in time.

VERS. 27.-- *In the sepulchres.* What else are the bodies of the faithless but certain tombs of the dead, in which the words of God do not dwell?

VERS. 28.-- *Jesus, Son of God.* Arius contends that he is a creature, whom the demon confesses to be the Son of God. The Jews say: *In the prince of demons he casteth out demons,* whom the demons deny to have anything in common with themselves. It is a torment to the devil to cease from injuring a man, and by how much the longer he had possessed him, by so much [0275B] the more hardly does he let him go. Let anyone therefore who has been injured by the devil take care to break his yoke quickly, because his dominion is escaped the more easily the more quickly it is escaped.

VERS. 29.-- *He was bound with chains.* The chains and fetters are the hard and heavy laws of the Gentiles, by which, even in their commonwealth, sins were restrained. Yet when these laws were broken, they were led by desire to those crimes which exceeded common usage.

VERS. 30.-- *Because many demons entered,* etc. The many demons which oppress one man signify the many cults of idolatry in one people of the Gentiles. On the contrary it is said: *The multitude of believers had one heart and one soul [n: (Act. IV)]*. Whence fittingly in Babylon the unity of tongues was rent, and in Jerusalem it was made one.

[0275C]VERS. 31.-- *And they besought.* The demons knew that by the Lord's coming they were one day to be plunged into the abyss: not themselves foreseeing things to come, but recalling the things said of them by the prophets.

VERS. 32.-- *A herd of swine.* The swine are men devoid of speech and of reason, given over to filthy deeds, feeding on the mountain of pride. And unless a man has lived after the manner of a swine, the devil receives no power over him, or receives it only for proving him, and not for destroying him.

VERS. 34.-- *They fled and told it in the city and in the villages.* The herdsmen fled, because neither the philosophers of the Gentiles nor the rulers of the Synagogue can bring medicine to the perishing; and although they flee the Christian law, yet by being astonished and by wondering they proclaim its power.

[0275D]VERS. 35.-- *And they went out to see what was done, and they came to Jesus, and found the man,* etc. It signifies the multitude, delighted with its own comely life, indeed honouring the Christian religion, but unwilling to endure it, while they say that they cannot fulfil it. They wonder also at the faithful people healed of its former manner of life, and sitting at the Lord's feet, that is, with a sound judgment of mind beholding the footsteps of the Saviour which it is to follow, and clothed with the pursuits of the virtues which, being deceived, it had lost.

VERS. 37.-- *And they besought him.* Conscious of their frailty, they judge themselves unworthy of the Lord's presence, not receiving the word of God, and not sustaining the weight of wisdom in a firm mind; and therefore he was not troublesome to them any longer, but [0276A] returns, because he does not deign to heal the unwilling, but quickly leaves the weak, to whom he sees his own presence to be a burden.

VERS. 39.-- *Return to thy house,* etc. Allegorically. After the remission of sins one must return into a good conscience, and must serve the Gospel, for the salvation of others also, that thereafter he may rest with Christ; lest, when he now wishes to be near with Christ, he neglect the mystery of preaching that is fitted to the deliverance of the brethren. Whence the Apostle: *I desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ [n: (Philip. I)]*, a thing much better, *but to abide in the flesh is needful for you.*

VERS. 43.-- *Upon physicians.* The physicians are the demons, who, as though taking counsel for men, demand to be worshipped in God's stead, and whom the Gentile world had served.

[0276B]VERS. 44.-- *She touched the hem.* He touches the hem who perfectly loves and believes the mysteries of the Incarnation, until he comes to the grasping of greater things. If we consider how great our faith is, and understand how great the Son of God is, we see that, in comparison with him, we touch only the hem, and cannot reach the upper part of his garment.

*And immediately,* etc. Being about to raise the dead girl, he returned to make faith; first he heals the woman with the issue of blood. So Elizabeth, barren, is declared as about to bear, that the Virgin about to conceive may be believed.

VERS. 45.-- *Who is it.* He does not ask that he may be taught what he does not know, but that the woman's faith may appear.



## CHAPTER VIII. (cont.)

VERS. 46.-- *For I also know.* Whoever touches is not hidden: [0276C] God does not need eyes in order to see, nor does he perceive bodily, but has in himself the knowledge of all things. The power also which is merciful to all our iniquities, and heals all our infirmities, goes forth not from us, but from God.

VERS. 47.-- *And the woman seeing.* This woman, modest, touches the hem; faithful, she drew near; religious, she believed; wise, she knows herself to be healed: so the people of the Gentiles, which believed in God, blushed at sin that it might forsake it, brought faith that it might believe, showed devotion that it might ask, put on wisdom that it too might perceive its own healing, took confidence that it might confess that it was seizing beforehand what belonged to another.

VERS. 51.-- *The father of the girl.* The father of the girl is the assembly of the teachers of the law, namely the Scribes and Pharisees, who, if they [0276D] shall have been willing to believe, the Synagogue that is subject to them will also be saved.

VERS. 52.-- *And they wept.* They weep who see the Synagogue estranged from God, who is life, and who grieve, and think that she can no longer be quickened; and she, who has lost the bridegroom's presence by which she ought to live, lying as it were dead among the mourners, does not understand even this very thing, why she is mourned. *Weep not.* Let them bewail their dead who think them dead; where there is faith of the resurrection there is not death, but the hope of rest. *She is not dead,* etc. The girl is dead to men who cannot raise her: not to God, to whom the soul lives, and to whom the flesh rests in order to be raised.

VERS. 53.-- *They laughed him to scorn,* etc. Rightly were they shut out, [0277A] who preferred to mock rather than to believe, unworthy to see the miracle of the resurrection.

VERS. 54.-- *Holding her hand.* He holds her hand, because unless the hands of the Jews (which are full of blood) shall first have been cleansed, the Synagogue will not rise. *Maid, arise.* Whosoever comes to himself from the death of the soul, Christ strengthening his hand for him, ought to rise from the filth of vices, and (as Mark says) to walk in good works, and it is necessary that he be presently filled with heavenly bread, that is, with the bread of the word of God and of the altar.

VERS. 56.-- *Whom he charged.* As a public offence needs a public remedy, so light and secret sins can be blotted out by a lighter penance. Whence the girl, lying in the house, rises with few witnesses, and these same are forbidden to publish the miracle abroad.



## CHAPTER IX.

[0277B]

VERS. 1.-- *And Jesus, having called them together.* The power of signs being first granted, they are sent to preach the kingdom of God, that the greatness of the deeds might also bear witness to the greatness of the promises, and that the power shown might give credit to the words, and that they who preached new things might also do new things.

VERS. 3.-- *Take nothing.* In Mark it is read, *that they should take nothing for the way but a staff only [n: (Marc. VI)]*. But it must be known that both were commanded by the Lord: that they should carry nothing but a staff, and that they should not carry even a staff. Not to carry a staff is not to be anxious about the things which are necessary for human sustenance, because these are owed to preachers by those to whom they preach, when they can be received without scandal. To carry [0277C] nothing but a staff is to use that power of receiving necessities which has been given to preachers. *Two coats.* A single garment is whatever is necessary to a man according to the character of the place or the time. A double one is what is beyond necessity. Allegorically, in the two coats he forbids duplicity.

VERS. 4.-- *There abide.* Do not run about through the houses and change the rights of an inviolable hospitality. Whence according to Matthew, the house in which they ought to remain must first be chosen, that the necessity of changing lodging may be taken away.

VERS. 5.-- *The dust of your feet.* The dust is shaken off in testimony of their labour, that they entered into the city, and that the preaching reached as far as them. [0277D] Or, it is shaken off, that they may receive nothing from those who have spurned the Gospel, not even the necessaries of life.

VERS. 8.-- *John is risen.* They who said that John, of whom they had seen no sign, had risen again, by the utmost malice denied that Jesus had risen again; they had seen many marvellous works, in whose passion many wonderful things had happened.

VERS. 10.-- *They told.* The apostles tell the Lord what they had done, and how they had taught, and how John suffered while they themselves were teaching. Whence it follows: *And taking them.* The nourishment of heavenly grace is imparted not to the idle, not to those residing in the city of the Synagogue, that is, of secular dignity, but to those seeking Christ amid the deserts. For those who do not turn away in loathing are received by Christ, and both spiritually and bodily, [0278A] if they have need, they are healed. And note, that after the mention of John's passion is completed, Christ refreshes, because after the failing of the law the food of the Gospel begins. Finally, after the woman who bears the figure of the Church has been healed, and after the apostles have been sent to preach, mention is made of the refreshment, because through the apostles true refreshment takes place in the Church.

VERS. 11.-- *They followed him.* Not on beasts, not in carriages, but by the proper labour of their feet, that they might show the ardour of their mind. *He healed.* No one receives the food of Christ unless he has first been healed, because after the remission of sins the heavenly nourishment is bestowed.

VERS. 12.-- *Now the day began.* He refreshes them when the day has declined, because, either as the end of the ages draws near, or when the sun of justice had set, we were [0278B] saved from spiritual famine. And note the order of the mystery: first medicine is applied to the wounds by the remission of sins; afterwards the nourishment of the heavenly table is dispensed.

VERS. 13.-- *Unless perhaps.* The apostles had not yet understood that the faith of a believing people is not for sale, but they had known that we are rather to be redeemed by his own free foods.

VERS. 14.-- *Five thousand.* By the five thousand are signified those who, being still in a secular habit, know how to use well, through the five senses of the body, the outward things which they possess. Who are fed with five loaves, because such must still be instructed by the precepts of the law, and restrained by legal fear. For those who renounce the world entirely both are four thousand, because of the evangelical [0278C] doctrine, and are refreshed with seven loaves, that is, with the grace of the sevenfold Spirit.

VERS. 15.-- *And all to sit down.* According to another evangelist the five thousand sit down upon the grass, because those who still have bodily senses are delighted with better things. *For all flesh is grass.* The four thousand recline upon the earth, for it is more to press the earth than to lie upon grass.

VERS. 16.-- *He distributed to his disciples.* He calls forth the apostles to the breaking of bread, that, while they testify that they have none, the greatness of the sign may become the better known. He insinuates that through their teaching fasting hearts are daily to be fed.

VERS. 17.-- *And they were filled.* That the people, eating, [0278D] is filled signifies that hunger is to be driven away for ever from the elect, because he who has received Christ's food will not hunger. That the apostles minister announces the future distribution of the Lord's body and blood. *Twelve baskets.* Why is more left over to the five thousand men, and less to the four thousand? Because the four thousand were with Christ for three days, and therefore received more of the heavenly pasture.

VERS. 18.-- *And it came to pass.* After the miracle, when the crowds wished to make him king, the crowd being dismissed, he fled and went up into the mountain alone to pray; and before the disciples went up into the boat, they were present with him. But he alone prays to the Father. For the saints can be joined to the Lord by faith and love, and can behold him as more excellent than men, and can follow his manner of life [0279A] in the flesh with the steps of humility; but he alone penetrates the incomprehensible secrets of the Father's position, and he alone prays what he knows must be prayed. But he is not found to have prayed with the disciples, but prays alone, because human hearts do not grasp the counsel of God, nor can anyone be a partaker of the inner things with Christ. *Whom do the crowds say that I am?* Being about to search out the faith of the disciples, he first inquires the opinion of the common people, lest the faith of the apostles should seem to be established by common opinion, but by the acknowledgment of the truth. Rightly are they named crowds who bring forward different opinions about the Lord, whose speech and understanding is unstable and wandering; and distinguishing his own from them he says. *But whom do you say that I am?*

VERS. 19.-- *John the Baptist.* The opinion about John [0279B] was perhaps for this reason, because, placed in his mother's womb, he perceived the Lord's presence. About Elias, because he was caught up into heaven, and is believed to be about to come. About Jeremias, because he was sanctified in his mother's womb.

VERS. 20.-- *But you.* He who discourses ought to consider the persons of his hearers, lest he be derided before he is heard. *The Christ of God.* He embraced all things, who expressed both the nature and the name. For in the name of Christ there is the expression both of the godhead and of the incarnation, and the faith of the passion. The heretic, although he does not deny the name of Christ, nevertheless denies Christ, since he does not confess all the things that are Christ's.

VERS. 21.-- *He charged them lest,* etc. He bids the disciples be silent, that he may deceive the prince of the world, and that he may avoid boasting and teach humility. At the same time, lest the disciples, still rude [0279C] and imperfect, should be crushed by the weight of a greater preaching.

VERS. 23.-- *And he said.* The things that pertain to faith in the Lord's nativity or passion he opened to the disciples alone, apart; but these things to all. *Let him deny himself.* Unless a man fall away from himself, he does not draw near to him who is above himself: and he does not lay hold of the newness to which he is called, unless he slay the oldness in which he was born; and when he ceases from vices let him also seek out virtues. Whence there is subjoined:

VERS. 24.-- *For he that. [n: (GREG.)]* As in time of persecution the soul must be laid down, so in peace earthly desires must be broken, that by how much a man seems the more secure, by so much he may be the more careful for his own keeping.

VERS. 26.-- *Him the Son of man.* The voice of profession does not suffice for the [0279D] proof of faith, a voice which the profession of the generality defends from shame.

VERS. 27.-- *Some standing here.* He who stands with Christ does not taste death, because he who has merited fellowship with Christ will not have even the slightest sense of eternal death, and for him the order of living is not interrupted even in death. *Till they see the kingdom.* Because it was hard to offer the soul to dangers and the body to death, he sustains the weakness of the human mind by the reward of present things, lest it be broken by despair or weariness. For when the eternal glory has been seen, even in passing and for a brief moment, they are nevertheless rendered stronger against the adversities of the world. He promises therefore that the glory to come is to be seen in passing upon earth, that the eternal glory may be the more surely hoped for in heaven. Or [0280A] he promises that some of the disciples will see the spreading of the Church before they depart from the world by death, so that, raised up in the present against the glory of the world, they may be the more surely believed to be about to reign in heaven.

VERS. 28.-- *After these words.* Others, who count only six days, signify that after the six ages of the world the saints must rest from labour. Luke, who counts in both the first day on which it is promised and the last on which the promise is fulfilled, hints that we shall rise on the eighth. For as Christ, after the sixth day of the week on which he ascended the cross, and after the seventh day of the week on which he rested in the sepulchre, rose on the eighth day, so we, after the six ages of the world in which we suffer and labour for the Lord, and after the seventh of the rest of souls which meanwhile is passed in another life, shall rise in the eighth age. *He took Peter.* [0280B] He names three only, because *many are called, few chosen [n: (Matth. XX)]*, and those who keep the faith of the Trinity inviolate deserve to rejoice in the eternal vision. He who knows merits knows that the three apostles were more perfect than the rest. They signify those who have faith, hope, charity. Or by Peter, prelates or the married; by James, the penitent or the active; by John, virgins or the contemplative.

VERS. 29.-- *And he was made.* Being transfigured, he did not lose the substance of true flesh, but showed the glory either of his own or of our resurrection; and such as he then appeared to the apostles, such will he appear after the judgment to all the elect. For in the judgment itself he will appear both to the good and to the evil in the form of a servant, that they may see him whom they pierced. [0280C] *And his raiment.* The Lord's garments are the saints who have put on Christ. Which garment, while the Lord dwelt on earth, seemed contemptible and like that of others, but shines when the Lord ascends the mountain, because *it hath not yet appeared what we shall be, but at length we shall be like to him, when we shall see him as he is.* [var: Vulg. 1 John 3:2 "Scimus quoniam cum apparuerit, similes ei erimus: quoniam videbimus eum sicuti est," We know that, when he shall appear, we shall be like to him, because we shall see him as he is] Or the garments of the Word are the discourses of the Scriptures, and certain clothings of the divine understanding. Because as he himself appeared to those apostles in another form and his garment shone, so also to the eyes of thy mind the sense of the divine readings already grows white.

VERS. 30.-- *Behold two men.* It is to be noted that not the bodies or the souls of Elias or Moses appeared, but that those bodies were formed out of some underlying creature: it can also be believed that this was done [0280D] by angelic ministry, so that angels took upon them their persons. A dead man and a living man appear, that they may signify that Christ was to die and afterwards to live, and that we should live to Christ and die to the world. To the apostles also he gives a sign from heaven, that their faith may be increased. But to the scribes tempting him he would not give one, that they might be hardened. *And they were Moses and Elias, seen.* By Moses are signified those of the lower regions; by Elias, the heavenly; by the apostles, the earthly, who shall come to the judgment.

VERS. 31.-- *They spoke of his decease.* Moses, that is the lawgiver, and Elias, the most eminent of the prophets, appear with the Word, that they may show that he is the one who spoke in the law and the prophets, and whom the law and the prophets promised. Who were seen not in the lowest places, but on the mountain, [0281A] because only those lofty in mind perceive the majesty of Scripture, which was fulfilled in the Lord.

VERS. 32.-- *They were heavy with sleep.* They are weighed down with sleep, because the sense of the human body is oppressed by the splendour of the incomprehensible godhead. Or they are weighed down with sleep, that they might see the glory of the resurrection after the rest of the body; and the saints will see the majesty of the Lord the more truly in proportion as they rejoice in the immortality of their flesh, in which they had conquered death. Then they shall behold Moses and Elias in glory, because they will better understand how *one iota or one tittle shall not pass from the law [n: (Matth. V)]*, and how the Lord did not destroy the law and the prophets, but fulfilled them.



## CHAPTER IX. (cont.)

VERS. 33.-- *It is good for us to be here.* He who had climbed to the mountains does not wish to descend to earthly things, [0281B] but always to persevere on high; and Peter stands in the person of those entering the kingdom of God. *It is good for us to be here.* It is good to be in the kingdom of God. As if he should say: We alone shall enjoy this light, not the envious Jews. *Three tabernacles.* That is, words, thoughts, works. *One for Moses.* How do they know those whom they had not seen before? By the majesty coming upon them, as in the resurrection to come each one will know the other, and the one will understand through charity the things desired in the other.

VERS. 34.-- *There came a cloud, and overshadowed them.* Although Peter is ignorant out of weakness, yet the devotion of his service pleases. Whence also the cloud overshadows; this overshadowing is of the divine Spirit, which does not darken the affections of men, but reveals hidden things, whose perfect one is [0281C] shown when the voice is heard saying: *This is my Son.* Because he sought a material tabernacle, he received the shade of a cloud, that he might learn that in the resurrection the saints are to be protected not by the covering of houses, but by the glory of the Holy Spirit. *And they were afraid.* They fear, because human frailty does not endure to bear the sight of a greater glory; and by how much the greater the things a man has sought, by so much the more does he sink down to lower things, if he has been ignorant of his own measure.

VERS. 36.-- *And the voice.* The voice of the Father, the terror being taken away, teaches Peter the truth. And note that, as when the Lord was baptized, so also when he was transfigured, the mystery of the Trinity is declared, because the glory which we confess in baptism we shall see in the resurrection. The Spirit in a dove, [0281D] here in a bright cloud, because he who now keeps the faith with a simple heart will then contemplate in the open light of vision what he believed, and by that very grace by which he shall be illuminated he shall be protected. *Jesus was found alone.* Not Elias, not Moses, but he whom alone you see: for him the tabernacle must be fixed in the heart. They therefore withdraw when the Lord began to be pointed out, lest the Father's voice should be thought to have been sent to the servants.

VERS. 37.-- *And it came to pass on the next day, as they came down from the mountain.* According to the quality of merits, the Lord daily ascends for some, while by lifting them up more loftily he glorifies the perfect, whose conversation is in heaven, and instructs and teaches them of eternal things which cannot be heard by the crowd. For others he descends, while he comforts and teaches and chastens the earthly and the beginners. *There met [0282A] them a crowd.* On the mountain he prays, teaches, shows forth his majesty, opens the Father's voice to the apostles; coming down, he is received by the crowd, is beset by the weeping of the wretched, reproaches the sins of unbelief, drives out the evil spirits.

VERS. 39.-- *And behold a spirit seizeth him.* Matthew describes him as a lunatic, Mark as deaf and dumb. But it signifies those who are changed like the moon, never remaining in the same state, but who wax and wane through diverse vices, who neither confess the faith, nor are willing to hear the discourse of that faith.

VERS. 42.-- *And as he was coming.* While the boy draws near to the Lord he is dashed down, because those converted to the Lord are for the most part more grievously assailed by the demon, that either they may be brought back to vices, or that the devil may avenge himself for his own expulsion. As in the beginning of the Church's birth he set many [0282B] and grievous struggles against those whom he saw being withdrawn from his kingdom.

VERS. 43.-- *And he rebuked.* Not the boy suffering violence, but the devil doing violence is rebuked, because he who wishes to heal a sinner ought to reprove and drive out the vice, but to cherish the man by loving him, until he render him healed to the spiritual fathers of the Church.

VERS. 44.-- *Lay you up.* You who cleave to my discipleship, to whom I have more openly disclosed my secrets, lay up in mind the issue of the precious blood by which the world is to be redeemed, while the rest wonder at such great deeds of the divine loftiness. *It shall come to pass that he be delivered.* Among the great works of the divine power he the more often repeats the abject things of his human passion, lest, coming suddenly, it should terrify, but that, thought upon beforehand, it may be more lightly borne.

[0282C]VERS. 45.-- *But they knew not.* This ignorance arises not so much from slowness as from love, because, being still carnal and ignorant of the mystery of the cross, they cannot believe that he whom they had known to be God was to die; but as they were accustomed to hear him speaking through figures, so also they thought that what he spoke concerning his own betrayal signified figuratively something else.

VERS. 46.-- *And a thought entered.* Because they had seen Peter and James and John led apart into the mountain, and that some secret had been delivered to them. And they thought that above the keys of the kingdom of heaven had been promised to Peter, and that the Church was to be built upon him; or that these three were set before the rest, or that Peter was set before all the apostles: or, because they had seen Peter made equal to the Lord himself in the payment of the tribute, [0282D] they judged that he was to be preferred to the rest. But it must be known that this question was raised both before the tribute and after the tribute was paid.

VERS. 48.-- *Whosoever shall receive a child.* Or simply: he teaches that Christ's poor are to be received for his honour by those who wish to be greater. Or he teaches that they should be little ones in malice, that they may keep the simplicity of childish innocence, so that the form of virtue which a child observes under nature's leading they themselves may follow of set purpose for the name of Christ. *Receiveth me.* He who receives an imitator of Christ receives Christ, and he who receives the image of God receives God.

VERS. 49.-- *John said.* John, loving God and beloved of the Lord, thinks that he is to be shut out from the benefit [0283A] who does not use the service; therefore he is taught that no one is to be kept back from the good which he has in part, but is rather to be called forward to that which he has not yet. Such men are judged to be admitted even for the salvation of others. Therefore in heretics or in bad Catholics we ought to detest and forbid, not the sacraments held in common, in which they are with us and are not against us, but the division contrary to peace and to truth, by which they are against us and do not follow the Lord with us.

VERS. 50.-- *Forbid not. For he that.* John is not reproved, because he acted out of love, but he is taught, that he may know that there is a distance between the weak and the strong, because the Lord, although he rewards the stronger, does not exclude the weak.

[0283B]VERS. 54.-- *Lord, wilt thou. [n: (BED.)]* The apostles, still rude and ignorant of the measure of vengeance, etc., *as far as* such a power has been conferred by God upon holy men.

*We say that fire come down from heaven.* At the word of these men fire would come down from heaven, because they are the sons of thunder. *And consume them? And turning he rebuked them and said.* In the likeness of Elias, who both by his own hand and by fire divinely obtained put the elders of Baal to death. And in this, that the apostles presume to themselves the power of such a prophet, it is shown that the merit of the prophets was in them.

[n: (AMBR.)] The apostles do not sin, who follow the law, etc., *as far as* he does not seek vengeance who does not fear.

VERS. 55.-- *You know not of what spirit you are.* By whose [0283C] spirit you have been sealed, whose deeds you imitate, now taking counsel mercifully, but in the future judging justly.

VERS. 56.-- *The Son of man came not to destroy souls, but to save,* etc. Perfect virtue has no zeal for vengeance, nor is there any anger where there is the fulness of charity.

[n: (BED.)] Vengeance is not always to be taken upon those who sin, etc., *as far as* from whom in this place the fire is kept back.

VERS. 57.-- *A certain man said. [n: (ID.)]* This man, moved by the greatness of the signs, wishes to follow, etc., *as far as* and he feigned the service of a disciple, which feigning is figured by the fox.

VERS. 58.-- *The foxes have holes. [n: (ID.)]* The fox is a deceitful animal, intent upon ambushes, practising the robberies of fraud, [0283D] dwelling in holes even amid the very lodgings of men: so the heretic, not having the house of faith, draws others into his own fraud and seduces them from the faith.

VERS. 59.-- *And he said to another.* Note that the Lord spurned not faults but frauds, because he who rejected the fraudulent man chose the innocent one; but him whose father he knew to be dead, that father of whom it is said: *Forget thy father's house [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*, etc.

VERS. 60.-- *Suffer that the dead. [n: (BED.)]* Mystically. The son is not called back from his duty to his father, etc., *as far as* he ought not to bury the devil's treacheries in himself.

VERS. 61.-- *To take leave of those.* If a disciple about to follow God is rebuked because he wishes to take leave of his house, what [0284A] will become of those who often, with no profit, visits the houses of their own people which they had forsaken.



## CHAPTER X.

VERS. 1.-- *And after these things,* etc. [n: (BED.)] Just as in the apostles there is the form of bishops, etc., *as far as* just as the sun is wont to accomplish the three-day circuit of its light in seventy-two hours.

*He sent them two and two.* By this, that he sends them two and two, it is intimated that no one ought to take up the office of preaching who has not charity toward another. Or they are sent two by two according as the animals were sent into the ark two by two, that is, male and female, unclean before in carnal generation, but cleansed by the sacrament of the Church through spiritual grace in the preaching of the disciples.

[0284B]VERS. 2.-- *The harvest indeed is great, but the labourers are few.* The harvest is the crowd of believers; the labourers are the apostles and those who follow them. And although the harvest has been sown by the word of God, still it requires the toil of tillage and the careful service of a labourer, lest the birds of heaven scatter the seeds that have been sown.

VERS. 3.-- *As lambs among wolves.* As the wolf lies in wait for the sheep, so does the heretic for the faithful. And as the wolf goes about the sheepfold by night, not daring to enter, scouting out the dog's sleep, the shepherd's absence or sloth, so the heretic tries to deceive the faithful by the night of his own interpretation, not entering the Church, and strives either to avoid the pastors of the Church, or to kill them, or to send them into exile. The wolf, stiff by the nature of its body, cannot easily bend itself; so the heretic is not wont to call back the purpose [0284C] of his hard heart from error.

VERS. 4.-- *Carry therefore neither purse nor scrip.* So great ought a preacher's confidence in God to be, that even if he does not foresee the means of the present life, he yet knows most certainly that they will not fail him, lest, while his mind is taken up with things temporal, he preach the eternal things less. *Salute no man by the way.* He bids them make haste and press on, lest they be turned aside from the journey enjoined them by conversation with someone they meet. Nor does he say simply, *Salute no man,* since it is a usage of human kindness to salute others, but he added, *by the way,* because it is not the diligence of greeting that is taken away, but the obstacle that hinders devotion is done away with, so that, when divine things are commanded, human things may be set aside for a little.

VERS. 7.-- *And in the same house.* If your peace be [0284D] received, it is fitting that you remain in it, receiving carnal wages from those to whom you offer heavenly things. Behold, he who forbade purse and scrip grants means from preaching, yet in such a way that they be content with the food and drink set before them. *Remain.* There must be no removing from house to house with a roving readiness, so that constancy may be preserved in the love of hospitality, lest the close bond of friendship once grown up be easily dissolved. *For he is worthy.* Note that two wages are owed to the one work of preachers: one on the way, which sustains us in labour; another in the fatherland, which rewards us in the resurrection.

VERS. 8.-- *And into whatsoever city.* Having described the various lodging of a house, he teaches what they ought to do in cities as well: namely, to have fellowship in all things with the godly, [0285A] and to be kept apart in all things from the company of the ungodly.

VERS. 11.-- *Even the dust.* He bids them shake off the dust: either as a testimony of the earthly labour which they undertook for them in vain; or that they may show that to such a degree do they seek nothing earthly from them, that they do not even suffer the dust from their land to cleave to them.

VERS. 12.-- *That for Sodom in that day.* The Sodomites, although they burned in vices and were inhospitable, yet among them no guests such as the prophets among the Jews, such as the apostles, were found. Lot, though just among them, still taught nothing, wrought no signs. And therefore *to whom much has been given, much is required of him, and the mighty shall mightily [0285B] suffer torments [n: (Sap. VI)]* [var: Vulg. Lc 12:48 *cui multum datum est, multum quaeretur ab eo*, future; the second clause is Sap 6:7, and the two are joined here under one citation].

VERS. 13.-- *Woe to thee, Corozaim.* Corozaim, Bethsaida, Capharnaum and Tiberias are cities of Galilee, on the shore of the lake of Genesareth. These the Lord laments, because after so many miracles and mighty works they did not repent: and they are worse than the Gentiles, who squander only the natural law, because after their contempt of the written law they did not fear also to trample upon the Son of God and, ungrateful, to spurn his grace. *And sitting in ashes.* We see the words of the Saviour fulfilled, for whereas those foresaid cities, with the Lord present, would not believe, Tyre and Sidon were once friends of David and Solomon, and afterwards, when the disciples of Christ preached the Gospel, devoutly received the faith. But why it was preached to those who would not believe, and not preached [0285C] to those who would believe, he knows who knows all things.

VERS. 16.-- *He that heareth you heareth me.* In hearing or in spurning a preacher of the Gospel, let each man know that he spurns or hears not persons of no account, but the Saviour himself, because in the disciple the master is heard, and in the Son the Father is honoured.

VERS. 17.-- *Lord, even.* They confess well, offering honour to the name of Christ: but because, their faith being still weak, they rejoice in mighty works and are lifted up by the working of signs, they are made afraid by an example and called back to humility. For if the devil was cast headlong from heaven for pride, much more shall these, brought forth from the earth, be humbled if they have been proud.

VERS. 19.-- *Upon serpents and scorpions.* Serpents [0285D] are evil men or demons who cast the venom of wicked persuasion against virtues at their beginning. Scorpions are those who strive to corrupt at the end virtues that are to be brought to completion.

VERS. 20.-- *Rejoice not in this, that spirits unto you,* etc. To cast out evil spirits and to work other mighty deeds is sometimes not the merit of him who works them, but the invocation of the name of Christ does this, either to the condemnation of those who invoke it, or to the profit of those who see and hear: where, though men despise those who work the signs, they yet honour God, at whose invocation the miracles come to pass. He forbids them to rejoice over the humbling of the devil, who fell through pride, but let them rejoice over their own exaltation: [0286A] so that whence those, being lifted up, fell, these, being humbled, may ascend. *But rejoice.* If anyone has done heavenly or earthly works, by these he is, as though adorned with letters, fastened eternally in the memory of God.

VERS. 21.-- *In that same hour.* When he was speaking of the subjection of unclean spirits and of the names written in heaven, he showed by what means so great a loftiness was given to men, namely by the humility of the Christian faith. *I confess to thee, O Lord.* I give thanks, because thou hast opened to the apostles the sacraments of my coming, which the scribes and Pharisees, who seem wise to themselves, do not know. *Thou hast revealed them.* He does not say, to the unwise and dull, but to little ones, that is, to the humble, to prove that he condemned swelling, not sharpness. *For so.* He does not render a reason why he chose [0286B] these and reprobated those, but says that it so pleased God. In which we receive an example of humility, that we should not rashly discuss the counsels above.

*All things are delivered to me,* etc. Humility being commended, through which one comes to faith, a passage concerning that same faith is beautifully joined on, when he says that all things are delivered to him, not the elements which he himself created, but those little ones to whom the Father revealed the sacraments of the Son. When thou hearest all things, thou acknowledgest one almighty, not discoloured, not degenerate from the Father. When thou hearest delivered, thou confessest the Son, to whom by the nature of one substance all things are by right his own, not conferred as a gift through grace.

VERS. 24.-- *That many prophets.* The prophets and the just saw the glory of the Lord from afar, through a glass [0286C] and in a dark manner; but the apostles, having the Lord present and asking of him what they would, were not taught through angels or through various kinds of visions.

VERS. 25.-- *A lawyer stood up.* The doctor of the law, who was lying in the law without faith, rises that he may stand: he stands while he calls him Master, but even in standing he tempts God by keeping silent about what he was.

VERS. 27.-- *He answering said.* He said what he had read, but did not fulfil what was written, because he had present with him the Lord whom he ought to love, yet he calls him Master, not God.

VERS. 29.-- *But he, willing.* The lawyer, that he may be said to have answered wisely, first confesses that he is ignorant of the commandment of the law, because, refusing to be humbled and willing to justify himself, he cannot see the hidden things [0286D] that are revealed to the little ones of Christ; to whom Christ so tempers his answer that both everyone who does mercy may be understood to be a neighbour, and especially the Son of God himself, who through his humanity has been made neighbour to us, may be designated. *Who is my.* No one is nearer to man than God, who knows him within and without and can care for all things; but every unbeliever or tempter has neither God nor man for a neighbour.

*A certain man,* etc. [n: (AUG.)] That man is understood to be Adam in the human race, etc., *as far as* he was nearer than the priest or the Levite of the same nation.

VERS. 31.-- *And it happened,* etc. The priest announces the law of God: the law indeed came down through Moses [0287A] into the world, and conferred no health upon such a one. The Levite came down, who shows the type of the prophets, but he too heals no one, because the law reproves sins, but passes by, since it does not bestow pardon.

VERS. 33.-- *Being moved with mercy.* This is not said of the priest and the Levite, because the law has not mercy, but judgment and vengeance.

VERS. 34.-- *He brought him to an inn.* He calls it not a house but a stable, by which name he marks the miseries and the stenches of this life, lest a man rejoice in this exile as though in his fatherland.

VERS. 37.-- *He that did.* Kinship does not make a neighbour, but mercy, which mercy is according to nature. For nothing is so according to nature as to help a partner of one's nature.

[0287B]VERS. 38.-- *It came to pass, as,* etc. A discourse having been held about the love of God and of neighbour, an example of both loves is set down, not so much in word alone as in the showing forth of work. For by these two sisters two spiritual lives are signified. By Martha, a devotion busy in works, by which we are joined to our neighbour in charity. By Mary, the religious intention of the mind upon the word of God, by which we sigh in the love of God. The active life gives bread, either bodily to one that hungers, or of doctrine to one that is ignorant; it corrects the erring, calls back the proud to humility, dispenses what is expedient for each. The contemplative holds fast the charity of God and of neighbour, but rests from outward action, cleaves to the desire of the Creator alone, and, all cares being trodden underfoot, burns to see the face of the Creator, and longs to be mingled [0287C] with the citizens above, who rejoice in eternal incorruption in the sight of the Lord. *Into her house.* When Jesus enters into the house, the wicked life, even if once it had been there, flees away; there remain two innocent lives, the laborious and the leisured, and between them, in the midst, is the very fount of life.

VERS. 39.-- *Sitting beside.* The more humbly she sits, the more abundantly does it fall. Water flows together into the valley from the swellings of the hill. Mary sits, because the contemplative life, the tumults of the vices being stilled, now enjoys the longed-for quiet of the mind in Christ. Martha stands, because the laborious active life sweats in the contest.

VERS. 40.-- *But Martha.* Mary was intent on how she might be fed by the Lord; Martha was intent on how she might feed the Lord: the latter prepares a banquet for the Lord, in the Lord's banquet the former already takes her delight.

[0287D]VERS. 41.-- *And answering.* Mary does not answer, but, as one at leisure, commits her cause to the judge. For if she were preparing words to answer, she would slacken her intentness on hearing. *Martha, Martha.* The repetition is a token of love, or perhaps of an intention to admonish, that she might listen more attentively.

VERS. 42.-- *But one thing is.* The one is set before the many. For the one is not from the many, but the many from the one; and yet many things are necessary to those who tend toward the one. *Mary the best.* Martha's part is not blamed, since it too is good, but Mary's part is praised, and why it is best is added below.



## CHAPTER XI.

VERS. 1.-- *And it came to pass.* After the history of the sisters [0288A] (who signify the two lives) he brings in the Lord as having prayed, and as having instructed the disciples to pray, because both the prayer which he taught contains in itself the mystery of either life, and the perfection of those lives is to be obtained not by our own strength but by prayers. *When he ceased.* For this reason the Saviour is often brought in praying, that he may lead the disciples on to the pursuit of prayer. Whence, the prayer being ended, he is asked by the disciples, that he may teach how they ought to pray.

VERS. 5.-- *Which of you hath a friend.* After he had set forth the form of prayer to those who asked, he lays upon them also instancy and frequency in praying, so that prayer may be offered not only by day but also by night; for he who at midnight asks loaves of his friend, and persists in the intention of asking, is not cheated of his prayer.

VERS. 7.-- *Trouble me not.* He does not take away the hope of obtaining, [0288B] but the more vehemently kindles the desire of praying, by showing the difficulty of obtaining. *The door is now.* The door which Paul asks to be opened to him for the speaking of the mystery of Christ, and which John saw opened to him, is the understanding of the divine word, which is shut in the time of a famine of the word of God, when understanding is not given. And the children, that is, those who, dealing out evangelical wisdom like bread, preached it through the whole world, are now with the Lord in eternal rest; and yet by praying it is brought about that he who prays receives understanding from the Lord himself, even if there be lacking a man through whom wisdom may be preached. *I cannot rise. [n: (AMBR.)]* No one fears that he may waken a sleeper, whom he knows to be always watching in prayer.

VERS. 8.-- *Yet because of his importunity,* etc. [0288C] Many are the snares, and heavy is the sleep of the body, because if the mind begins to sleep, it loses the vigour of its own strength. Shake off therefore thy sleep, that thou mayest knock at the door of Christ, that when thou hast entered there may be opened to thee the treasures hidden and in darkness in Christ Jesus.

VERS. 9.-- *Ask, and it shall be given you.* If a man who is a friend rises and gives, compelled not by friendship but by weariness, how much more does God give, who without weariness most bountifully bestows what is asked. Lest therefore the soul, turned back from the vanity of error, waste away any longer in spiritual want, let us ask the banquet of the word, let us seek the friend who may give, let us knock at the door where hidden things are kept. For he gives great hope, who in promising does not deceive. *For every one that asketh receiveth.* There is need of perseverance, that we may obtain. But if it is given to him that asks, and he that seeks [0288D] finds, and to him that knocks it is opened, then as for him to whom it is not given, and who does not find, and to whom it is not opened, it appears that he has not sought and asked and knocked well.

VERS. 10.-- *Seek, and you shall find.--The Lord is nigh unto all them that call upon him in truth, and he will do the will of them that fear him [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*. Others, who do not so ask, James rebukes, saying: *You ask and receive not, because you ask amiss [n: (Jac. IV)]*, because you persevere in your sins.

VERS. 11.-- *And which of you, if he ask his father bread, will he give him a stone,* etc. [n: (BED.)] Mystically. Bread signifies charity, on account of the greater appetite for it and its necessary use, etc., *as far as* let him stretch himself forth to those things which are before.

[0289A]VERS. 13.-- *How much more.* An open comparison. For if a sinful man, still weighed down with frail flesh, does not refuse to give temporal things to his sons when they ask, much more does the heavenly Father bestow in heaven goods that do not fail upon sons endowed with his fear and his love.

VERS. 14.-- *And he was casting out.* After the words of preaching which he set before the humble, he works miracles also, so that those who do not believe him preaching may believe when they have seen miracles. Matthew says that this demoniac was blind as well. In whom three miracles are wrought by the Lord: he sees, he speaks, he is freed from the demon. And what was then done carnally in one man is done daily in the conversion of the Gentiles, so that first, the demon being cast out along with all the filth of idolatry, they may see the light of faith, and then mouths that were silent before may be loosed to the praise of God.

[0289B]VERS. 15.-- *In Beelzebub.* King Ninus, the founder of Nineveh, consecrated a statue to Belus his father, and appointed divine honours for him; and the Chaldeans, receiving the likeness of that image, called it Bel. The Palestinians, receiving it, called it Baal, the Moabites Beelphegor. But the Jews, worshippers of the one God, called it Beelzebub for a derision of the Gentiles. For Baal means man, Zebub a fly; whence Beelzebub, that is, the man of flies, or one having flies, on account of the filth of the gore that was sacrificed in his temple. The Jews also asserted that the prince of demons dwelt in this image, by whose power they said that Jesus drove out demons.

VERS. 18.-- *But he, when he saw their thoughts.* This the Lord has reserved as proper to himself, that he should behold [0289C] and judge thoughts. It is proper to the Jews to seek signs, whence Paul: *The Jews require signs, the Gentiles wisdom [n: (I Cor. I)]* [var: Vulg. 1 Cor 1:22 *et Judaei signa petunt, et Graeci sapientiam quaerunt*, "and the Greeks seek after wisdom"]. *But he, when he saw.* He answers not to their words but to their thoughts: in which he gives a sure sign of his own power, by which he searches out the secrets of hearts. According to the letter it is plain that every kingdom, solid and firm, if it is divided into parts, is reduced to a wilderness, emptied of its inhabitants; and house shall fall upon house, when the inhabitants of one house are either oppressed or punished by the household of another house. But whither these things tend spiritually he sets forth when he adds: *And if Satan also be divided against himself.* If I, as you say, cast out demons in the prince of demons, then the kingdom of the devil is divided against itself, and so the power of his malice [0289D] cannot stand. But if a demon cannot drive out a demon, you say falsely that I cast out demons in the prince of demons. But if he can, as you say, look to yourselves, that you go out from his kingdom which is divided, lest you be involved alike in its ruin. But by what power the Lord casts out demons, lest they think him the prince of demons, let them attend to what follows.

VERS. 19.-- *But if I in Beelzebub cast out demons, your sons.* He calls the apostles the sons of the Jews, who among the other miracles they had received from the Lord drove out demons also. Which casting out they assigned not to the devil but to God, being conscious to themselves that they had learned nothing of evil arts from the Lord. Therefore they themselves, choosing the ignoble and contemptible things of the world, in which [0290A] there was no crafty malice, shall sit *upon twelve seats, judging the twelve tribes of Israel.* Or, *sons,* that is, the exorcists of that nation, who by invoking the name of the Lord drove out demons. As though he said: If the casting out of demons in them is imputed not to the devil but to God, why should not the same work in me have the same cause? *Therefore they shall be your judges.* Not by the power of judgment, but by comparison, since they assign the casting out not to the devil but to God. *But if.* As though he said: A divided kingdom cannot stand, but the kingdom of Satan is divided, as you say, in that Satan drives out Satan; therefore, etc.
