VERS. 20.-- *But if by the finger.* The Son is called the hand or the arm of the Father; the Holy Ghost is called the finger, on account of the difference of the various gifts which are given [0290B] to men through the Holy Ghost, as the fingers are distinct one from another; nor does the inequality of the members do harm. For although the arm is greater than the finger, yet the arm with the finger is one body. By this finger the law was written on the tables. This finger the magicians confessed in Egypt. *Assuredly is come,* etc., *the kingdom.* That is, the judiciary power *of God:* by which he will separate the good from the evil in the day of judgment. Or, *Kingdom,* that is, the beatitude above. As though he said: If I cast out demons in the Spirit of God, know without doubt that the entrance of the heavenly kingdom is laid open to them that believe.

VERS. 21.-- *When the strong man his court.* As though he said: If I, as you say, were casting out demons in Beelzebub, then I should be consenting in something to the works of the devil; but in no way do I consent with him, but rather I hasten to overcome him and to cast him out [0290C] from my faithful ones. *When* the devil, *the strong man,* to do harm, *armed,* with many spiritual wickednesses, *keepeth,* in his own servitude, *his court,* that is the world, which is set in wickedness, in which, until the coming of the Son of God, the devil bore rule without contradiction, and used an ill-pacified sovereignty in the hearts of the unbelieving.

VERS. 22.-- *But if a stronger.* He calls himself the stronger, who laid him low not by a concordant peace and working, as they slanderously alleged, but by a mightier power, and freed the world from his dominion. The arms of the devil which he took away are the most crafty wiles of spiritual wickedness. The spoils are the souls deceived by him, which Christ the victor distributes, which is the mark of one triumphing, because *leading captivity captive, he gave gifts to men, [0290D] some indeed apostles, others evangelists (Ephes, IV)*, etc. [var: Vulg. Eph 4:8 *captivam duxit captivitatem*, a finite verb; and Eph 4:11 reads *quosdam quidem apostolos, quosdam autem prophetas, alios vero evangelistas* — the prophets are absent here].

VERS. 23.-- *He that is not,* etc. And truly my works do not agree with the works of Satan, because I myself in nothing agree with him, but we are altogether contrary, because I am humble, kindly, willing to save souls; he is proud, envious, desirous to destroy. I by my preaching gather in the virtues; he scatters and severs from the unity of the Church. *And he that gathereth not.* These things may over and above be understood of all heretics or schismatics, who are all shut out from the kingdom of God.

VERS. 24.-- *When the unclean spirit.* Because you withdraw yourselves from my kingdom, attributing my works to the devil, [0291A] therefore the devil dwells in you, as in his own kingdom, more grievously than at the beginning. *Is gone out.* The devil went out from the Jews, when that people received the law and the worship of the one God, and he passed over to the Gentiles, who were dry and barren of the fatness of the Holy Ghost and of the love of neighbour. In whom now he does not find rest, because now he is driven out from the hearts of the Gentiles when the faith of Christ has been received, when he says: I will go back to the Jews whom I formerly left. And he finds that people swept with brooms, that is, with the superfluous observances of the Pharisees and the ceremonies of the law, which now avail nothing after the coming of Christ. And that he may possess it the more firmly, he takes to himself the whole company of demons. And the Israelites are now worse, blaspheming Christ in their little assemblies, than they had once been in Egypt before the law was received, because it is a greater unbelief [0291B] not to receive him when he comes than not to believe in him who is to come.

*Seeking rest, and he findeth not.* Because he who feeds upon depraved thoughts and deeds perceives that in the minds of the faithful all his temptations come to nothing. Then he says: *I will return into my house whence I came out.* I will return to that man's conscience, whence I had been cast out in baptism, and I will subject my former possession to my accustomed lordship.

VERS. 25.-- *And when he is come he findeth it swept with brooms.* Because when he has gone back to a reprobate of this kind, his offences compelling it, he finds the conscience of the sinner cleansed from vices in baptism, but heaped up with no good work.

VERS. 26.-- *Then he taketh seven.* The fulness [0291C] of all the vices. Which spirits, namely such as these, are called *more wicked* than the devil, because, though the devil is evil, yet those who, driven on by their own deserts, are made over to vices, are not only evil, but strive besides to make a display of the appearance of goodness through hypocrisy by affected virtues. *And the last state of the man becometh.* It were better to be ignorant of the way of truth than after acknowledging it to be turned back again.



## CHAPTER XI. (cont.)

VERS. 27.-- *Blessed.* Here Mary is praised, who bore the Lord. And the perversity both of the Jews then present and of the heretics to come, who deny the true humanity of Christ, is confuted, because she confesses the true Son of God against the blaspheming Jews, and testifies him a true son of man, consubstantial with his mother. For unless he were one flesh with his mother, in vain [0291D] would that womb of the Virgin and those breasts be called blessed. *And the paps.* According to the natural philosophers, from the same fount there flows both milk for children to be nourished and seed for children to be begotten; therefore he could be conceived of the seed of the Virgin who could be nourished by her milk.

VERS. 28.-- *Yea rather.* As though he said: Not only is Mary to be praised, because she bore the Word of God in her womb, but she is most of all blessed, because she kept the precepts of God in her work. So also all are blessed who conceive the word of God by the hearing of faith, and bring forth and nourish the keeping of good work in their own heart or in their neighbours'. *Who hear.* In praise of the Church. On the contrary the Pharisees are condemned, who sought neither to hear nor to fulfil the word of God, but to blaspheme it.

[0292A]VERS. 29.-- *He began to say.* Certain men were slandering his works, to whom he has replied thus far. Others, tempting him, were seeking a sign from heaven, and to these he replies here. *A sign is sought, and a sign shall not be given to it,* etc. To the disciples he gave a sign of his divinity, both when he was transfigured before on the mountain, and afterwards when, as they looked on, he was lifted up into heaven. But to unbelievers, a sign from the lower passion of his humanity.

VERS. 31.-- *The queen of the south shall rise.* Here, the people of the Jews being condemned, the mystery of the Church is set forth, which is gathered together from all the ends of the world: in the Ninevites through penance (which does away with sin), in the queen of the south the zeal of receiving wisdom (which guards against sin), that she may know the words of the true Peaceable One. For the Church stands upon two things: that thou either cease to sin, [0292B] or know not how to sin. *In the judgment.* Not a thousand years before the judgment, as the Jews feign, but in the judgment itself shall she rise. *With the men. [n: (BED.)]* Behold, a common resurrection is shown, of the good as well as of the evil. *Shall condemn.* Not by the power of judgment, but by the comparison of a better deed, because, leaving her empire behind, she came into Judea to hear a man famed for wisdom, whereas the Jews do not hear but blaspheme the true Wisdom of God dwelling among them.

*In the preaching.* Jonas preached for a few days, Christ for a long time. He to strangers, Christ to his own fellow-citizens. He wrought no sign, Christ wrought many.

VERS. 33.-- *No man. [n: (BED.)]* He who in the figure of the queen and of the Ninevites set the Church before the Synagogue, etc., *as far as* they will not enter by believing the open gate of the light.

[0292C]VERS. 35.-- *Take heed therefore lest,* etc. After the instruction of right faith he instructs also concerning good works and good intention, so that not only works but also the thoughts and intentions of the heart may be made clean; as though he said: I kindle the lamp of faith in your senses and set it in the Church. But you, so far as in you lies, kindle your own lamps, that is, cleanse the intentions of your heart, that from a clean intention your works also may be pleasing. These things, spoken particularly against the hypocrisy of the Pharisees who craftily sought signs, instruct us generally according to the moral sense.

VERS. 37.-- *And as he was speaking.* He does not say, as he was speaking these things, but *as he was speaking.* In which it is intimated that he turned aside to the Pharisee not immediately when these words were finished, but with other things coming between, because when this [0292D] discourse was finished, while he was still speaking, behold his mother and his brethren were seeking to speak to him. Concerning whom, after he had answered the man who announced them: *He that doth the will of my Father, he is my brother and sister and mother,* [var: Vulg. Mt 12:50 *Quicumque enim fecerit voluntatem Patris mei, qui in caelis est, ipse meus frater* — Migne's opening and *hic frater meus* follow Mc 3:35, which however reads *voluntatem Dei*] he afterwards went in to the Pharisee's banquet.

*Why not washed.* The Jews frequently wash their hands before they eat, holding the tradition of the elders, and returning from the market place they do not eat unless they be washed.

VERS. 39.-- *Of the cup.* Mark relates that the Jews were wont to observe the washings of cups and pitchers and beds and brazen vessels. The cup is a glass vessel; the dish is earthenware, of clay. By this is figured the frailty of the human body, in which outwardly they make a show of holiness [0293A] and feign justice: what was without they washed, within most wicked. *But that which is within you.* Let no one think that fornication and bodily vices of this kind are alone grievous, and that spiritual vices are light, such as avarice, anger, pride, and the rest, as the Pharisees thought.

VERS. 41.-- *But yet that which remaineth.* Now the good teacher teaches how the contagion of the body ought to be cleansed. And here is more fully set out what he had said briefly above about cleansing the eye of the heart. For this whole passage is directed to this end, that it may invite us to the pursuit of simplicity, and condemn the superfluous and earthly things of the Jews, who by understanding the law according to the body are likened to the glass and the dish because of their frailty. In which, although they sin grievously, yet to them the absolution of sin is promised, if they strive to do mercy. [0293B] What remains over from necessary food and clothing, give to the poor. Or, I give counsel: what alone is left after so great crimes, give alms. The first alms is to heal your own souls by believing in me who cleanse hearts; and when hearts have been cleansed through faith, all outward things also will be clean. He who wishes to give alms in an ordered way begins from himself, and gives it first to himself. For alms is a work of mercy, whence it is said: *Have mercy on thy own soul, pleasing God.*

VERS. 42.-- *You pass over judgment.* He has briefly touched upon many of their vices, who, intent upon tithing worthless things, have not the fear of the judgment to come nor the charity of God. They pass over judgment, because they do not refer all [0293C] that they do to the judgment. They pass over charity, because they do not love God from affection. But again, lest it make you, zealous for faith, negligent of works, he sets down the perfection of the faithful man, that after faith he may work.

VERS. 44.-- *Who are as monuments.* Here he rebukes the superstition of those same teachers, who outwardly make a show of the appearance of right doctrine, but hide the foulness which they carry within.

VERS. 45.-- *Answering, however.* Hearing the word of God, the Pharisee out of an evil conscience thinks that an insult is being done to him, and, the punishment of the faithless being called to mind, understands that he is to be condemned.

VERS. 47.-- *Woe to you.* They are not reproved because they adorn the monuments of the prophets, but because they imitate the slayers [0293D] of the prophets; by building sepulchres they accuse their fathers' crimes. But by persecuting Christ and the apostles they imitate the crimes of their fathers, and the sentence of damnation which they were pronouncing upon their fathers they turn back upon themselves.

VERS. 48.-- *Truly.* You build for the sake of catching the favour of the common people, as though you shuddered at their crimes; but by the very work you show that you consent to their treachery, because you do not do this good thing well, but for vainglory. *But you.* For this reason: because you are imitators of them, that you may fill up what was wanting to your fathers, namely that you may persecute me and mine, whom they persecuted in the prophets.

VERS. 49.-- *Prophets and apostles, and some of them they will kill, [0294A] and persecute.* Heretics lie, who say that the founder of the Old Testament is one and of the New another.

VERS. 50.-- *From the generation.* All the evil are one generation, one city, one body of the devil, as all the good are called one generation and one body.

VERS. 51.-- *As far as the blood of Zacharias.* Why as far as this man, when after him many were slain before the birth of Christ, and, Christ being born, the children were straightway destroyed by this generation? But because Abel was a shepherd of sheep, and was slain in the field, and Zacharias a priest slain in the court of the temple, they signify two orders of martyrs, the laity and those who are made over to the service of the altar.

[0294B]VERS. 52.-- *Because you have taken away the key.* The key is the humility of Christ, and they who have it understand the Scriptures. This the lawyers neither themselves understand by the law and the prophets, nor do they wish it to be understood by others. To enter the key is not to be content with the surface of the letter but to penetrate as far as the hidden things that are to be understood. Or: a teacher, if he scandalizes by his example those whom he builds up by his word, neither himself enters the kingdom of God and shuts out those who were able to enter.

VERS. 53.-- *And as he was saying these things unto them, the Pharisees and the lawyers began.* How true were the charges of their treachery and dissimulation and impiety which they heard, they themselves bear witness, who at so great a thundering whirlwind do not come to their senses, but assail the teacher of truth.



## CHAPTER XII.

[0294C]VERS. 1.-- *Beware.* To this leaven belong all the things which he disputed above in the house of the Pharisee; whence also the Apostle: *Not with the old leaven, nor with the leaven of malice [n: (I Cor. V)]*, etc. *Beware of the leaven.* A beautiful passage for the holding of simplicity and the emulating of faith, that we may not after the manner of the Jews put forth one thing in affection and feign another with the voice, since we know that in the day of judgment there can be no hiding-place for fraud.

VERS. 2.-- *Nor hidden, that shall not be known.* Therefore do not emulate dissemblers, because assuredly the time will come, in the day of judgment, in which both your virtue and their hypocrisy shall be revealed to all; whence there follows. *For the things which you have spoken in darkness shall be said in the light.* Not only in the world to come shall all things lie open, but even in the present time they shall be publicly preached in the whole Church throughout the world [0294D]: the things which in darkness, that is, in the shadow of oppressions and of prisons, you have spoken, when your acts shall be read on high. *For the things which you have spoken in darkness shall be said in the light. [n: (AMBR.)]* What you have spoken in darkness, that is, in fear, shall be said in the light, that is, in the confidence of truth when the Holy Ghost has been received. And what you have spoken in the ear, that is, in secret, shall be preached upon the housetops, that is, when the dwelling-place of the flesh has been trodden underfoot.

VERS. 4.-- *And I say to you. [n: (ID.)]* Since the cause of faithlessness is twofold, etc., *as far as* so fortitude is the foundation of faith.

VERS. 5.-- *But I will show you.* Because there are two kinds of persecutors, one of those who persecute openly, the other of those who fraudulently flatter. As above [0295A] against hypocrisy, so here he arms against the executioners. This whole passage instructs unto the undergoing of suffering for the confession of the Lord, either by contempt of death, or by hope of reward, or those who will abide, in the denunciation of punishment.

VERS. 6.-- *Are not five. [n: (AMBR.)]* Five sparrows, the five senses of the body; or two according to Matthew, the body and the soul, etc., *as far as* there is no doubt that the contemplation of the faithful will prevail with him.

*They are sold for two farthings.* A dipondius is a weight composed of two asses. What in numbers is one, that in weights is the as; what is two, that is the dipondius. *And one of,* etc. He had breathed in the disposition of simplicity, he had raised up the strength of the mind, faith alone was wavering; this he strengthens from the meaner things. As the Apostle says: [0295B] *Doth God take care for oxen? [n: (I Cor. IX.)]* But care is one thing, knowledge another.

VERS. 7.-- *Yea, and the hairs.* Not by the act of counting, but by the faculty of knowing, from the likeness of money which is numbered that it may be kept. Wherein is the great providence of God, that neither small nor idle words lie hidden from him.

VERS. 8.-- *Every one whosoever.* Lest anyone in the time of persecution should think the faith of the heart sufficient for himself, he says: *Every one whosoever shall confess.* A man confesses Jesus with that charity which works through love; he denies who does not obey the commandments. *The Son of man.* The Son confesses before the Father him who through the Son has access to the Father. He is denied by the Son before the Father, to whom the Son and the Father are not made manifest in the power of the Godhead. And lest one condition be thought to belong to all, [0295C] both to those who deny of set purpose and to those who deny out of weakness or ignorance, he adds: *And every one that speaketh a word in the Son of man, it shall be forgiven him.* [var: Vulg. Lc 12:10 reads *in Filium hominis*, the accusative — "against the Son of man". The plate sets the ablative *in Filio*, read at 400 dpi on leaf n153, so the sentence Migne prints forgives a word spoken *in* the Son rather than *against* him.]

VERS. 10.-- *But to him.* To him also who, being scandalized in the weakness of the flesh, shall have suspected me to be a mere man or a glutton, it shall be remissible; but he who, not acknowledging the grace of the Holy Ghost (by which penance is inspired and a return is made to the congregation), shall have remained with an impenitent heart, since this is now not human but devilish, for him it shall be irremissible.

VERS. 11.-- *And when they shall bring you into the synagogues, and to magistrates and powers.* The terror of such assemblies is wont to take away freedom of speech. *Be not solicitous.* Offer your will for Christ. But Christ will speak for himself.

[0295D]VERS. 13.-- *Master.* Upon one who was commending the grace of the peace and unity above, this man wishes to thrust the trouble of an earthly division; whence also he is called a man, that is, earthly. For those among whom there is a zeal and contention of this kind are men, and walk according to man. Among brethren it is not a judge in the midst but family affection as arbitress that ought to divide a patrimony, although the patrimony of immortality is not money to be sought by men.

VERS. 14.-- *Who hath appointed me.* I am not the God of dissension but of peace and unity, who came to make men at peace with the angels, that many may have one heart and one soul. Not that they may be divided, but that they may have all things in common, and that there be not anyone in need among them. [0296A] He who does not gather with me is a divider of brotherhood and an author of dissension.

VERS. 15.-- *Of all covetousness.* No one would think it a crime to divide an inheritance, to gather fruits into barns; but the judge above declares that this too is covetousness, whence also he adds: *of a certain rich man.* As above against the Pharisees and hypocrites, so here, on the occasion of a foolish petition, he disputes against covetousness. *Not in abundance. Man liveth not by bread alone, but in the word of God [n: (Deut. VIII)]* [var: Vulg. Deut 8:3 *non in solo pane vivat homo, sed in omni verbo quod egreditur de ore Dei*, "in every word that goeth forth from the mouth of God"], nor is temporal life prolonged by a multitude of riches.

VERS. 16.-- *And he spoke,* etc. After the precept he subjoins an example for the avoiding of covetousness for temporal things. *The plentiful fruits of a certain rich man.* The rich man is not blamed because he tilled the ground, or laid up his fruits [0296B] in barns, but because he placed the confidence of his life in them, and did not lay them out upon the poor, that he might be received by them into everlasting dwellings.

VERS. 17.-- *What shall I do,* etc. Reckoning the fruits to be his own and the goods his own, he does not lay out upon the poor what is over and above, but is eager to keep it for his own luxury against the time to come, and knows not who will make use of what he has gathered.

VERS. 19.-- *My goods, and I will say. [n: (AMBR.)]* Those are not a man's goods which he cannot carry away with him. For mercy alone is the companion of the dead.

*Take thy rest.* Behold, to the plague of covetousness is joined the plague of sloth, so that he did not even call upon God for temporal things.

VERS. 20.-- *But he said.* For God to speak is to check a man's depraved contrivances by a sudden chastisement. *Thou fool.* Because thou wast promising thyself long seasons [0296C] of riches, this night, snatched away, thou shalt leave to others what thou hast gathered. *They require,* etc. In the night the soul is taken away, which in the darkness of the heart would not foresee what it could suffer.

VERS. 21.-- *And is not rich toward God.* He is rich toward God who, despising things transitory, distributes to the poor; whose expectation is the Lord; whose substance, that is, the possession of a conscience by which he is sustained and fed, is with God, not in the purses of earth.

VERS. 22.-- *And he said to his disciples.* He strengthens the apostles through faith in the hope of things eternal, because there are many whom the love of things present and the despair of things to come make sluggish. *Be not solicitous.* It would be more perfect to give all to the poor, and to make friends of the mammon of iniquity; but because for the most part the devotion of faith is called back by weakness, [0296D] he does not forbid foresight, by which bread is prepared in the sweat of the face, but he forbids the solicitude which disturbs the mind and calls it back from things eternal.

VERS. 24.-- *Consider.* The saints are deservedly compared to the birds, who, having nothing in the world and not labouring, seek eternal things by contemplation alone, being already like the angels. *How much more.* If to the birds, which do not labour, nor claim the common fruits for themselves more particularly by any lordship, the providence of God bestows what is needful, it is true that the cause of our want is shown to be covetousness, in that we claim common things as though our own, and grudge them to others.

VERS. 26.-- *If then neither.* If you cannot work even in the increase [0297A] of your own body, an operation which is the least thing for God in comparison with the spiritual creature, why are you solicitous about the rest, which are greater, that is, about the preservation of the soul.

VERS. 27.-- *Consider the lilies.* An open comparison. Lilies do not, like other fruits, require any tillage of husbandmen. By the lily also the heavenly beatitude is wont to be signified, to which the Lord invites us. Lilies are born not in the woods but in gardens; so we too ought to be born in the gardens of the virtues. *Nor Solomon. [n: (AMBR.)]* Solomon is here said to be clothed, and elsewhere to be covered with glory: he who used to clothe with the glory of works the weakness of bodily nature, covered over as it were with a certain strength of mind.

*Was clothing as one.* What purple of kings, what [0297B] painting of weavers can be compared to flowers? The colour itself is called the garment of the flower, as it is said: Redness has covered it.

VERS. 28.-- *To-morrow into the oven.* To-morrow in the Scriptures is put for the future, as Jacob says: *My justice shall hear me to-morrow [n: (Gen. XXX)]* [var: Vulg. Gen 30:33 *Respondebitque mihi cras justitia mea*, "and my justice shall answer for me"].

VERS. 29.-- *Seek not what you shall eat, or what you shall drink.* He does not say: Seek not, or be not solicitous about food or clothing, but more expressly *what you shall eat or what you shall drink.* In which those seem to be reproved who, despising the common food or clothing, seek finer or harsher meats or garments than those among whom they live. *Be not lifted up on high.* Solicitude for food or clothing, which are for necessity, being forbidden, he forbids also the pride which is wont to follow from these things when they are superabundant: therefore do not seek these things, because such solicitude [0297C] makes men like the unbelieving.

VERS. 30.-- *And all these things.* Because all things belong to the sons, therefore all these things shall be added to you, even when you do not seek them, so that neither in the present nor in the future may any grace be lacking to the faithful; from whom, if these things are taken away, it is for a testing; if they are given, it is for a giving of thanks, because *all things work together unto good for the good.* [var: Vulg. Rom 8:28 *diligentibus Deum omnia cooperantur in bonum*, "to them that love God"]

VERS. 32.-- *Fear not.* Seek the kingdom, and do not distrust concerning the kingdom; because the Father with a well-pleased goodness will give you the kingdom, not by your own merits. *Little.* Either on account of the comparison with the greater number of the reprobate, or rather on account of the devotion of humility, through which humility the Church grows, through which one comes to the kingdom.

[0297D]VERS. 33.-- *Sell.* Not only should you not fear that the necessaries of life may be lacking to those who fight for the kingdom's sake, but sell also what you possess for the sake of alms, which is the way to the kingdom, since through it sins are redeemed. Not only share your food with the poor, but sell also your possessions, so that, when all that is yours has once been despised for the Lord's sake, you may afterwards work with the labour of your hands, whence you may live or may give alms. *Give alms.* He who for heaven's sake despises all worldly things, let him sell what he has and distribute it; he who is not of so great virtue, let him give alms out of what he has. *That faileth not.* Temporal things either fail of their own frailty, or, if anything solid remains, such as precious stones, it can be taken away by thieves. [0298A] But things given for Christ's sake bring an eternal fruit in the heavens.

VERS. 34.-- *For where your treasure is.* Universally, both of money and of all pleasures. The glutton's treasure is his belly, where he has his heart; and so of the rest. For if it is on earth, the heart is below; if in things heavenly, it is fixed in Christ. For it is necessary that where the treasure of love has gone before, there the affection of thought should follow.

VERS. 35.-- *Let your loins be,* etc., *and lamps.* The one without the other does not please God, when a man either does good things while luxury is not yet bridled, or lives chastely without exercising himself in good works. And that you may be able to be drawn away from the love of things temporal, and may do the good things you do with a simple intention of things eternal, gird your loins, that is, bridle the motions [0298B] of carnal desires, and have your lamps burning, that in your working you may burn with the love of God alone and may offer an example to others.



## CHAPTER XII. (cont.)

VERS. 36.-- *Waiting.* Whether you avoid evils or do good, you are cleansed in nothing, but only await the coming of the Redeemer. *When he shall return.* The Lord went to the marriage when, after the resurrection, the new man joined to himself the multitude of angels: from whom he returns when he is made manifest to us through the judgment. Whence too he fitly subjoins concerning those that wait: *That when he cometh, they may open to him immediately.* [var: Vulg. Lc 12:36 reads *ut, cum venerit et pulsaverit, confestim aperiant ei*, "when he cometh and knocketh"; the gloss at once expounds the knocking (*pulsanti*) that the lemma as printed does not carry.] He does not wish to open to the judge who knocks, who, fearing to see angry the one whom he despised, dreads to go forth from the body. He opens who bears with the judge glad and untroubled, and rejoices at death drawing near; whence it is subjoined: *Blessed are those servants whom, when [0298C] he cometh,* etc.

VERS. 37.-- *Watching.* He watches who keeps his eyes open in the true light, that he may avoid the darkness of negligence; who also works what he has believed; who is solicitous in the care of the flock committed to him. But what is owed to those that watch he subjoins: *Amen I say to you.*

VERS. 38, 39.-- *And if he shall come in the second,* etc. He calls them watches from the likeness of men keeping guard. In the night of this world we ought always to be solicitous against the enemy, and to await the light that is to come, that is, the coming of the judge. The first watch, therefore, is the guarding of boyhood, the second of youth, the third of old age. But if anyone has neglected to watch in boyhood, let him not despair, but let him come to himself even in youth, or at least at last in old age, because the merciful judge [0298D] patiently awaits our delays. He, to shake out the sloth of the mind, brings in the likeness of outward condemned men, that by this the soul may be roused to the guarding of itself. Whence, *But this know ye, that if the householder knew.* While the householder knows not, the thief digs through the house, because, while the spirit sleeps from the guarding of itself, unforeseen death bursts into the dwelling of the flesh and drags it to punishments. But he would withstand the thief if he watched, because, taking precaution against the coming of the judge who comes secretly, he would meet him by repenting.

VERS. 40.-- *At what hour you think not, the Son of man will come?* The Lord willed the last hour always to be unknown, that it may always be suspected, and that men may always be prepared for it.

[0299A]VERS. 41.-- *And Peter said to him.* Since the Lord had set forth two things, namely both that he would come suddenly, and that they by waiting ought to be ready, Peter asks whether those lofty ordinances of the heavenly life, which are necessary for men preparing themselves, pertain to the apostles alone and to such as they, or to all who are to be saved.

VERS. 42.-- *And the Lord said.* Answering the things asked, the Lord says that the first and the second judgment will be present to all, and that each severally is to be rewarded or condemned according to his merits. Then he adds what he had chiefly asked, namely that the grace of the virtues which he had brought to the world is to be followed by each as far as he can, which is in that place: *I came to send fire upon the earth. Who dost thou think to be the faithful steward?* As if he said: To you, who have been appointed stewards, this [0299B] more especially belongs concerning watchfulness and preparation, that you may prepare both yourselves and others for the coming of the judge, because few are they who well administer the stewardship committed to them. *And prudent, whom the Lord hath set over his household, that,* etc. How rare is he that serves the Lord for the Lord's sake, feeding the sheep of Christ not for gain but out of love of Christ, prudent for what is to come, providing for himself, whom he hath appointed; that is, how rare is he who is called by God as Aaron was, and has not rather thrust himself forward, and who feeds not himself more than the sheep!

VERS. 43.-- *Blessed.* As if he said: Few are the faithful. But he who shall have been found faithfully ministers the allowance of the word, and, watching in the care of the flock, shall be glorified with eternal blessedness.

[0299C]VERS. 44.-- *Over all that he possesseth.* As great as is the distance of merits between good hearers and good teachers, so great is that of rewards also. The good hearers he makes to sit down, and passing by he ministers to them; the stewards he sets over all that he possesses, not that they alone, but that beyond the rest they may have things eternal, both for their own life and for the guarding of the flock.

VERS. 45.-- *But if the servant shall say.* Note that it is reckoned among the vices of the servant that he thinks his lord's return slow. Among the virtues of the good it is not counted that he hoped for it soon, but only that he ministered faithfully. Nothing therefore is better than that we patiently bear to be ignorant of what cannot be known, yet labour that we may be found fit.

*And to eat.* That is, to be taken up with all the crimes and enticements [0299D] of the world, which make the mind to go astray; or literally, if he be given over to gluttony and drunkenness.

VERS. 46.-- *And his portion.* The wicked shall be punished with the unfaithful, but with a difference. For those who sin knowingly shall be punished more grievously; less grievously, those who sin out of ignorance. And among the unjust each shall have a damnation the more tolerable, the less iniquity he had.

VERS. 48.-- *With few.* The mildest punishment of all is that of those who beyond original sin have added no offence besides.

[n: (AMBR.)] Many, thinking that they shall be beaten less if they know not, etc., *as far as* but they are judged as despisers.

[0300A] *And to everyone.* Deservedly the wicked man who knows shall be beaten more, and he who knows not, less, because to the one more, to the other less, has been committed: and of everyone to whom more is committed, more is required.

VERS. 49.-- *Fire,* etc. As if he said: Thou wast asking whether I admonish all men, with loins girt and lamps burning, to await the coming of the Lord? But I, who came into the world only for this, that I might kindle men from the desires of the world to heavenly things, will nothing else save that the radiance of this burning may light up all the parts of the world as far as the end of the age, and that it may so blaze in the hearts of the faithful that it be not quenched by any onset of waves or of blasts.

*But I have a baptism,* etc. And that this fire may be able to be kindled in the hearts of the faithful, so that they may be able perfectly to despise all temporal things [0300B] and themselves also, I must first be baptized with a baptism, that is, be steeped in the anointing of my own blood: for the Spirit would not be given unless Jesus were first glorified. *And how.* So great is the condescension of the Lord that he testifies there to be in him an eagerness for devotion to be poured into us, and for perfection to be consummated in us, and for the passion to be hastened for us: he who, though he had in himself nothing whereat to grieve, was yet straitened by our troubles, and at the time of death put forward a sadness which he had taken on not from the fear of his own death, but from the delay of our redemption. *Until it be accomplished.* He who is straitened as far as the accomplishment is untroubled about the accomplishment, nor is he straitened about it; but rather the death of the body is the loosing of anxiety, not the heaping up of grief.

[0300C]VERS. 51.-- *Think ye.* How, after the baptism of the passion and after the coming of the fire, the earth is to burn, he says: Because for the faith of Christ the whole world is divided against itself, and every house had faithful and unfaithful, these fighting against the faith, those for the faith.

VERS. 52.-- *In one house.* Each single man is one house, either God's or the devil's. And so a spiritual man is a spiritual house. *Divided.* The first cause is that of religion toward God, the second that of dutifulness toward one's neighbour. For thou oughtest to set human things after divine. Kindred pledges are not therefore to be renounced, but God is to be preferred to all. For indeed the pledges of nature are God's benefits, nor ought the benefit that is received to be loved more than God, by whom the benefit received is preserved. *Three against two.* That is, the son and the daughter and the daughter-in-law, against the father and [0300D] the mother; and two against three, that is, the father and the mother, where the mother-in-law also is understood, against the son and the daughter and the daughter-in-law.

VERS. 54.-- *And he said.* When he had instructed the apostles also concerning his first coming, intimating what was done in it, and had admonished them concerning the coming to be, how they ought to await it, he turned his discourse to the crowds, among whom were the Pharisees, as Matthew indicates: because, neither believing the first coming to have taken place nor rightly awaiting the second, they were seeking a sign from heaven, tempting him. These therefore he reproves, because if they were willing to attend, they could have from the words of the prophets a sure sign of both comings. *When you see.* Mystically. The cloud rising from the west signifies the flesh [0301A] of Christ rising again from death, from which time the shower of the Gospel preaching has been poured out upon all lands. The south wind blowing before the heat signifies the lighter tribulations before the judgment.

*A shower cometh.* Literally the sense is plain: they who from the changing of the elements can foreknow the state of the breezes could also know the time of both comings, both from the sayings of the prophets and from the magnificence of his works.

VERS. 56.-- *Hypocrites,* etc. And lest any unlearned men in the crowd, flattering themselves upon their want of skill, should excuse themselves, saying that they knew not the law and the prophets, he adds that they can discern by natural reason either that he who does works which no other had done is above man and is God, or that after so many injustices of the age [0301B] the just judgment of the Creator will come.

VERS. 58.-- *And when thou goest.* When in this life thou preparest thyself to see the appeased face of the judge, although the word of God be contrary to thy flesh, yet use it as a guard given thee on this journey, lest, being despised, it accuse thee. *With the adversary.* Our adversary in the way is the word of God, contrary to our carnal desires in the present life, from which he is set free who is humbly subject to its precepts: otherwise the sinner, guilty from his contempt of the word, will be held at the judge's examination, and the judge will deliver him to the exactor, that is, to the devil; because he will permit the devil to drag his soul to punishment, and the exactor himself, who exacts the punishment for the guilt which he suggested, will thrust it back into hell.

[0301C] *Give diligence,* etc. These things too pertain to the treading down of the world's enticements and to the awaiting of the judge's coming. As if he said: Since it is certain that a strict judge will come, live therefore here in such wise that you be not condemned in that judgment.

VERS. 59.-- *Thou repay.* He repays always by suffering punishments for his sins, but never by obtaining pardon.



## CHAPTER XIII.

VERS. 1.-- *And there were present.* While the master of truth was instructing those present how they ought to prepare themselves for the coming of the judge, and how they that do not prepare themselves are to be condemned, tidings are brought him of the Galileans, who as it were justly sought this for the cause that they had not well prepared themselves: and the Lord answers both that they truly [0301D] had been sinners and had justly perished, and that other sinners shall likewise perish, if they take no heed that they are sinners. *Bringing tidings.* As if they said: It appears that these men were sinners and to be condemned for ever, who by so wicked a death were slain by an impious governor in the midst of their own sacrifices. *Of the Galileans.* The Galileans pay the impious penalties of their own crimes by a wicked death; yet it was not that death itself that harmed them, so that they should be sent into the second death, but their wicked life. And they were punished for the correction of the living, that when the pestilent man is scourged the fool may become wiser, or for an example to those who will not be corrected, and who therefore shall perish most wretchedly.

VERS. 3.-- *All,* etc. This was fulfilled after the Lord's passion, when the Romans so laid waste the impenitent [0302A] Jews, beginning from Galilee, that not only the courts of the temple but even the inner parts of the house, whither the Galileans had no access, were defiled with human blood.

VERS. 4.-- *As those eighteen.* These men of Jerusalem, like the Galileans also, were not the only sinners, but were punished for the terror of the rest; and they, crushed by the fall of the tower, signify that the Jews who would not repent were to perish together with their own walls. Nor is it without purpose that they are eighteen, which number among the Greeks is expressed by iota and ita, that is, by the same letters with which the name Jesus begins. And it signifies that those Jews chiefly were to perish who would not receive the name of the Saviour. That tower signifies him who is the tower of strength, which is rightly in Siloe, because Siloe, which is interpreted [0302B] *sent,* signifies him who, sent by the Father, came into the world, who shall crush all upon whom he shall fall.

VERS. 6.-- *A fig tree.* In the beginning of the human race Adam and Eve, when they had sinned, made themselves aprons of fig leaves. By the leaves of the fig tree, therefore, sins are understood. And they were under the fig tree as under the shadow of death, of whom it is said: *They that sat under the shadow of death, light is risen to them [n: (Isa. XI)]*. [var: Vulg. Is 9:2 reads *habitantibus in regione umbrae mortis, lux orta est eis*, "to them that dwelt in the region of the shadow of death, light is risen"; the *qui sedebant* form belongs to Mt 4:16, and neither witness has *sub umbra* or *lumen*.]

VERS. 7.-- *Seeking fruit. The vineyard of the Lord sabaoh is the house of Israel,* in which house the Synagogue was founded as a fig tree in a vineyard. But he who permitted the one sinning in the vineyard to be plundered by wayfarers commanded this fig tree also to be cut down. In it he seeks fruit, not as one who did not know that it was there, but he shows in a figure that in the Synagogue [0302C] it ought by now to have fruit. Nor does he seek before the time, who comes through three years. He who through Moses established the Synagogue, himself appearing in the flesh teaches often in the Synagogue, seeks the fruit of faith, but finds it not in the Pharisees.

*And he said to the dresser of the vineyard.* The dresser of the vineyard: these are the teachers and the apostles, by whose prayers and admonitions the unceasing care of God is supplied; to whom the Lord often complains of the unfruitful nation of the Jews, that through the three years of his visitation, that is, in the edicts of the law, in the testimonies of the prophets, in the grace of the Gospel, it has been negligent.

*Why doth it also the ground,* etc. By the ground occupied by the barren fig tree may be signified the Jewish people, which is pressed by the harmful shadow of its rulers, that it may not see the light of truth, [0302D] and is hindered by their examples, that it may not grow warm with the sun of love; whence [n: (Matth. XXIII)]: *Woe to you, Scribes and Pharisees,* etc. He occupies the ground who does not work with good works the place that he holds, who by the example of his crookedness affords a hindrance to the rest.

VERS. 8.-- *Until I dig,* etc. That is, until I make the root of the unfruitful mind humble, by striking into it the fear both of present afflictions and of eternal damnation. For a ditch is in the lowest place, and rebuke, while it shows the mind to itself, humbles it. He knows how to till the ground who knows how to reprove vices. He understood that the hardness and pride of the Jews was the cause of their barrenness. And so he promises that hard hearts are to be dug with apostolic mattocks, and proud hearts to be called back to humility. [0303A] *And put on dung.* That is, I will bring back to their mind the abomination of the evils they have done, and will rouse up the grace of compunction, with the fruits of good work, as it were from the richness of the dung. For the sins of the flesh are called dung, which are laid to the root of the tree when the memory of the thought touches the conscience of its own crookedness; and while a man repents thereof, he returns, as it were by the touch of dung, to the fruitfulness of work.

VERS. 9.-- *And if indeed.* Although the order of repentance required that he should add "it shall be spared," or something of the kind, yet he subjoined nothing, but left the sentence hanging.

*But if not.* That is, if it shall not bear fruit, he at once annexed the judgment of the damnation to come, because he saw men more prone [0303B] to deny than to believe.

VERS. 10.-- *And he was teaching.* This similitude of the fig tree the Lord is said to have taught in the synagogue, that it might be intimated that the parable belonged to the Synagogue itself. For that he seeks fruit in the fig tree and finds none signifies that he lends the word to the Synagogue and she does not receive it; yet understanding that she is not to be rooted out wholly, but that a remnant is to be saved through faith, straightway in the same place there follows the healing of the primitive Church under the figure of the bowed woman. For the fig tree spared signifies the same thing as the woman set upright. *On the sabbaths.* On the sabbaths he teaches and raises up her that was bowed, because through the word of preaching he leads those raised up from temporal things to eternal into the rest of the resurrection.

[0303C]VERS. 11.-- *And behold a woman,* etc. This woman could not be raised up save after eighteen years, because human nature will not be able to lay aside this corruption and be raised up to put on the incorruption of eternal blessedness, unless it first fulfil the law which is contained in the ten precepts, and the grace in which the resurrection of Christ has been wrought and our own is awaited.

*Years,* etc. The number six, in which the creature of the world was perfected, signifies the perfection of works, and multiplied by three it renders eighteen; and it signifies those who in the three times of the Lord's visitation, namely before the law, and under the law, and under grace, have perfection of works. In this time therefore Judea was bent away from the uprightness of her mind, she who had known earthly things rather than heavenly, works.

[0303D]VERS. 14.-- *Because on the sabbath.* God rested on the sabbath from the works of the world. Whence Moses also commanded that on the sabbath men should keep holiday from servile and harmful action, prefiguring the time in which our secular works, but not indeed the religious, that is, of praising God, shall cease. In sign of this thing the Lord heals on the sabbath also. But the ruler of the synagogue, not understanding this, is deceived and deceives, because the law forbade not to heal a man on the sabbath, but to do servile works, that is, to be weighed down with sins.

VERS. 15.-- *Hypocrites.* He rightly calls them hypocrites, that is, dissemblers, who, while they desire to be seen as masters of the people, are not ashamed to put the healing of a man after the care of a beast. *Doth he not loose his ox.* Mystically. The ox that knew its owner and the ass that knew [0304A] its master's crib signify the Jewish and the Gentile people, each of which, loosed from the bonds of sin, laid aside the thirst and the heat of this world by a draught from the Lord's fountain. In these two animals, therefore, the Lord foretells the calling of the two peoples, the Jews withstanding it.

VERS. 16.-- *And this woman,* etc. A daughter of Abraham: every faithful soul is a daughter of Abraham, the Church gathered to the faith out of both peoples, which, the time of the law and of the Lord's resurrection being fulfilled, broke the bonds of a long captivity through the sevenfold grace of the Holy Spirit: and so it is the same thing for the ox or the ass to be loosed from the manger and led to drink, as for the daughter of Abraham to be raised up from the bond of a harmful bowing down

VERS. 17.-- *All were ashamed.* They who are ashamed of the sayings [0304B] of the Saviour show themselves like to the barren fig tree. They therefore who rejoice at the miracles belong to the daughter of Abraham, that is, to the Church, glorifying God for her raising up. *The adversaries.* The Scribes and Pharisees, who can neither openly gainsay the truth nor, because of envy, are willing to believe. *All the people rejoiced.* The simple and the humble, who love the words of truth and the miracles; and these are a figure of the Gentiles, who, the Jews being blinded, are to be gathered to the faith through humility. Whence the Saviour also commends their faith under the comparison of the grain of mustard seed.

VERS. 18.-- *He said therefore.* Since there were present those who rejoiced and glorified, he spoke to the commendation and the instruction of those who through humility and fervour of faith are to be exalted in the Church.

[0304C]VERS. 19.-- *It is like,* etc. The grain of mustard seed is a cheap and simple thing, but if it be crushed it pours out its own strength. The kingdom of heaven, therefore, that is, the faith through which one comes to the kingdom, is compared to a grain of mustard seed: because at first it is humble and as it were abject and of bitter savour, because of the weakness of Christ and the scandal of the cross; but crushed by adversities it pours forth the grace of its own power and fills those standing about with its odour, since even after death it preached the glory of the resurrection and of the ascension through the whole world. Or the body of Christ is the grain of mustard seed, which Joseph took and buried in the garden, and which afterwards grew in the resurrection and the ascension. It spread out its branches, sending preachers through the whole world, in whose words and consolations the faithful take breath from the weariness of the world.

[0304D]VERS. 21.-- *Which being taken,* etc. The woman is the Church, whose meal we are, as many of us as are ground, by the exercise of fear and of hope, as by an upper and a nether millstone, that we many may be one bread in Christ. The Church therefore hides the leaven of love in three measures of meal, because she bids us love God with the whole heart, with the whole soul, with the whole strength.

VERS. 22.-- *And he went,* etc. Everywhere he preached without distinction, not discerning persons, but seeking salvation for them that believe.

VERS. 24.-- *Strive to enter,* etc. As if he said: Truly they are few, and those with great labours, because unless the intention of the mind be fervent, the wave of the world is not overcome, by which the soul is always called back to the depths.

[0305A]VERS. 25.-- *But when,* etc. Christ the householder, though by his Godhead he is everywhere, is said to be already within to those whom, present in heaven, he gladdens by the sight of himself; but he is as it were without to those whom, hidden, he helps as they strive in this pilgrimage. He will enter therefore when he shall bring the whole Church to the contemplation of himself. He will shut the door when he shall take away from the reprobate the place of repentance, who, standing without, shall knock; that is, being separated from the just, they shall implore in vain the mercy which they despised. *You shall begin to stand without.* Strive now, in the time of mercy, because in the time of judgment the place of repentance is taken from the reprobate, which is now open to all that seek it dutifully.

*I know you not.* The light knows not the darkness, that is, it does not look upon it, because if it looked upon it, there would be no darkness.

[0305B]VERS. 26.-- *We have eaten,* etc. The feasting of festivals does not profit, which the piety of faith does not commend. The knowledge of the Scriptures does not make him known to God whom the iniquity of his works shows to be unworthy. Or it is to be understood simply of the Jews, who, spurning the mysteries of the faith, think themselves acceptable to God because they feast before the Lord in the temple upon the victims offered, and give ear to the reading of the prophets. Or mystically: he eats and drinks before the Lord who eagerly receives the food of the word.

*In the streets,* etc. Scripture in its more obscure places is food, because it is broken as it were by expounding and is swallowed by eating. For it is drink in the more open places, where it is drunk down just as it is found. Both the hidden things, therefore, and the open they testify that they have [0305C] understood, who, when the judge reproves them, complain of what they have eaten.



## CHAPTER XIII. (cont.)

VERS. 28.-- *There shall be weeping,* etc. Weeping is wont to be stirred up by heat, the gnashing of teeth by cold, because there they shall pass from the waters of snow to excessive heat. Or the gnashing betrays the feeling of one indignant, that he repents too late of what he stubbornly did amiss. Or they shall gnash with their teeth who here shall rejoice in gluttony. Those eyes shall weep which here shall wander through concupiscences. And note, through the weeping which belongs to the eyes and the gnashing of the teeth, the true resurrection of the wicked.

VERS. 29.-- *And they shall come.* Many who are fervent at first grow torpid afterwards. Many who are cold at first suddenly take fire. Many despised in this world are to be glorified in the world to come. Others glorious among men are to be condemned in the world to come. [0305D] Or the Gentiles, who had long lived without faith, are to be called to the faith, and the Jews to be execrated.

VERS. 32.-- *Say ye to the fox,* etc. The fox is a crafty animal, lurking in a hole for ambush, foul of smell, never going by straight paths. All these things suit the heretics, whose type Herod holds, who strive to quench Christ, that is, the humility of the Christian faith, in them that believe. *Behold I cast out demons,* etc. As if he said: Neither Herod nor the heretics, of whom he himself is the figure, shall be able to disturb what I purpose to do. And I purpose to cast out demons from the hearts of men, that, leaving vanities behind, they may believe in me; and to perfect healings, that they may live according to my precepts; afterwards I am consummated in my body, [0306A] which is the Church, which, glorified on the day of the resurrection, I shall bring to the fellowship of the angels.

VERS. 33.-- *Nevertheless I must.* Herod shall not be able to disturb it but that I so act and am so consummated; nor shall he be able to work the death of my body, which must come to pass in Jerusalem, where not he but Pilate bears rule. *To-day and to-morrow,* etc. The first day is to renounce vanities through the grace of God. The second, to be in accord with the truth in manners and in life. The third is the final glorification.

VERS. 34.-- *Jerusalem,* etc. Where my prophets are, there I, the head of the prophets, must be sacrificed; for which sin she herself shall justly be destroyed, and with that destruction of theirs I grieve as a devoted father. *How often would I have gathered.* As if he said: I would, and thou wouldest not: and as many as [0306B] I have gathered by my will, which is always effectual, I did it while thou wast unwilling, because thou hast always been ungrateful. *Thy children.* In this, that he says: *How often would I have gathered thy children,* etc., he bears witness to all the prophets sent by him in time past. *Even as a bird,* etc. Beautifully does he who had called Herod a fox, while he was plotting his death, compare himself to a bird, because the deceitful fox always lays ambushes for birds.

VERS. 35.-- *Your house.* The temple or the city, because, the Lord being slain, the Romans, plundering as it were an empty nest, took away both the place and the nation. *You shall not see me,* etc. Unless you do penance and confess me to be the Son of the almighty Father, you shall not see my face at the second coming. *When you shall say,* etc. This indeed the crowds said when the Lord was coming into Jerusalem. [0306C] But because Luke does not say that the Lord afterwards returned to the Galileans, so that this might be chanted to him by them, he compels it to be understood mystically of the coming of his brightness.



## CHAPTER XIV.

*And it came to pass,* etc. Ὕδωρ , water, whence the dropsy, a watery disease beneath the skin, born of a defect of the bladder, with swelling distension and fetid breath. And it is proper to the man with the dropsy that the more he drinks, the more he thirsts; and therefore he is compared to the man whom the overflowing flux of carnal pleasures weighs down, and he is compared also to the covetous rich man.

VERS. 3.-- *Is it lawful,* etc. On the sabbaths especially Jesus teaches and works, not only because of the spiritual sabbath, but also because of the more crowded gathering of the people, [0306D] seeking the salvation of all.

VERS. 4.-- *But they held their peace.* Rightly do they hold their peace, who see that whatever he had said would be said against them. For if it is lawful, why do they watch him? If it is not lawful, why do they care for their cattle? *But he.* He therefore heals the man with the dropsy before the Pharisees, and straightway disputes against avarice, that he may convict them of the avarice through which they violate the sabbath and ill interpret the law. For on the sabbath one must keep holiday not from good but from evil works, whence it is said: *No servile work shall you do therein [n: (Lev. XXIII)]*, that is, sin. So in the eternal rest there shall be holiday from evils only, not from good things.

VERS. 5.-- *Which of you.* By a fitting example he solves the question, that he may show that they violate the sabbath [0307A] in a work of covetousness who accuse him of violating it in a work of charity. *An ox into a pit.* Fittingly does he compare the man with the dropsy to an animal that has fallen into a pit, because he was perishing by a harmful humour. And well does he name the ox and the ass, that he may signify either the wise or the dull, or both peoples, namely the Jewish, pressed by the yoke of the law, and the Gentile, tamed by no reason but stolid and brutish; all of whom, sunk in the pit of concupiscence, he draws out: *For all have sinned and do need the grace of God [n: (Rom. XXXV)]*. [var: Vulg. Rom 3:23 reads *egent gloria Dei*, "do need the glory of God."]

VERS. 7.-- *And he said.* After he had convicted them of avarice and of the violation of the sabbath, he begins to teach concerning the grace of the Gospel those present who were fit, inviting them to humility against the swelling which the Pharisees had in the law, or intending in his teaching [0307B] how they ought to choose the places at table in the Church.

VERS. 8.-- *When thou art invited:* When, called through the grace of faith by a preacher, thou hast joined thyself to the members of the Church, do not, by glorying in thy merits, exalt thyself as though loftier than the rest. *Lest perhaps one more honourable.* He gives place to one more honourable invited after him, who, made too secure by confidence in his own manner of life, is quickly outstripped by the nimbleness of those who followed him in Christ. And he holds the last place with blushing, etc., when, coming to know better things of others, he humbles whatever lofty thing he had thought of his own working, saying with the Prophet: *But being exalted, I have been humbled and have confessed [n: (Psal. LXXXVII)]*. [var: Vulg. Ps 87:16 reads *conturbatus sum*, "I have been troubled"; the plate prints *confessus*, read at 450 dpi on leaf n159.]

VERS. 9.-- *And then thou begin,* etc. And then, blushing that thou didst once presume greater things of thyself, thou shalt begin to be humbled unto repentance, and to know thyself [0307C] of lesser merit, lest thou now begin to seek what is kept for thee at the end, because the inheritance to which men hasten in the beginning shall be wanting in the last blessing.

VERS. 10.-- *Then shalt thou have glory,* etc. Daily the Lord, while he visits and judges his guests, bestows such gifts of his Spirit upon the humble that the company of the faithful glorifies them with wonder; and, astonished, it leaps forth into the praise of its author, saying: *But to me thy friends, O God, are made exceedingly honourable [n: (Psal. CXXXVIII)]*.

VERS. 12.-- *And he said.* According to the order of the letter. After he had admonished those invited how they ought to sit at table, he admonishes the inviter also whom he ought to invite, lest the temporal spending of goods which he makes [0307D] pass away in vain, or perhaps increase his sin: but let him so spend temporal things that through them a way may lie open to things eternal. *And to him that had invited him.* He speaks to the chief of the Pharisees, who perhaps might grow proud because he had called so many to the banquet as though out of mercy; or, as may be gathered from the Lord's words, he had rather invited them for this, that he might be invited by them in turn, and so no special recompense would follow. *Do not call thy friends.* That brethren and friends and the rich should hold banquets for one another he does not forbid as though it were a crime, but he shows that it does not avail for the rewards of life. And there are certain banquets of such rich men which receive not only requitals in the present but even damnation in the time to come; whence the Apostle: *Not in rioting [0308A] and drunkenness [n: (Rom. VIII)]*, that is, in luxurious banquets, which are either celebrated by the contribution of all or are wont to be furnished by turns among companions, where no man is ashamed to do or say anything unseemly. *Lest perhaps.* As if he said: If thou purposest to invite that thou mayest be invited, therein thou canst be cheated; but if thou distribute to the poor, thou shalt not be cheated of thy reward.

VERS. 13.-- *Call the poor.* He that calls the poor shall receive a reward in the time to come. He that calls friends and brethren and the rich has received his reward. But if he does this for God's sake, after the example of the sons of Job, then, as in the other offices of brotherly love, he himself who commanded it rewards it. He that calls the wanton for the sake of lasciviousness shall not lack punishment.

VERS. 14.-- *In the resurrection.* Though all are to rise again, [0308B] yet the resurrection of the just is called singular and as it were their own, since they are to be changed in it.

VERS. 15.-- *When one had heard these things,* etc. Allured by the sweetness of the resurrection he had heard of, he breaks forth into the praise of it, and asserts that men shall be truly blessed therein, although he does not well understand by what merits that blessedness may be attained. *Blessed,* etc. *If any man eat of this bread, he shall live for ever [n: (Joan. VI)]*. If any man, perfectly incorporated into the sacrament of the incarnation, shall have deserved to enjoy the vision of the divine majesty, he shall rejoice in eternal blessedness. But because many receive it only so far as faith, as it were by praying, yet loathe to attain its sweetness truly, their torpor is reproved.

VERS. 16.-- *A certain man,* etc. The Word incarnate [0308C] has prepared for many the fulness of inward sweetness, but few come, because many who are subject to him through faith gainsay him continually by living ill. *A great supper.* This banquet is called not a dinner but a supper, because after the dinner of which Matthew treats a supper remains, and after the supper no banquet remains: from the dinner many are cast out, and from the supper no one.

VERS. 17.-- *And he sent his servant.* The hour of the supper is the end of the world; whence the Apostle: We know *upon whom the ends of the ages are come [n: (I Cor. X)]*. In this end the servant is sent, that is, the order of preachers, to those invited through the law and the prophets, that, loathing being put away, they might prepare themselves to taste the supper, because all things are now ready. For Christ being sacrificed, the entrance of the kingdom [0308D] lies open.

VERS. 18.-- *And they began,* etc. They therefore make excuse, because the kingdom is shut against no man save him whom the profession of his own voice shall have shut out. And every man excuses himself who loves earthly things more than heavenly, even if he say that he is reaching toward heavenly things. *I have bought a farm.* The farm is earthly substance. He goes out therefore to see the farm who thinks of outward things alone for the sake of substance; and this is what is first sought by the lovers of the world, namely the substance of the body.

[n: (AUG.)] In the farm that is bought, lordship and pride are noted. For to have a farm, etc., *as far as* the love of earthly things is birdlime to spiritual wings.

[0309A] *I pray thee.* While he says: *I pray thee, hold me excused,* humility sounds in his voice while he scorns to come, pride in his deed.

VERS. 19.-- *Yokes of oxen.* The five yoke of oxen are the five senses of the body, which are rightly called yokes, since they are doubled in either sex; and because they know and touch not inward things but outward only, by them curiosity is rightly designated, which, while it searches out the life of a neighbour without, knows not its own inward parts. And the mind of a curious man, the more skilled it is in what is another's, the more ignorant it is of its own. Nor do the words of the man excusing himself disagree with the signification of his own vice, when he says: *I go to try them.* For trying is wont to belong to curiosity.

VERS. 20.-- *I have married a wife.* Because many take wives not for the sake of fruitfulness but for the desires of the flesh, [0309B] therefore by this thing the pleasure of the flesh is designated, for the sake of which the fastidious man refuses to come to the supper of God.

VERS. 22.-- *It is done,* etc. Many such of the Jews have we gathered to thy supper, but these do not fill the place of thy banquet; there remains a place where the great number of the Gentiles may be received.

VERS. 23.-- *Go out into the highways and hedges,* etc. Note that in this third invitation it is not said, Invite, but Compel them to come in. For they who, broken by the adversities of this world, return to the love of God, and are corrected from the desires of the present life, do not enter unless compelled. But some are called and despise it, who receive the house of understanding but do not fulfil it in work. Some are called and come, who fulfil in work the [0309C] understanding they have received.

VERS. 25.-- *And there went crowds,* etc. The crowds that follow Jesus as he makes his way to Jerusalem signify those who, invited or compelled, come to the supper, and with their whole intent imitate Jesus going before them to the vision of eternal peace. These therefore he instructs, who ought with sincere love to seek that country, and for its sake to renounce the affections of carnal love.

VERS. 26.-- *If any man come.* We can at once both hate and love: so that we love both those joined to us by acquaintance and by the divine precept, and those whom we suffer as adversaries in the way of God we may not know, by hating and fleeing them. *Yea more.* Then do we rightly hate our own soul, when we do not consent to its carnal desires, but struggle against them and break them.

[0309D]VERS. 27.-- *And whosoever doth not carry.* The cross is carried in two ways: either when the flesh is afflicted by abstinence, or when the mind is afflicted by compassion for a neighbour. *And come after me.* For if a man tears his flesh for empty glory, or has compassion on his neighbour carnally so as to favour his fault, he seems to bear the cross, but he does not follow God.

VERS. 28.-- *For which of you.* After the Lord had given to those following him into Jerusalem lofty precepts concerning contempt of the world and the casting away of the body, he adds a precept concerning perseverance, for which patience with humility is necessary. And this by the likeness of one that builds. *To build a tower.* Because he had given lofty precepts, he subjoins a comparison of a loftiness to be built. [0310A] *Reckoneth the charges.* He reckons the charges who discerns that temporal money must be paid out, and the heart turned away from the covetous desires of the world, and the soul made ready against the adversities of the world.

VERS. 29.-- *All that see.* As the Apostle says: *We are made a spectacle to the world, to angels and to men [n: (II Cor. IV)]*. And in everything that we do we ought to consider our hidden adversaries, who lie in wait for our works; and unless we watch against them, we suffer as mockers those whom we have as persuaders to evil.

VERS. 31.-- *Or what,* etc. After the similitude of the tower to be built, he adds the similitude of a greater matter, of a king about to fight. In which it is shown that by the fulfilment of the preceding commandments no man can attain to glory [0310B] unless the sentence of the severe judge be tempered by mercy. For in the sight of God every man is imperfect. *If he be able.* He meets God as it were with ten thousand who offers the works of the ten commandments which he has performed outwardly. But God comes against him as it were with a doubled number, when he examines him, scarcely prepared in work alone, at once concerning both work and thought. And therefore, while he is still far off through the delay of the judgment, we, considering the insufficiency of our frailty, ought to send before us, for the obtaining of the severe judge's peace, an embassy of tears, of good works, of holy affection.



## CHAPTER XIV. (cont.)

VERS. 33.-- *So likewise,* etc. He infers this from the foregoing similitudes. Since he cannot build well who does not reckon the charges, nor can he safely go to meet another [0310C] who does not send an embassy before him, therefore neither can he be my disciple who does not renounce all things.

VERS. 34.-- *Good.* Truly, whoever wishes to be my disciple must renounce all things, for so he will be good salt, that is, worthy to be set in the number of the apostles, for the seasoning of the hearts of his hearers with spiritual wisdom.

VERS. 35.-- *Nor into the dunghill.* Salt makes the ground barren. Whence the wrath of conquerors used to sow with salt the cities they had destroyed, that no shoot might spring up in them. So the apostles, the kingdom of sin being destroyed, restrain the shoot of vices. Salt seasons food and dries flesh. Preaching keeps human nature unharmed for its Creator from the worms and the rottenness of vices.



## CHAPTER XV.

[0310D]

VERS. 4.-- *Who of you,* etc. In what went before thou hadst learned to renounce negligence, to shun arrogance, to take up devotion, not to be held fast by worldly occupations, not to prefer things that fall to things that abide. But because human frailty cannot keep a firm footing on so slippery a world, the physician there shows a remedy against error also, namely, that the judge does not deny the hope of pardon. *A hundred sheep.* Because the number of a hundred is a perfect number. God had a hundred sheep, that is, a perfect number, when he created the substance of angels and of men; but one perished, when man by sinning forsook the pastures of life: therefore, because [0311A] the number of the rational creature was diminished by the perishing of man, he seeks man on earth that the sum may be made whole.

*Doth he not leave.* As if he said: You murmur, because I receive sinners, but I came not save to gather those going astray, as each one of you labours to bring back a straying sheep.

VERS. 5.-- *He layeth it upon his shoulder.* He set the sheep upon his shoulders, who, taking up human nature, himself bore our sins: whence the shoulders of Christ are the arms of the cross; there he laid down our sins, on that neck of the noble gibbet he rested.

VERS. 6.-- *And coming.* When man was repaired, the shepherd returns to heaven; there he finds the choirs of angels. They are friends, because in their steadfastness they keep his will continually; [0311B] and they are neighbours, because they enjoy the brightness of his vision. And note that he does not say: Rejoice with the sheep that is found, but *with me,* because his joy is our life.

VERS. 7.-- *I say to you.* In the ninety-nine, the number of the just is understood; in the one sheep, that of sinners. *Joy.* The angels, because they are rational, rejoice over a man reconciled to them, which sets us on fire toward uprightness: let us do what is pleasing to them, whose patronage we ought both to court and whose offence we ought to fear. *Than over.* If in the ninety-nine just who have not erred nor need penitence the angels are understood, it seems a greater thing to justify the impious than to create and preserve the just, because although each is of equal power, here there is greater mercy and greater joy.

[0311C]VERS. 8.-- *Or what woman.* The woman, that is, the Wisdom of God, had ten drachmas, when she created men and angels to her own image. But she lost one, when man withdrew from the likeness of his Creator. But the Wisdom of God kindled a lamp to seek with, when she appeared in the flesh, because a lamp is a light in an earthen vessel, that is, the Word in flesh. And when this shone forth among men, it overturned the house, because it troubled the consciences of men concerning their own guilt, and so the drachma is found again, while in man the likeness of the Maker is repaired. *Having ten drachmas.* The nature of angels and of men, which the Lord willed to subsist unto eternity, he created to his own likeness. Ten drachmas therefore the woman had, when to the nine orders of angels man was added, that the tenth [0311D] number of the elect might be completed. And that number did not perish from its Creator even after the fault, because the Wisdom of God, flashing with miracles through the flesh, repaired it from the light of the earthen vessel. *She calleth together.* The friends and the neighbours are the powers above, which are near to God in the measure that they contemplate him more nearly. And she calls them, while through the showing of the grace bestowed upon men she kindles them more vehemently to the love of herself.

VERS. 11.-- *A certain man.* By the two parables set before, he intimates how greatly he himself with the angels rejoices over the salvation of the penitent. In this third one he not only shows the joy, but also rebukes the murmur of the envious.

VERS. 12.-- *And he said,* etc. As God has no other by whom he may be formed, by whom he is denied, so this man wishes to use his own power, [0312A] that he may live with none forming him, none ruling him. *Father, give.* The half of the patrimony is given to those who ask, nor is it the fault of the father who gives to the younger. No age is weak in the kingdom of God, nor is faith weighed down by years, unless indeed he who asks has judged himself unfit. If this man had not withdrawn from his father, he would have known no hindrance of age, but having journeyed into a far country he deservedly wastes the patrimony. *And he divided.* To the faithful, by imparting the protection of his grace which they desired: but to the unfaithful, by granting only the natural benefit of native wit, with which they were content.

VERS. 13.-- *Not after many.* Not long after the institution of the human race, it pleased the soul to carry off with it free choice, as a kind of power of its own nature, and to desert him by whom it was founded, [0312B] trusting in its own strength. And it consumes those strengths the more swiftly, the more it deserts the giver. This is the prodigal life, which loves to pour itself out and to range abroad in outward shows, leaving him who is more inward to it

VERS. 14.-- *And he began.* Because nothing is enough for prodigal pleasure, because pleasure always has a hunger of itself; deservedly therefore is he in want who has left behind the treasures of wisdom and the height of heavenly riches.

VERS. 15.-- *To one of the citizens.* That one of the citizens is he who, being set over earthly concupiscences by the desert of his own depravity, is called the prince of the world. Who sends him into a farm, because he subjects him to the greed of worldly substance. He feeds swine, when he works those things in which unclean spirits rejoice. *That he might feed swine.* The swine is an unclean animal, which delights in filth; so the devil, [0312C] who is fed in idols with the blood of cattle, and with the death of man himself. He sent him therefore into the farm, that is, made him his own servant, that he might feed swine, sacrificing to him his own soul.

VERS. 16.-- *And he desired.* The food of demons is drunkenness, fornication and the like, which are enticing and provoke to their own use. Nor have the wanton any other care save to fill their belly, *whose god is their belly [n: (Philip. III)]*. But he cannot be satisfied with them, because pleasure always has a hunger of itself. *No man gave unto him.* Because when the devil makes any man his own, he takes no further trouble for an abundance of vices, knowing him to be already dead.

VERS. 17.-- *How many hired servants.* They who with an eye to the reward to come are busy to work worthy things, are refreshed with the daily [0312D] nourishment of grace from above.
