But he perishes of hunger who, living outside the father's house, that is, without faith, seeks the blessed life in empty philosophy.

VERS. 18.-- *Father, I have sinned.* Although God knows all things, yet he awaits the voice of confession. Without danger thou mayest disclose what thou knowest is already known: thou hast a cause why Christ should intercede for thee, nor has he died for thee in vain; the Father too has a cause for pardoning, because what the Son wills the Father also wills.

VERS. 19.-- *Make me,* etc. He does not presume to aspire to the affection of a son, who does not doubt that all that is the Father's is his own, but he desires the station of a hired servant, now about to serve for wages, and yet he testifies that he cannot earn even this except by the father's condescension.

[0313A] [n: (AMBR.)] There is a difference between a slave, a hired servant and a son. A slave, etc., *as far as* which after the father's kiss he most nobly disdains.

VERS. 20.-- *But when he was yet a great way off,* etc. Before he understood God, but yet when he was already seeking him dutifully, yet was not yet returning worthy in works, through the Word incarnate he anticipates the son's return. He came to the earth before that man came to the house of confession, not deserting the Word itself, but in the Word condescending as far as our pilgrimage. The arm, that is, the Son, he humbled into the embrace of the one returning. The father fell upon the son's neck, when he laid upon him the light yoke of his own love, and kissed him, that there might be shown what is said elsewhere: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth [n: (Cant. I)]*. Returning, [0313B] he receives the kiss of charity from the father, when through grace he is made certain of pardon.

VERS. 22.-- *Bring forth quickly,* etc. The first robe, the garment of innocence, in which the first man was founded, but after the fault he knew himself naked, and took a garment of skin, that is, a mortal one. *A ring.* That is, the seal of faith, with which the promises are sealed in the hearts of believers. *In the hand.* That is, in working: that through works faith may shine out, and through faith works may be made firm. *And shoes.* That is, fortify the examples of those before you in the office of preaching, that both the work may be adorned by living well, and the course by preparing for things eternal.

VERS. 23.-- *Bring the calf.* Preach Christ and make known his death, that a man may both believe in his heart, [0313C] by imitating him who was slain, and receive with his mouth the sacrament of the passion unto amendment.

VERS. 24.-- *And they began to feast.* Not only is the son refreshed for whom the calf was slain, but the father also and his servants, because the refreshment of God and of the saints is the salvation of sinners. And note that after the robe, the ring, the shoe were given, the calf is sacrificed, because unless a man has put on the hope of the first immortality, unless he has fortified his works with the ring of faith, unless he has preached that very faith by confessing it, he cannot take part in the heavenly sacraments.

VERS. 25.-- *Now his son was,* etc. The son, that is, the people of Israel, who had not gone far off to the worship of idols. Neither was he in the house, because he did not penetrate the inward things of the law: in the literal sense, content, working earthly things, [0313D] awaiting earthly things. This is he who bought a field, and five yoke of oxen, who, pressed by the burden of the law, enjoys earthly senses, and took a wife in the affection of the flesh. *And when he came.* The son draws near to the house when someone among that people, understanding the law spiritually, condemns the labours of servile work, and considers the liberty of the Church. He hears music and a choir, that is, men full of the Spirit preaching the Gospel with concordant voices. He calls one of the servants, when he takes up some one of the prophets to read. And he questions him, while he searches in him what these feasts of the Church may be in which he does not see himself to be.

VERS. 26.-- *He asked.* And now Israel asks why God rejoices in the taking up of the Gentiles, and by reason of [0314A] envy he does not know the father's will, and stands outside, although he is entreated through heralds.

VERS. 28.-- *Being indignant.* The Jews were indignant and complained, because Jesus feasted with the Gentiles. And still they are indignant at the salvation of the Gentiles and will not go in. But when the fulness of the Gentiles has come in, the father will go out to entreat Israel, when the calling of the Jews shall be made manifest.

VERS. 29.-- *Behold, so many years. [n: (HIER.)]* Following the righteousness of the law, he is not subject to the righteousness of God. For what greater righteousness of God is there than to pardon the penitent?

*Thy commandment,* etc. As though it were not to transgress it, to envy another's salvation, or to boast oneself of one's righteousness.

VERS. 30.-- *Thou hast killed for him the calf.* They confess that [0314B] Christ has come, but envy wills not to be saved. So it is of the Jew and the Gentile. But generally it can be taken of the just man and the sinner. *He hath devoured his substance,* etc. The harlots are the superstitions of the Gentiles, with whom he squanders his substance who, having left the wedlock of the Word of God, fornicates with the demon in foul greed. *Son, thou art always,* etc. He does not refute him as one lying, but approving his perseverance with himself, and inviting him to the full enjoyment of a mightier exultation. *All that is mine,* etc. In eternal blessedness, both the single things of all and all the things of each single one.



## CHAPTER XVI.

VERS. 1.-- *To the disciples.* Because the Pharisees, being proud and avaricious, just as they denied pardon to those who asked it, so [0314C] denied money to those in need; therefore the Saviour now speaks to them, now in their hearing speaks to the disciples, concerning mercy. *A certain man.* A bailiff, properly, is the keeper of a farm, but the word is put here for a steward, that is, a dispenser, who dispenses the whole substance of the house. For in this dispenser we ought not to take all things for imitation, namely that we should act fraudulently toward God. But on the contrary these similitudes are given, that we may understand that if he who wrought fraud could be praised by his lord, how much more does he please God who does those works according to his precept.

VERS. 6.-- *A hundred cadi.* A cadus in Greek is called in Latin an amphora, holding three urns. A chorus is filled with thirty pecks. It can simply be taken thus: whoever [0314D] relieves the need of a poor man, whether by a half or by a fifth part, is to be endowed with the wages of his own mercy.

VERS. 8.-- *And the Lord commended.* If the dispenser of the unjust mammon is praised by the Lord's own voice because out of an unjust thing he made ready righteousness for himself, and, though he suffered loss, yet he praises his prudence, not because he had acted fraudulently against his lord, but because he had acted prudently for himself: how much more shall Christ, who can sustain no loss and is inclined to clemency, praise the disciples, if they have been merciful toward those entrusted to them. *The children of this world.* Whose works a man does, his son also he is called; whence: *The children of this world,* that is, of darkness; and the children of light, of eternal life.

[0315A]VERS. 9.-- *Make unto you.* Mammon, in the tongue of the Syrians, is the riches of iniquity, because they were gathered out of iniquity. If therefore iniquity well dispensed is turned into righteousness, how much more do the feasts of the divine word, in which there is no iniquity, lift up a good dispenser into heaven.

VERS. 10.-- *He that is faithful.* In money that is to be shared with the poor man. *And in that which is greater.* Cleaving to the Creator, and made one spirit with him. But he who does not well dispense the temporal things he possesses, empties out for himself the glory of eternal things, over which he swells.

VERS. 11.-- *If therefore,* etc. He who does not give to his brethren for their use what was created by God for all, will be unjust in dividing spiritual money, so that he divides the Lord's [0315B] teaching not according to need, but according to persons.

VERS. 12.-- *And if in that which is another's.* If you do not well dispense the carnal riches which slip away, who will give you the true and eternal riches?

VERS. 13.-- *No servant.* Rebuking avarice, he says that the lovers of money are not able God with love; he therefore who loves God despises money: for it avails not to love things transitory and things eternal at once.

*Or he will hold to the one. [n: (AUG.)]* He will not indeed love. For no man loves, etc., *as far as* and he flatters himself concerning his goodness and his impunity.

*You cannot,* etc. He does not say to have riches, but to serve them. He serves who guards them as a servant; but he who has shaken off the yoke of servitude distributes as a lord. He who serves mammon assuredly serves him who, being set over these things by the desert of his own perversity, [0315C] is called by the Lord the prince of the world.

VERS. 14.-- *Now they heard.* The Lord was admonishing the scribes and Pharisees not to be proudly wise, not to presume upon their own righteousness, but to receive penitent sinners: and to redeem by alms their own sins into which they might chance to fall. But they deride the teacher of mercy, of humility and of thrift, as though, being harmful, he were commanding things less useful and never to be done, or else things useful indeed, but, being superfluous, were pressing upon them things already accomplished by themselves.

VERS. 15.-- *And he said,* etc. While you despise sinners as weak and as men without hope, and believe yourselves perfect in all things and free of every frailty, and to have no need of the remedy of almsgiving. But how justly this height of swelling [0315D] is to be condemned, he sees who enlightens the hidden things of darkness, and makes manifest the counsels of hearts.

VERS. 16.-- *The law and the prophets.* They derided him as he disputed against the love of money, as though it were contrary to the law and the prophets: since in the law many rich men pleased God, and since the law promises to those who keep it the good things of the earth; whom he meets, teaching that there is a distance between the law and the Gospel: because in the law lesser things are commanded to carnal men for the sake of the earth, and in the Gospel greater things are commanded to spiritual men for the sake of heaven.

*And every one.* Great is the violence, when by birth from earth he seeks to possess by virtue heaven, which he could not hold by nature, when he despises even [0316A] the tongues of those who deride, as the Lord is made void by the Pharisees. Every one who does violence hastens with vehement zeal, he does not slacken with a torpid affection. There is therefore a religious violence of faith, and a criminal sloth. Let us then do violence to nature, that it may not sink us to earthly things, but raise itself to things above.

VERS. 17.-- *It is easier.* Although I say, *The law and the prophets until John,* yet I do not preach the destruction of the law: because it is easier for the greatest elements of the world to pass than the least precepts of the law, because even those which seem light or superstitious are not empty of spiritual sacraments, since all things are recapitulated in the Gospel also.

VERS. 18.-- *Every one* etc. [n: (AMBR.)] Not every wedlock is from God. For Christian women are not [0316B] joined to Gentiles, since the law forbids it. Where there is an unequal wedlock, the law of God is not there.

VERS. 19.-- *A certain man.* Against the avaricious mockers he builds up by an example what he had set forth, namely that the rich man is tormented among those below, who did not make Lazarus his friend, by whom he might have been received into eternal tabernacles; and it seems rather a narrative than a parable.

*Who was clothed in purple,* etc. [n: (GREG.)] Purple is the colour of royal apparel, from sea-shells, etc., *as far as* because he knew nothing of the bowels of pity, because he was unwilling to redeem the precepts with a price.

VERS. 20.-- *And there was a certain.* If we see anything blameworthy in the poor, we ought not to despise them, because perhaps those whom weakness of manners wounds, the medicine of poverty heals. *Full of sores.* At this man's [0316C] sores the fastidious rich man shudders, lest amid precious banquets and perfumed guests he should endure the stench of sores with the dogs licking them.

VERS. 21.-- *Desiring.* In one thing two judgments of God are fulfilled: from the sight of the poor man a heap of damnation is brought upon the rich man who does not pity; and again from the sight of the rich man the poor man, tempted daily, is proved, whom, for his own greater proving, poverty together with sickness, and the beheld plenty of the rich man, and no consolation applied to him, afflict. *The dogs.* In the tongue of the dogs there can be signified the tongue of flatterers who lick wounds, while with wicked favour they praise the evils which we blame in ourselves.

VERS. 22.-- *Into Abraham's bosom.* Abraham's bosom is the rest of the blessed poor, whose is the kingdom [0316D] of heaven, into which after this life they are received. The burial of hell is the depth of punishments, which devours the proud and the unmerciful after this life. *And he was buried.* In the law not niggardliness but rapine is condemned; in the Gospel the rich man is tormented because he did not give what was his own.

VERS. 23.-- *And lifting up.* The unfaithful see afar off, while, set in the depth before the day of judgment, they mark the faithful above them in rest, whose joys afterwards they cannot behold. What they descry is far off, because they do not reach thither by merit.

*He saw Abraham. [n: (GREG.)]* It is to be believed that before the retribution of the last judgment the unjust in rest, etc., *as far as* and they are tormented by the punishment of those whom they loved uselessly.

[0317A]VERS. 24.-- *That he may dip.* He who did not give to Lazarus the crumbs falling from the table, desires that water be distilled into his mouth from the tip of that man's finger; and he who gave not the least things in life, in hell seeks the least things.

VERS. 25.-- *Because thou hast received. [n: (AUG.)]* Note that this rich man had something of good, out of which he received a transitory good in this life, and Lazarus had something of evil, but the want of fire purged it.

VERS. 26.-- *And in all these things.* Just as the reprobate wish to pass over from their punishments to the glory of the saints, so the just wish in mind through mercy to go to those set in torments, that they may free them; but they cannot, because the souls of the just, though in the goodness of their own nature they have mercy, are yet now joined to the righteousness of their Author and are bound with so great a rectitude that they are moved by no [0317B] compassion toward the reprobate.

VERS. 27.-- *I beseech thee therefore.* After hope for himself is taken from the burning man, his mind runs back to his kinsmen, because the punishment of the reprobate sometimes uselessly instructs their mind unto charity, so that then at last they love even their own spiritually, who here, while they loved their sins, did not even love themselves. There is kept therefore for the rich man unto punishment both the knowledge of the poor man whom he despised, and the memory of the brethren whom he left behind, that he may be the more tormented by the beheld glory of the despised and by the punishment of those he uselessly loved. *That thou wouldst send.* Too late does this rich man begin to be a master, when he has now neither time for learning nor for teaching.

VERS. 31.-- *If Moses.* They who despise the words of the law, by how much the more subtle the precepts of the Redeemer, who rose from the dead, are, by so much the more hardly will they fulfil these, [0317C] and him whose sayings they refuse to fulfil, him without doubt they refuse to believe.



## CHAPTER XVII.

VERS. 1.-- *And he said to the disciples.* It is to be known that when the Lord was speaking of mercy, the avaricious Pharisees persevered in mockery, wherein they made a scandal for weak hearers, that these might not attend. These therefore he rebukes while speaking to the apostles, namely for what they were doing by their own vice, so that scandals came about through them which were not able not to come about in a world most full of hardships and errors.

VERS. 2.-- *If a millstone,* etc. According to the custom of the province where this punishment of crimes obtained, that with a stone bound to them they should be plunged into the deep. And truly it is more profitable for an innocent man to end his life by the heaviest punishment, than by harming a brother to earn [0317D] perpetual death. *Of the little ones.* Not in age, but in faith and mind, who seek occasions how they may be scandalized; therefore you must take counsel for these, lest by occasion of you they fall away from faith and from salvation.

VERS. 3.-- *Take heed to yourselves.* He intimates by what order we may turn aside scandals and escape the everlasting woe. But if we take heed that we harm no one, if we rebuke the penitent with the zeal of justice, if from the heart we forgive him who repents, we are not commanded to forgive one who sins at random, but one who does penance. First therefore is it to rebuke him who sins, that afterwards we may be able to forgive him who repents. For he who sees a man sinning and is silent sins as much [0318A] as he who does not pardon a penitent. *And if he do penance.* We are bidden to give pardon in one way to a penitent brother, in another to a persecuting enemy. To a brother we impart fellowship in charity with the remission granted; to a persecutor who wills evil to us, or who does it if he can, we will good things and do what we can.

VERS. 4.-- *Seven times.* Universality is wont to be designated by the number seven. It is commanded therefore that all sins be forgiven, or that it be always forgiven to one who repents.

VERS. 5.-- *Increase.* Since he had said above [n: (Luc. XVI)]: *He that is faithful in that which is least,* etc. The apostles, who in that which was another's and in that which was least, that is, in the contempt of earthly things, had been faithful, ask that faith be increased in what is their own and greater, that they who from faith already have the beginnings of virtue may advance in the same, until they come to perfection.

[0318B]VERS. 6.-- *To the mulberry tree.* By the mulberry, of blood-red colour, whose fruit and twigs are ruddy, the Gospel of the cross is expressed, which by the faith of the apostles was uprooted out of the nation of the Jews (in which, as in the stock of the race, the word of preaching was held) and transplanted into the sea of the Gentiles; to which the parable subjoined concerning the ministers of the word also fits. The leaves of the mulberry laid under a serpent bring death, and the word of the cross, as it confers all wholesome things, takes away all noxious ones. The fruit of the mulberry is white in flower, when formed grows red, in ripeness turns black: so the devil, of one shining red with the flower of the angelic nature and with power, by prevaricating bristled up, a terror, in odour, who is shut out through faith.

VERS. 7.-- *But which of you,* etc. The servant ploughing or feeding is the doctor of the Church, of whom it is said: *No man [0318C] putting his hand to the plough and looking back is fit for the kingdom [n: (Luc. IX)]* of God. And the Lord says to Peter: *Feed my sheep [n: (Joan. XXI)]*. Which servant returns from the field when, the work of preaching being intermitted, returning as it were to the court of conscience he goes over his own words or deeds; whom the Lord does not at once bid to pass out of this life and be cherished in eternal rest, but to prepare at home what he may sup on, that is, after the labour of open speech, to display the humility of his own manner of life, for in such a conscience God sups.

VERS. 8.-- *And minister to me.* He ministers to his Maker who, considering his nature and greatly fearing his judgments, humbles himself from his own virtues. For a minister is so called from a lesser estate.

VERS. 10.-- *So you also.* After he has exhorted to the perfection [0318D] of faith, through which wonderful works are done even against the nature of things, he warns that no one should glory of his own works, but that by how much faith is outwardly more excellent in virtues, by so much the conscience within should be more humble, because we owe obedience to the Lord by right. *Unprofitable servants.* If a man exacts from a man who is his servant not a uniform but a manifold ministry, and gives him no thanks, how much more ought you who without me can do nothing not to practise the use of one work or one labour only, but always to increase former things with new zeal, nor should you boast, because you serve well.

*What we ought to do.* He who came to minister and not to be ministered unto has made us debtors to himself, that not trusting in our own works, but fearing his examination, when all things [0319A] are fulfilled which have been commanded, we may recognize that we are unperfected, so long as we are pilgrims from God.

VERS. 11.-- *And it came to pass.* After the parable by which he invites to kindness and to perseverance in the diverse offices of God, the ungrateful are reproved concerning benefits bestowed.

VERS. 12.-- *There met him,* etc. The lepers are heretics, who, having as it were various colours in the same body, mingle various sects, now of falsehood, now of truth, in the same preaching. But because these are shunned and removed far from the Church, they must needs cry out with a great clamour.

*Who stood,* etc. They who have sinned against the Decalogue, loving neither God (of whom they think ill) nor the neighbour, from whom they are divided, cry under the number ten to God [0319B] and are healed, while in the fellowship of the Church they attain a whole and true teaching, and expound all things according to the rule of the Catholic faith, and are free of the variety of lies as of leprosy. But one gives thanks, that is, they who remain in the unity of the Church through humility; but they who, puffed up through pride, are ungrateful to him who cleansed them, are nine, because by one unit they fall short of the perfection of the number ten.

VERS. 13.-- *And they lifted up their voice.* Because they acknowledge that they have erred in the words of the Saviour, they call upon the master who is to save them, and through the knowledge of the master they return to the form of salvation.

VERS. 14.-- *Go,* etc. Whoever, cleansed by the grace of God, has been rid of heretical depravity, or of Gentile superstition, or of Jewish faithlessness, or of brotherly schism, [0319C] must needs come to the Church, and show a true colour of faith like to that of others.

VERS. 15.-- *But one.* And the one who returned signifies the devout humility in Christ of the one Church, who, falling at his feet and giving thanks, because with the thoughts of presumption repressed he sees how weak he is, ascribes nothing of virtue to himself; the good things which he does he understands to be of mercy.

VERS. 16.-- *And he fell on his face.* He falls on his face who blushes for the evils he has committed. For a man falls there where he is put to confusion. He who falls on his face sees whither he falls; he who falls backward does not see. The good therefore fall on their face, because they humble themselves in these visible things, that they may be raised to the invisible; the evil fall backward, because they fall among invisible things, where they do not see what follows them.

[0319D]VERS. 19.-- *Arise.* He who, acknowledging his own weakness, has lain humbly before God, is bidden by divine consolation to rise to stronger works, and to go on increasing as far as things more perfect. *Thy faith.* The faith which inclined him to give thanks; on the contrary, faithlessness condemns those who are ungrateful for benefits received. *Hath made thee whole.* In the parable above it is determined that faith ought to be increased through humility. In this one it is manifestly shown by the facts themselves, that it is not the acknowledged reason of faith only, but the executed working of faith, that makes the believer whole.

VERS. 20.-- *Being asked.* They ask for this reason, because, as Luke discloses further on, they thought that, when the Lord came [0320A] to Jerusalem, his kingdom would forthwith be made manifest.

*The kingdom of God cometh not,* etc. Because, when it shall come, it can be observed neither by angels nor by men, as the time of the Lord's incarnation was both foreappointed by the oracles of the prophets and made manifest by the proclamations of angels, so that he was declared conceived, born, baptized, preaching, dying, rising, ascending to the heavens, with the tokens either of angels, or of men, or certainly of miracles unceasingly accompanying him.

VERS. 21.-- *For behold the kingdom.* It will not be able to be observed, because my kingdom is not bodily, as you think, but spiritual, which has already begun by faith.

*The kingdom of God.* Or the Lord's answer can be taken thus: [0320B] *The kingdom of God is within you,* who, one day to come as the Judge of all, even now reigns in the hearts of the faithful. But when heretics trouble the Church with depraved teachings, the faithful desire that the Lord, returning to the earth for one day, if it could be, would make known the truth of the faith. But they shall not see it, because that which has once been displayed by the brightness of the Gospel has no need to be confirmed by a bodily vision of the Lord.

VERS. 22.-- *The days shall come.* He would not say when he should come, but he said that he would come, that he might strike into all the terror of the imminent judgment, and not bring the security of a delay.

*You shall desire.* After my ascension, when you, groaning in this exile of the world, shall desire to see me in my glory, which will is to be praised, yet not [0320C] to be fulfilled in this world. *One day.* Well does he say one, because in that glory, of which the prophet says: *Better is one day in thy courts above thousands [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*, there is no interruption of darkness, no memory even of misery or of grief. It is good to desire the presence of this day, yet not by the greatness of desire to feign dreams for ourselves, as though the day of the Lord were at hand, which he foretells will be done by some.

VERS. 23.-- *And they will say,* etc. There have been some who, reckoning the courses of the ages, said that they had found the certain year, day and hour of the consummation of the world.

VERS. 24.-- *For as,* etc. Do not believe them, because the second coming will be sudden, and so manifest that none will be able to be ignorant of it from under heaven, [0320D] because the crisis of the judgment is carried on under heaven, that is, in the midst of the air.

VERS. 25.-- *But first,* etc. Speaking of the glory of his coming, he inserts something also of the passion, so that when they saw dying him whom they had heard was to reign, they might soften the grief of the passion by the hope of the promised brightness.

VERS. 26.-- *And as it came to pass.* He builds up the sudden day of his coming by many examples, and that which he had compared to lightning (which swiftly flies across all things) he makes equal to the days of Noe or of Loth, when a sudden destruction came upon mortals.

VERS. 27.-- *They did eat,* etc. It is not marriages or foods that are condemned, since in these are set the supports of succession, in those the supports of nature, but the immoderate [0321A] use of things lawful is reproved. For because by giving themselves wholly to these they were despising the judgments of God, they perished by fire or by water. *Until the day.* Allegorically: Noe builds the ark, when the Lord gathers the *fideres* into the Church. And when it is finished he enters it, when on the day of judgment he lights it up with his presence. But while the ark is being built the wicked are wanton, and while it is being entered they are struck with eternal damnation.

VERS. 28.-- *In like manner,* etc. Passing over the greatest crime of the Sodomites, he mentions those things alone which might be thought either light or nothing at all, that thou mayest understand with what punishment things unlawful are struck, if things lawful and those without which life is not led, when done more immoderately, shall be punished with fire and brimstone.

*Loth.* Allegorically: Loth, who is interpreted *declining,* is the people of the elect, who while they dwell in Sodom, [0321B] that is, among the reprobate as a stranger, decline their crimes as far as they can. When Loth goes out, Sodom perishes, because *in the consummation of the world the angels shall go out, and shall separate the wicked from among the just, and shall cast them into the furnace of fire [n: (Matth. XIII)]*.

VERS. 30.-- *The Son of man.* He who, appearing in the meantime, sees all things, then appearing will judge all things, when he shall have beheld all men, forgetful of his judgments, made bondsmen to this world, so that they may deservedly be blotted out together with the very world they inhabit.



## CHAPTER XVII. (cont.)

VERS. 31.-- *In that day.* [var: Vulg. Lc 17:31 *In illa hora*, "in that hour"] Thus far concerning this, that the kingdom, that is, Christ, comes not with observation but unlooked for: now, by what sort of men that coming ought to be awaited. He is on the housetop who, going beyond carnal things, lives spiritually as it were in the open air. His vessels [0321C] in the house are the carnal concupiscences which he had loved before, but, seeking things above, had left behind in the world, and which, with the judgment imminent, he must needs not seek again.

*And he that shall be in the field.* What he had said, that he who is in the field ought not to turn back, lest it should seem to have been said only of those who are openly to return from the field, that is, who are to deny the Lord openly; he shows that there are many who, while they seem to keep their face toward the things before, yet look backward in mind.

VERS. 32.-- *Be mindful.* Loth's wife signifies those who look backward in tribulation, and turn themselves away from the hope of the divine promise, and therefore she was made a statue of salt, because by admonishing men not to do the like, she seasons their hearts that they be not foolish.

VERS. 33.-- *Whosoever.* He who shall wish to save the carnal life (by denying [0321D] Christ) in the world, shall lose it in the world to come; or he who seeks the eternal salvation of his soul, let him not hesitate to give it up unto the death of this present time.

VERS. 34.-- *In that night.* That is, in that tribulation so dark *that they may be led into error, if it be possible, even the elect [n: (Matth. XXIV)]*. *Two in a bed.* Those, namely, who choose leisure, occupied neither with secular nor with ecclesiastical business.

VERS. 35.-- *The other shall be left.* He who, whether by human praise or by any corruption whatever, forsakes the state of life in which he was steeped. Of whom Jeremias says: *The enemies saw her, and mocked at her sabbaths [n: (Thren. I)]*. *There shall be two,* etc. Two differences, of those who follow the round of moveable things. Which are designated by the feminine gender, [0322A] because they are ruled by the counsel of their elders, as women by men; which nevertheless grind together in so far as out of their own business they serve the uses of the Church. But some only for the sake of things eternal, *who have as though not having,* [var: Vulg. 1 Cor 7:29 *et qui habent uxores, tamquam non habentes sint*, "and they that have wives, as though they had none"] some for the sake of things temporal, *who hope in the uncertainty of riches [n: (I Tim VI)]*.

VERS. 37.-- *Who said to them.* To those asking two things he answers one, namely that the saints are to be taken up with him; the other he intimates by subjoining, namely that the evil, driven back from him, are to be damned with the devil. *Wheresoever,* etc. Wheresoever the Lord shall be bodily, thither shall the elect be gathered, who by humbly imitating his passion are filled as it were from his flesh, whose youth shall be renewed as the eagle's through the resurrection.



## CHAPTER XVIII.

[0322B]VERS. 2.-- *A certain judge.* This parable exhorts to praying and to believing, and to presuming not upon ourselves but upon God.

VERS. 3.-- *And a widow.* This widow can bear a likeness of the Church, which seems desolate until the Lord come, who nevertheless even now bears care for her in secret. *Avenge me.* Although the just are admonished to pray for their persecutors, yet, righteousness being safe, they seek this vengeance upon the evil: that all the evil may perish, either by being converted to righteousness, or by the loss through punishment of that power by which they now, against the good, so long as this very thing is expedient for the good, are able to do somewhat even temporally. And when the just desire this end to come, although they pray for their enemies, yet they are not absurdly said to desire vengeance.

[0322C]VERS. 8.-- *But yet.* I say that I will execute the vengeance of the just, but they will be exceedingly few, because when on the day of judgment I shall appear in the form of the Son of man, so great will be the rarity of the just that the ruin of the whole world must then be hastened, not so much for the cry of the faithful unjustly condemned, as for the torpor of those justly to be condemned. *Thinkest thou.* In the word the unbelief of doubting is rebuked; the Divinity does not form an opinion. For he through whom all things are knows all things, but by doubting he rebukes the hearts of the unbelieving.

VERS. 10.-- *Two men.* Because the parable by which he invited to prayer he so concluded as to say that, when the Judge comes, faith would hardly be found on earth. Lest anyone should flatter himself with a superfluous knowledge of faith, or even with a confession of it, in this parable he shows more diligently [0322D] that not the words of faith but the works are to be examined by God, among which humility chiefly reigns; and because the proud do not have it, presuming upon their own righteousness and despising the weak, they, as though empty of faith, are not heard when they pray.

VERS. 11.-- *God, I give thee thanks,* etc. There are four kinds of swelling: when either a man reckons that the good which he has he has of himself; or, if he believes it given by God, he thinks it given for his merits; or when he boasts that he has what he has not; or when, despising the rest, he seeks to be seen singularly to have what he has. With this plague the Pharisee laboured, who therefore went down not justified, because ascribing to himself singularly the merits of good works, he set himself before the publican.

[0323A]VERS. 12.-- *I fast twice.* Behold, the Pharisee has an eye to the showing of abstinence, to the obtaining of mercy, to the returning of thanks to God; to the keeping of humility he has none. And what does it profit if the whole city be guarded, and one opening be left through which the enemies may enter?

VERS. 13.-- *And the publican.* As a humble man, he does not dare to draw near, that God may draw near to him: he does not look up, that he may be looked upon; he presses down his conscience, but lifts up his hope; he strikes his breast, he exacts penalties of himself that God may spare; he confesses that God may pardon; God pardons what he acknowledges. *God, be merciful.* The publican praying humbly belongs to those of whom it is said: Shall not God revenge his elect crying to him? The Pharisee boasting of his merits belongs to those of whom it is added: [0323B] *The Son of man coming, thinkest thou, shall he find faith on earth?*

VERS. 14.-- *For every one,* etc. The controversy of the Pharisee and the publican being set down, the sentence of the judge is set down, that pride may be shunned.

VERS. 15.-- *And they brought.* After the controversy spoken, after the sentence given by the humble master, infants are offered, that it may be shown that an innocent and simple age belongs to grace. The apostles rebuke, not because they were unwilling that infants should be blessed by the hand and voice of the Saviour, but because, not yet perfect in faith, they thought that he was wearied, after the likeness of men, by the importunity of those who offered them.

*They rebuked.* This perhaps for the reason that they wished the people of the Jews, from whom they were according to the flesh, to be saved first; yet they also made supplication for the Chanaanite woman. [0323C] They knew therefore the mystery, that the calling of both peoples was due, but perhaps they did not yet know the order.

VERS. 16.-- *Suffer the children.* Let no one forbid the sick to come to the physician. These have as yet committed nothing in the branch, but they have perished in the root. They who cannot plead for themselves have committed their cause to their elders. Let the physician touch, and *let him bless the little ones with the greater [n: (Psal. CXIII)]*. *For of such is.* He does not say, of these, but of those who have from endeavour the innocence and simplicity which these have by nature, because it is not age that reigns, but manners. For it is not a virtue not to be able to sin, but not to will it. As a child does not persevere in anger, when hurt does not remember it, does not covet a woman, does not think or speak evil, so you, unless you have such innocence and purity of mind, [0323D] shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven.

VERS. 17.-- *The kingdom of God.* That is, we are bidden to receive the teaching of the Gospel as a child does. A child in learning does not contradict his teachers. He does not compose reasonings and words to resist, but faithfully receives, obeys and rests.

VERS. 18.-- *And he asked him. [n: (BEDA.)]* This ruler had heard that those like children were worthy, etc., *as far as* to those who wish to be perfect he adds: *Sell all and give to the poor [n: (Marc. X)]*.

*Good master.* Because he said a good master, and did not confess him God or the Son of God he [Christ] says that even a holy man, in comparison with God, is not good. But Christ does not refuse the testimony of goodness, [0324A] but excludes the goodness of a master apart from God.

VERS. 19.-- *And he said. [n: (AMBR.)]* A crafty question, and therefore a keen answer. For the tempter, etc., *as far as* since by these words it is defined that a merciful rich man is against nature.

*Thou knowest the commandments.* This is the chastity of childish innocence, which is set before us for imitation, if we wish to enter the kingdom of God.

VERS. 23.-- *He was rich.* No rich man, that is, none trusting in his riches, shall enter in, as Jesus expounds according to Matthew. Not therefore those who remain rich, but those who have ceased to be so, shall enter in.

VERS. 25.-- *For it is easier.* Mystically. It is easier for Christ to suffer for the lovers of the world, than for the lovers of the world to be converted to Christ. Christ is called a camel, [0324B] because, humbled of his own will, he took up the burdens of our infirmity. The needle is a pricking, by which he signifies the straits of the passion, by which passion, as by a needle, he deigned to sew up again the as it were rent garments of our nature, that is, to repair them after the fall. Otherwise: it is easier for the Gentile people, deformed by the hump of sins, to enter by the narrow way of life, than the Jew who glories in his merits and is unwilling to humble himself to take up the light burden of Christ.

VERS. 26.-- *Who can?* They understand that all who love riches, even if they have them not, are to be reckoned in the number of the rich: for otherwise, since the crowd of the poor is greater, they would not say: *Who can be saved?*

VERS. 29.-- *No man.* He who shall have let go carnal things for the Lord shall receive spiritual things. Mark says: *A hundred times as much [0324C] now,* etc. [n: (Marc. X)], that is, in this life full of persecutions they taste the joy of the kingdom, and enjoy the sincere love of all the elect.

VERS. 30.-- *He shall receive,* etc. He who shall have let go all riches for the Lord, and shall have despised the affections of the flesh, will receive much more in the present, and from brethren and sisters bound together by the spiritual glue of his own purpose he will receive in this life a much more gracious charity.

VERS. 31.-- *He took.* Foreseeing that the disciples would be troubled by his passion, he foretells both the penalty of the passion and the glory of the resurrection, that when they beheld him dying they might not doubt that he would rise again. *And they shall be accomplished.* In this he shows that whatever the prophets foretold had regard to the mystery of his dispensation. In which he strikes the heretics, who say that [0324D] Christ preached a thing contrary to the law and the prophets, and that the God of the Old *Testatamenti* is one and of the New another. He confutes also the madness of the pagans, because they deride his cross, since he both showed the time of his approaching passion as one foreknowing things to come, and approached the place as one not fearing death.

VERS. 34.-- *And they,* etc. The disciples are not able to understand the discourse of the Lord's passion, so often repeated, for this cause, that of him whom they most desire to see they cannot bear to hear the death, and him whom they know to be not only an innocent man but also true God, they do not think able to die.

VERS. 35.-- *A blind man.* The blind man signifies the human race, which, shut out from the brightness above, suffers the darkness of its own [0325A] damnation, but is healed by the Lord drawing near to Jericho. Jericho is interpreted *moon*, by which the failing of mortality is understood. When therefore the Word of God takes up the infirmity of our flesh, man returns to the things that are to be known. The blind man sits by the way when he begins to believe in him who says: *I am the way [n: (Joan. XIV)]*. He begs, when he asks.

VERS. 39.-- *And they that went before,* etc. They who go before Jesus as he comes signify the blind man of carnal vices, which scatter the thought of man and disturb the voice of prayer, that Jesus may not be able to come to enlighten the heart of man; but he, now feeling himself weighed down by the phantasm of his former vices, and the voice of his prayer hindered, lest [the Lord] be entreated on his behalf, cries out the more ardently.

VERS. 40.-- *And standing,* etc. Passing by, he hears the blind man; standing, he enlightens him, because through his humanity he suffers with the voices of our [0325B] blindness, but through the power of his Divinity he pours into us the light of grace. *To be brought unto him.* He who somewhat perceived him passing by, while in prayer he suffers crowds of phantasms, pressing the more vehemently upon prayer is led to him as he stands, and receives light, because God is fixed in a firm heart, and so the lost light is repaired.

VERS. 41.-- *What wilt thou,* etc. He does not ask out of ignorance, but for this cause, that he may be asked, that he may rouse the mind to prayer. Understanding which, the blind man subjoins: *Lord, that I may see.* The blind man does not ask for gold or anything temporal, but only for light. Let us therefore imitate him whom we have heard was healed in mind and in body; let us ask of the Lord the light of the eternal vision, and he will say to us: *Receive thy sight, thy faith hath made thee whole.*

VERS. 43.-- *And immediately he saw.* He sees and follows, [0325C] who works the good which he understands, and imitates Jesus present or passing by. And such a one not only profits toward God for what he has obtained, but also kindles others to the praise of God. *And all the people.* The people praises God not only for the obtained gift of light, but also for the merit of the faith that obtained it, because it praises not only that he enlightens powerfully and mercifully, but because he hears the cry of a faith that cries firmly.



## CHAPTER XIX.

VERS. 1, 2.-- *And entering in,* etc. Behold, the camel, having laid down the burden of its hump, passes through the eye of the needle, while, despising the revenue got by frauds, it climbs up to the narrow gate which leads to life. And because by the devotion of faith he supplies, by the climbing of a tree, what he had too little of by nature for seeing the Saviour, [0325D] justly he receives what he did not dare to ask, the blessing of the Lord's welcome which he had desired. Mystically, Zacchaeus, who is interpreted *justified,* signifies those believing from among the Gentiles, who had been pressed down by the business of temporal things, and were least, but were sanctified by the Lord.

*And he was rich.* Who should despair of himself, when this man came to God whose revenue was from fraud? By him it appears that not every rich man is covetous.

VERS. 3.-- *Because of the crowd.* What crowd, but the confusion of an unskilled multitude, which cannot see the summit of wisdom? Zacchaeus therefore, not in the crowd, but having passed beyond the common people, beholds the Messiah whom he desires.

VERS. 4.-- *A sycomore.* The sycomore is a tree, [0326A] like the mulberry in its leaves, but surpassing it in height, and it is interpreted *the foolish fig,* that is, the cross. Which, as a fig, feeds believers; as foolish, is mocked by unbelievers. The little man climbs it when anyone humble and conscious of his own weakness glories in the virtue of the cross, and through this praiseworthy foolishness discerns the Lord passing near; because if not yet so solidly as he now is, yet hastily, and as it were in passing, he strains toward the light of heavenly wisdom.

*Because he was to pass that way. [n: (ISID.)]* In that part where the sycomore was, or where he was to believe, etc., *as far as* he stood out as in the fruitful height of a work.

VERS. 5.-- *And when he was come.* Through the heralds of his word sent before, in whom Jesus was and was speaking, he came to the people of the nations, which, already lifted on high by faith in his passion, burns to acknowledge his divinity also; [0326B] looking up he sees, for he chose him who through faith had been raised from earthly things and who was choosing him, and loves him who loves him. *Come down.* That he is bidden to come down from the sycomore, and so to make ready a lodging in his house, this is what the Apostle says: *Although we have known Christ according to the flesh, yet now we know him not. For if he died of weakness, yet now he lives by the power of God [n: (II Cor. V, XIII)]* [var: Vulg. 2 Cor 13:4 *crucifixus est ex infirmitate*, "was crucified out of weakness"; and 2 Cor 5:16 *sed nunc jam non novimus*]. *To abide.* The Lord once abode in the house of the chief of the Pharisees, that is, he was teaching in the synagogue of the Jews.

VERS. 7.-- *They murmured.* It is manifest that the Jews always hated, or did not understand, the salvation of the Gentiles. Whence also it is said against Peter: *Why didst thou go in to the Gentiles that have uncircumcision [n: (Act. IX)]* [var: Vulg. Acts 11:3 *ad viros praeputium habentes*, "to men that have uncircumcision"]?

VERS. 8.-- *But standing,* etc. While others were calumniating him as a sinful man, Zacchaeus himself also standing, [0326C] that is, persisting in that truth of faith which he had begun, proves that he was not only converted from a sinner, but had even lived among the innocent, since he lived innocently even before his conversion, and he who before his conversion could give the half, after his conversion can give the whole also. *If I have taken anything,* etc. The first thing is to restore according to the law, then to give out to the poor what remains. This fruit of life the publican gathers from the sycomore through wise folly, in that he restores what he has seized, leaves what is near, despises things visible, and longs to follow the footsteps of him whom he does not yet see.

VERS. 9.-- *He is a son.* Not by flesh, but by faith, because as Abraham at God's command forsook his country for the hope of a future inheritance, so also this man, who acquires by hope a treasure in the heavens.

[0326D]VERS. 10.-- *He came,* etc. Even to those murmuring he does not disdain to expound his mysteries: that he himself came upon earth chiefly for the sake of the repentance of sinners, which is to be looked upon, where also he calls himself the Son of man, that he may intimate to us the dispensation of his mercy.

VERS. 11.-- *These things to them,* etc. The repentance of the rich publican having been received and commended, he teaches by a parable that the repentance of sinners pleases him more than the righteousness of the proud, and that he will reign more widely among the Gentiles, who are humble concerning their ignorance of the law, than among the Jews, who are proud concerning the righteousness of the law.

*Because that.* Not only those who persevere as just, but also those who are converted from injustice, belong to the children of the promise.

[0327A]VERS. 12.-- *A nobleman went away.* This is the nobleman to whom the blind man cried: *Have mercy on me, son of David,* and to whom the trumpets cried: *Hosanna to the son of David: blessed is he that cometh in the name of the Lord, the king of Israel,* [var: Vulg. Matt 21:9 ends *hosanna in altissimis*, and *rex Israel* stands only at Jo 12:13] who was appointed king by the Father, to whom the Gentiles were given for an inheritance. *A far country.* Leaving the blinded Jews, he passes over to enlighten the Gentiles, who cry to the Lord from afar (because from the ends of the earth), or because *salvation is far from sinners [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*: God, who is everywhere present, is far from those who worship idols; but they too have been made near in the blood.

VERS. 13.-- *And having called ten.* He calls ten servants, when he chose disciples steeped in the letter of the Decalogue. He gives ten pounds, when after the resurrection he rises and opens their understanding, that it may spiritually understand [0327B] the sayings of the law The mna weighs ten drachmas. And every word of Scripture, because it suggests the perfection of the heavenly life, shines as with the weight of the hundredfold number. *Trade ye.* Offer to the peoples the words of the law sifted by mystical interpretation, and receive from them the confession of faith and uprightness of manners.

VERS. 15.-- *And it came to pass.* Daily Christ, having received the kingdom from the Father, returns, because he always beholds the state of the Church journeying on earth. To this servant he lends money for trading; in another he examines the measure of the work accomplished. This one, labouring prudently, he endows with ampler grace; that one, following after wanton things, he deprives even of what he had given. But in the judgment many who seemed fit for teaching will, for their own negligence, [0327C] be reckoned among the unlearned. Certain men, a devotion of simpler observance, will receive the highest rewards among the apostolic. *For he that receiveth a prophet in the name of a prophet shall receive the reward of a prophet [n: (Matth. X)]*.

VERS. 16.-- *And the first came.* The first servant is the order of teachers sent into the circumcision, who received one pound to trade with, because he was sent to preach one God, one faith, one baptism. But he gains ten while by teaching he joins to himself the people set under the law.

VERS. 17.-- *Thou hast been faithful in a little.* Little is whatever of gifts we receive in the present, in comparison with those to come. *For we know in part, and we prophesy in part [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. *Ten [0327D] cities.* The ten cities are souls coming through the words of the law to the grace of the Gospel, over whom he is then set to be glorified who shall have worthily commended to God the money of the word for them; whence the Apostle: *What is our hope, or joy, or crown of glory? Are not you before the Lord Jesus [n: (I Thes. II)]*?

VERS. 18.-- *And another,* etc. A good order: being about to call the Gentiles, and about to send the Jews to be slain, who would not have Christ reign over them, he set this comparison first, lest it should be said: He had given nothing to the people of the Jews whereby they might be made better, so that what should be exacted from him who receives nothing? Out of one pound he makes five, who has moral things on account of the five senses.

VERS. 20.-- *And another,* etc. He who was sent [0328A] to the uncircumcision, to whom the Lord entrusted one pound, and the same faith which was committed to the circumcision, to be preached. Which makes five pounds, because it converts to the grace of the evangelical faith the Gentiles that were enslaved to the five senses of the body. *In a napkin,* etc. He lays up the money in a napkin who, being fit for preaching, either refuses to undertake the office of preaching, or, having undertaken it, does not discharge it well, but hides with himself the gifts he has received under the idleness of the time, saying: It is enough for me that I render an account of myself; why should I preach to others, or be compelled to render an account of others? since even with God those are inexcusable to whom the law was not given, nor did they sleep when the Gospel had been heard, because through the creature they were able to know the Creator.

VERS. 21.-- *Thou reapest what.* For God reaps where he did not [0328B] sow, that is, he holds guilty of impiety even those to whom he has not ministered the word of the law or of the Gospel. Fearing therefore the peril of the judgment, I will rest from the administration of the word.

VERS. 22.-- *Thou knewest,* etc. What he had thought to have said for an excuse is turned into a fault. If thou hadst known me so hard and cruel, why did that thought not strike fear into thee, that thou shouldst know I would seek my own the more diligently?

VERS. 23.-- *The money.* That is, the evangelical preaching, and thou didst not make it known to the prepared hearts of the faithful. *Mine to the bank.* Not thine. Whence the Apostle: *If any man speak, let it be as the words of God [n: (I Petr. IV)]*. *With usury.* He who buys the money of the word from a teacher by believing must needs pay it back with usury by working. Or, [0328C] he pays usury on the loan of the word he has received who, from what he hears, studies to understand other things also.

VERS. 24.-- *Take away,* etc. He loses the grace conferred who has neglected to share it with others by preaching. But it is increased to him who has laboured in it. As the royal chrism which Saul lost by being proud, David merited by obeying. For the Spirit of the Lord who departed from Saul was directed from that day forward into David.

VERS. 26.-- *And he shall abound.* Mystically: when all Israel shall be saved, then the fulness of spiritual grace, which now we exercise sluggishly, shall be abundantly conferred upon the teachers of that time.

VERS. 28.-- *And having said these things.* The parable finished, he goes to Jerusalem, to show that the parable had been set forth concerning the fate of that same greatest city, which [0328D] not long after, being itself also about to kill him for hatred of his kingdom, was to perish by an enemy's slaughter.

VERS. 29.-- *To Bethphage.* Bethphage is a hamlet of priests on the Mount of Olives, and is interpreted *the house of the mouth.* Bethany is a farmstead or city on the side of the same mountain, and is called *the house of obedience.* These the Lord, being about to come to Jerusalem, exalted by his presence, because before his passion he filled many, by teaching, with the gifts of devout confession and of spiritual obedience. Which are well placed on the Mount of Olives, that is, in the Lord himself, who cherishes us with the unction of spiritual chrisms and with the light of conscience and of mercy. And because he does not wish a city set above itself to be hidden, he sends the disciples into the village which is over against them, that through [0329A] them he may penetrate the fortifications of the whole world set over against him.

VERS. 30.-- *Entering in you shall find.* The preachers, entering into the world, find the people of the nations, entangled in the bonds of unbelief, free and wanton. *Upon whom no man,* etc., that is, no rational teacher has put the bridle of correction, who might either restrain its tongue from evil, or compel it to go into the narrow way of life. No man, by persuading what was useful, bestowed the garments of salvation whereby it might *be warmed.* *No man sat,* because none corrected its folly by pressing it down. *Tied.* Tied with the bonds of unbelief, in which, made over or bound fast, he was serving a lord by error; but he whom not nature but fault had made lord could not claim the mastery for himself. And therefore when the Lord is spoken of, one is acknowledged. For, [0329B] although there be many gods and lords, yet in general there is one God and one Lord.

VERS. 33.-- *And as they were loosing him,* etc. Despise not this little ass, because as under the clothing of sheep there are ravening wolves, so under the spoils of the body (which is common to us with the beasts) a mind full of God is quickened. *They said.* He had many masters, who, given over not to one superstition, but at the pleasure of unclean spirits, was hurried away to various errors. *Why do you loose him.* Those who had spoken against the loosing of the colt are quiet when the name is heard, because the masters of errors, who withstand the teachers coming for the salvation of the Gentiles, defend their own darkness, until, with miracles bearing witness, the power of the true owner stands forth. But after the power of the Lord's faith appears, the complaints of the adversaries giving way, [0329C] there is brought forward an assembly that may bear God in its heart.



## CHAPTER XIX. (cont.)

VERS. 35.-- *The garments.* The garments of the apostles are the teaching of the virtues, or the discernment of the Scriptures, and grace spiritually, wherewith they cover the hearts of men, before naked and cold, that they may deserve to have Christ for their rider. *They laid them on.* While the people of the Gentiles carries Christ, the disciples strew their garments in the way, because, putting off the covering of their own body, they make ready a way for the simple by their own blood, that with unhindered step of mind they may press on to Jerusalem, whither Christ leads, that is, to the true vision of peace.

VERS. 37.-- *At the descent.* As the Lord came down from the Mount of Olives, that is, as he who was in the form of God humbled himself to the weakness of our flesh, the crowds come down with him, [0329D] that is, he is humbled who need his mercy, that they may be exalted by him. *Rejoicing.* Often he entered the city of Jerusalem, but was not called king, which he had always fled, save only now when he went up to suffer. Which was done for this reason, that he might the more stir up their envy against himself, because the time of the passion was now at hand. *For.* Chiefly for the raising of Lazarus, which had lately been done. For on that account also the crowd came to meet him, because they had heard that this sign had been done by him. They praise him also for the signs and the teaching which he wrought when he went up for the day of the feast of tabernacles, which John relates.

VERS. 38.-- *Blessed.* The crowd, that after a little was to crucify him, calls him king, and declares that the awaited son of David according [0330A] to the flesh has come, so that a testimony against themselves is wrung from them unwilling. *Peace.* As at the Lord's birth the heavenly companies of men and of angels sang: *Glory to God in the highest. And on earth peace to men,* etc. [var: Vulg. Luke 2:14 *Gloria in altissimis Deo*; *in excelsis* is the reading of Luke 19:38, the verse here glossed], so to the same, soon to triumph, men sing together: Peace in heaven, and glory in the highest.

VERS. 39.-- *Master.* Wondrous madness of the envious! Him whom they had known to teach true things, when they hear him called master, they judge that his disciples are to be reproved, as though they themselves were the better taught, those disciples whom he had instructed who by his miracles appears to be God.

VERS. 40.-- *The stones.* When the Lord was crucified and the apostles held their peace for fear, the rocks that were rent, the tombs being opened, openly [0330B] cried out that he was God and the Lord of the world.

Mystically: if blindness has befallen Israel, so that it falls silent from the praise of God, the people of the Gentiles, their stony heart softened, shall believe and announce their Creator.

VERS. 44.-- *And they shall not leave.* For when the perverse man adds perversion, he builds as it were stone upon stone. But when the soul is hurried away to vengeance, such a building of thoughts is scattered, and this for this cause, *because thou hast not known the time.* For God visits the perverse soul, sometimes by a precept, sometimes by a miracle. But because in its pride it despises, and does not blush for its own evils, at the last it shall be delivered to its enemies, with whom in the eternal judgment it is gathered into the fellowship of damnation. *Unto peace,* etc. While thou givest the flesh to pleasures, not foreseeing the evils to come, thou hast present goods [0330C] which can be for a temporal peace. And why this, he subjoins, namely, because it does not foresee the evils that are to come upon it. For if it did foresee them, it would not have rejoiced in present prosperities.

VERS. 45.-- *And going in.* After the evils had been enumerated, going into the temple he cast out those that sold and bought, intimating by this that the ruin of the people was chiefly from the fault of the priests; for while he strikes those that sell and buy, he shows whence the root of perdition proceeded.

VERS. 47.-- *And he was teaching.* The house of God is the conscience of the faithful, which he makes a den of thieves who, having left the simplicity of holiness, plots to do that whereby he may be able to hurt his innocent neighbour. But on the contrary the Lord instructs the minds of the faithful to beware of evils. *They sought to destroy him.* Therefore they were not able [0330D] to destroy him, because they feared the tumult of the people; or therefore they wished to destroy him, because, their own office of teaching being neglected, they saw the whole people flowing together to his doctrine.



## CHAPTER XX.

VERS. 2.-- *By what authority.* They doubt of God's power, and would have it understood that what he does is the devil's. Whence they add: *Or who is he,* etc. In which they plainly deny the Son of God, whom they believe to work signs not by his own powers, but by another's.

VERS. 3.-- *I will ask you,* etc. He could have refuted the calumny of the tempters by an open answer, but he prudently asks a question, that they may be condemned either by their own silence or by their own sentence.

[0331A]VERS. 4.-- *The baptism of John,* etc. He whom you confess to have had his prophecy from heaven bore witness to me, and from him you have heard by what authority I do these things. Understanding which, they doubt what they ought to answer.

*Neither do I,* etc. For two causes the truth is to be hidden from those who ask it. Either because they are less able to hear it; whence it is said: *I have yet many things to say to you, but you cannot bear them now [n: (Joan. XVI)]*. Or because they ask out of hatred and contempt; whence it is said: *Give not that which is holy to dogs [n: (Matth. VII)]*.

VERS. 9.-- *And he began.* The chief priests and scribes having been refuted out of their own question, the Lord pursues what he had begun, and, in their hearing, addresses the common people, which hears his words more gladly, bringing in [0331B] a parable whereby he may both convict them of impiety and show that the kingdom of God is to be transferred to the Gentiles.

*A vineyard.* Morally: *The vineyard of the Lord of sabaoth, the house of Israel [n: (Isa. V)]*: [var: Vulg. Isa 5:7 *Vinea enim Domini exercituum domus Israel est*, "of the Lord of hosts"] the husbandmen are the labourers who were hired to till it at the first hour, the third, the sixth and the ninth. *And he into a far country,* etc. He who is everywhere is the more present to those who love him, and is absent to those who neglect him; and he was absent a long time, lest the exaction should seem wrongful. For the more indulgent the liberality, the more inexcusable the obstinacy.

VERS. 10.-- *And at the season.* The first servant who is sent is Moses the lawgiver. Who for forty years sought from the tillers the fruit of the law which he had given, but they beat him and send him away empty.

VERS. 11.-- *And he added,* etc. The second servant is David [0331C] the prophet, who after Moses invited the husbandmen of the vineyard by the measure of psalmody and the sweetness of the harp to the exercise of good work; but him also they treat with contumely and send away empty, saying: *What part have we in David, or what inheritance in the son of Isai [n: (Reg. XII)]*? Yet he entreats for this vineyard, that it be not utterly rooted up: *O Lord God of hosts, turn and look down from heaven, and see, and visit this vineyard [n: (Psal. LXXVII)]*. The third servant is the choir of the prophets. Who rebuked the people, and foretold the evils that hung over the vineyard; but them also they persecuted and killed.

VERS. 14.-- *Whom when they had seen.* He plainly proves that the Jews crucified the Son of God not through ignorance, but through envy. For they understood that this was he to whom it was said: *Ask of me, and I will give thee the Gentiles for thy inheritance, [0331D] and the utmost parts of the earth for thy possession [n: (Psal. CXVII)]*. And therefore, taking counsel for themselves, they say: *Behold, the whole world is gone after him [n: (Joan. XII)]*, etc. The inheritance of the Church, therefore, which the Father did not leave by dying, but which he himself, bought by his own death, possessed by rising again, this the Jews wish to snatch away, while they endeavour to extinguish the faith which is through him, not being subject to the justice of God, but wishing to establish their own. *This is the heir:* Christ is the heir, because he outlived his own death, and of the testaments which he himself made he obtains, as it were by inheritance, the profits in our advances.

VERS. 15.-- *And having cast him out.* Mark changes the order: *They killed him and cast him out of the vineyard (Marc. [0332A] XII)*. In which their obstinacy is noted, who after the resurrection had refused to believe the apostles preaching, but, casting him out as it were a useful corpse, gave him to the Gentiles to be taken up.

VERS. 16.-- *God forbid.* They contradict the sentence which they know was spoken against their own unbelief. For what they were carrying in their mind they understand to be contained in the parable. Since therefore the Jews deny that it would be just that the divine knowledge of the law (which they themselves despised) be transferred to the Gentiles, mark what the Saviour answers.

VERS. 17.-- *What is this,* etc. How shall this prophecy be fulfilled? Except that Christ, rejected by you, is to be preached to the Gentiles who will believe, so that in himself, the one corner stone, he may found two walls. *The builders.* Those who above are husbandmen are here called builders, [0332B] because they who were bidden to till the people that it might bear fruit were bidden to set it up as a house worthy of the Lord himself for its indweller.

VERS. 18.-- *Every one that shall fall.* He who believes and yet sins falls upon the stone, and is not ground to powder, but is broken, because by patience he is reserved unto salvation. But he upon whom the stone falls, that is, he who utterly denies, is ground to powder, so that not even a shard remains. Or he falls upon it who now despises and does injuries: he does not yet utterly perish, but is shaken, so that he does not walk upright. The stone falls upon him whom, coming in judgment, it crushes.

VERS. 19.-- *And they sought,* etc. They seek to kill him as a liar, in which the things he had said are proved true. For he himself is the heir, and they the wicked husbandmen, who [0332C] are held back by human fear alone until his hour should come.

VERS. 20.-- *And watching him.* What for fear of the people they cannot do by themselves, they wish to accomplish by empty helps, so that they may seem free from his death.

VERS. 22.-- *Is it lawful for us,* etc. When lately under Augustus Judaea had been made tributary to the Romans, the Pharisees, who applauded themselves for their righteousness, who gave tithes and first-fruits to God, denied that tributes were to be paid to a man; for which sedition their kingdom and their whole country were afterwards destroyed.

VERS. 24.-- *Whose image hath it,* etc. One is the image of God, another that of the world. If then Christ had not the image of Caesar, why did he pay the tribute? He did not pay of his own, but rendered to the world the things that were the world's. And thou, if thou dost not wish [0332D] to be liable to Caesar, do not have the things that are the world's. If thou hast riches, thou art liable to Caesar. If thou wishest to owe nothing to an earthly king, leave all that is thine and follow Christ. And well does he determine that first the things that are Caesar's are to be rendered. For no one can be the Lord's, unless he has first renounced the world.

VERS. 25.-- *Render therefore.* As Caesar exacts the impress of his own image, so also does God the soul sealed with the light of his countenance.

VERS. 27.-- *And there came to him,* etc. There were two heresies among the Jews: that of the Pharisees, who set foremost the righteousness of traditions and observances, whence also they were called the separated from the people; and that of the Sadducees, who likewise claimed for themselves what they were not, that is, just men.

[0333A]VERS. 28.-- *Master.* They who do not believe the resurrection of bodies, and think that souls perish with bodies, invent the baseness of a tale, which convicts of raving those who assert the resurrection. But it may be that this once happened in their own nation. *That he may take,* etc. In the law of the letter one was compelled to marry in this life. But in the law of the spirit chastity is commended.

VERS. 35.-- *But they,* etc. Although all, both good and bad, are to rise again and to remain without marriage, yet the Lord, that he may stir men up to the glory of the resurrection, discourses of the elect alone. But if in the resurrection they shall neither take wives, then those bodies shall rise which are able to marry and to take in marriage. Which is said of those who have generative members, but shall be enslaved by no pleasure or necessity [0333B] of marrying. And note that although he speaks secret mysteries with liers-in-wait and tramplers, yet he does not give what is holy to dogs; but there were present many fit persons who ought not to be neglected because of the uncleanness of others. *Neither shall they marry,* etc. Since marriages are for the sake of children, children for the sake of succession, succession for the sake of death, where therefore there is no death, neither are there marriages.

VERS. 37.-- *But that the dead shall rise again.* After he had satisfied the question proposed concerning the conditions of the resurrection, he treats of the resurrection itself against their unbelief.

VERS. 40.-- *And after that,* etc. Refuted in argument they ask no more, but openly seize him and deliver him to the Roman power; from which we understand [0333C] that the poisons of envy can indeed be overcome, but hardly be laid to rest.

VERS. 41.-- *How do they say,* etc. The Lord, being about to complete his precepts, at the end of his testament concludes the faith, that we should believe the Lord Jesus, true God and true man, to sit at the right hand of the Father.

VERS. 43.-- *Till I make.* That his enemies are made subject by the Father signifies not the weakness of the Son, but the unity of nature, whereby in the one he works otherwise. For the Son also makes the enemies subject to the Father, because he glorifies the Father upon earth.

VERS. 46.-- *Beware of the scribes.* For a twofold reason we are bidden to beware of those greedy of vainglory: lest either we be deceived by their pretence, or be inflamed by their example to the love of temporal things. *And they love salutations [0333D] in the marketplace.* He does not forbid being saluted in the marketplace, nor the first seats, to those to whom they are due by the order of their office, but to those who love them unduly. For he reproves the mind, not the rank. Nor indeed is he without fault, if he wishes to take part in the lawsuits of the marketplace who desires to sit in the chair of Moses.

*Who devour,* etc. Mark: *Under the pretence of long prayer,* they who, that they may appear the more religious, pray at greater length, where from any weak persons whatsoever, and from those troubled by the conscience of their own sins, they may receive as patrons both praises and moneys; whose prayer is turned into sin, so that not only can they not intercede for others, but neither profit themselves unto salvation; nay rather, for those very prayers they shall be the more damned. *These shall receive damnation.* In this he intimates that those also who stand praying in corners, [0334A] that they may be seen by men, do indeed deserve damnation, but these a greater.



## CHAPTER XXI.

VERS. 1.-- *And looking on,* etc. He who had warned that the seekers of primacy and of vainglory must be guarded against, discerns also with a sure weighing those who bring gifts into the house of the Lord, that he may repay each one according to the intention of the heart.

VERS. 4.-- *Out of their abundance.* The Jew casts in out of abundance, who, presuming upon his own righteousness, prays thus within himself [n: (Luc. XVIII)]: *God, I give thee thanks that I am not as the rest of men.* The Church casts all her living into the gifts of God, she who understands all that she intimates to be not of her own merit but of the divine gift, saying: *God, [0334B] be merciful to me a sinner.*

VERS. 5.-- *And some.* After the widow's offering, in which the Church of the Gentiles is signified, the destruction of the temple is subjoined, so that, with the veil of the concealing letter, the hidden sacraments of the Church may begin to be disclosed. For God took care to overthrow the city and the temple and all figurative things, lest anyone after the coming of Christ should run back to them.

VERS. 7.-- *They asked him.* Because to those praising the buildings of the temple he had foretold that all was to be destroyed, the disciples (as Mark says), Peter, James, and John and Andrew, ask apart the time and the signs of the foretold destruction.

VERS. 8.-- *For many.* When the destruction of Jerusalem was at hand, there were many leaders who said that they were Christs [0334C] and that the time of liberty was drawing near. In the times of the apostles also many heresiarchs came forth, who among their other falsehoods preached that the day of the Lord was at hand, whom the Apostle also refuted.

VERS. 9.-- *Wars and seditions.* These abounded in Judaea from the passion of the Lord; and lest the apostles, terrified by them, should forsake Jerusalem and Judaea, they are warned *that the end is not immediately,* but after the forty-second year.

VERS. 10.-- *Then he said.* Of these, some are from heaven, some from the earth, some from the elements, some from men. Mystically. Kingdom against kingdom, and the pestilence of those whose speech creeps like a canker, and the famine of hearing the word of God, the shaking of the earth, the separation from the true faith, ought to be understood of heretics, who, fighting against [0334D] one another, make the victory of the Church. *And great signs.* These also are found fulfilled at that time in Josephus, who relates that a star like a sword hung over Jerusalem for a year, and that chariots and armed horsemen fought in the air for forty days, and that a heifer brought forth a lamb among the hands of those sacrificing.

VERS. 13.-- *And it shall happen,* etc. Of those who either persecute, or are not changed by their living. For the death of the just is to the good a help, to the wicked a testimony of damnation, so that the perverse may perish without excuse from that whence the elect take an example that they may live.

VERS. 16.-- *By parents,* etc. It is a greater torment when a man suffers from those upon whom he counted, [0335A] because along with the loss of the body the evils of lost charity torment him. And because the things foretold of the affliction of death are hard, a consolation concerning the joy of the resurrection is subjoined.

VERS. 18.-- *And a hair.* As if he said: Why do you fear that that should perish which hurts when cut, seeing that not even there can that perish in you which does not hurt when cut? For the flesh when cut hurts, the hair does not hurt.

VERS. 19.-- *In your patience.* Patience is the root and the guardian of all the virtues. In it therefore we possess our souls, which possess the body, when we govern those souls by reason unto suffering. But true patience is to endure with equanimity the evils of another, and to be moved by no grief against him who inflicts the evils.

VERS. 21.-- *And they that are in the countries.* The Ecclesiastical [0335B] History relates that the Christians who were in Judaea, when the destruction was at hand, were warned by an angel, and dwelt beyond Jordan in the city of Pella, until the desolation of Judaea should be fulfilled.

VERS. 23.-- *For there shall be distress,* etc. This distress and wrath endures to this day for that people scattered through all the nations. Yet it will not endure for ever.

VERS. 24.-- *Until they be fulfilled,* etc. That is, *Until the fulness of the Gentiles shall have come in, and so all Israel be saved [n: (Rom. XI)]*, and as it were return rejoicing to the throne of its own birth. For what follows when the times of the nations are fulfilled and all Israel is saved, the Lord sets down in order. For these things also the apostles asked according to Matthew: not only the time of the overthrowing of the temple, but also the sign of his coming and [0335C] of the consummation of the world.
