VERS. 25, 26.-- *And there shall be signs.* These things are of his coming itself, when all the virgins, wise and foolish, roused by an unwonted cry, trim their lamps, that is, count over their works with themselves, for which with the greatest fear they await the now impending outcome of the eternal decision. As the last judgment draws near, the sound of the sea and of the waves is confounded, and the round world is stained with husbandmen pressing on every side; the greatest lights of heaven, their rays struck with a new horror, veil their troubled face, because, as their own end draws near, the elements as if in dread waver and roar.

VERS. 27.-- *And then they shall see.* Him whom, set in humility, they would not hear, so that they may then feel his power the more strictly, the more that now they do not incline their hearts to his [0335D] patience. But because these things were said against the reprobate, he presently subjoins matter for the consolation of the elect.

VERS. 28.-- *But when these things begin to come to pass.* That is, when the plagues grow thick, when the terror of the judgment is shown by the shaking of the powers. *Lift up your heads.* Gladden your hearts, because, while the world to which you are not friends is being ended, the redemption which you seek is at hand. But that the world ought to be despised is made plain by a provident comparison.

VERS. 29.-- *See the fig tree.* He compares the kingdom of God to summer, because then the clouds of our sorrow shall pass away, and the days of eternal life shall shine under the brightness of the sun. For as from the fruit of the trees the nearness of summer [0336A] is known, so also from the ruin of the world the kingdom of God is known to be near. From these words it is shown that the fruit of the world is ruin. For it grows to this end, that it may fall; it buds to this end, that whatsoever it shall have budded it may consume with disasters.

VERS. 33.-- *Heaven and earth.* Nothing in the nature of bodies is more lasting than heaven and earth. Nothing passes more swiftly than speech. Words, so long as they are unfinished, are not words; if they be finished, they now are not at all: for they cannot be finished save by passing away. He says therefore: *Heaven and earth shall pass away.* As if he said: Everything that with you is lasting and without change is not lasting unto eternity, and everything that with me is seen to pass is held fixed and without passing, because my speech, which passes, expresses abiding [0336B] judgments. *But my words shall not pass away.* And since my words are thus without doubt to be fulfilled, see that, against the attestation of my words, you be not given over to surfeiting and drunkenness and the cares of this world, as certain men will be in the time of the impending judgment: of whom it is said: *When they shall say: Peace and security, sudden destruction shall come upon them [n: (I Thess. V)]*.

VERS. 37.-- *And he was,* etc. What he taught by word he confirms by example. He who, before the universal judgment and the uncertain departure of each man, warned men to renounce the pleasures and cares of the world, and to watch and to pray, himself, when his own passion was at hand, presses on with teaching, with watchings and with prayers, and those for whom he was to suffer he calls forth by his word and commends to the Father by his prayer. So we also, when among prosperous things we live justly and devoutly, let us not among [0336C] adverse things despair of the divine mercy. By day we teach in the temple, because we offer to the faithful the form of good work; by night we abide on the Mount of Olives, because in the darkness of straits we breathe again with the consolation of spiritual joy.



## CHAPTER XXII.

VERS. 1.-- *It drew near,* etc. *Which is called the Pasch,* etc. The Pasch is named not from the passion, but from the passing over, because on that day the destroyer passed through Egypt, or the Lord, delivering his people; by which name it was signified that the Lamb was on this day to pass out of the world to the Father. But between the Pasch and the unleavened bread this is the difference, that the Pasch is called that day alone on which the lamb was slain at evening, that is, the fourteenth moon [0336D] of the first month. The days of the unleavened bread were from the fifteenth moon to the twenty-first day of the same month. But the evangelists set them down indifferently, both the day of the Unleavened Bread for the Pasch, and the Pasch for the days of the Unleavened Bread; whence Luke here: *The feast day of the Unleavened Bread, which is called the Pasch [n: (Luc. XII)]*, because the days of the Pasch also were commanded to be celebrated with unleavened loaves. And we too, as it were keeping a perpetual Pasch, are bidden always to pass out of this world: the lamb having been immolated at evening on one day, there follow the seven days of the Unleavened Bread, because Christ, having suffered once for us in the fulness of times, commanded that throughout the whole time of the world (which is run in seven days) men should live in the unleavened bread of sincerity and truth.

[0337A]VERS. 2.-- *And they sought,* etc. Matthew witnesses that these things were done two days before the Pasch, when the chief priests and scribes were gathered into the hall of Caiphas.

VERS. 3.-- *He entered.* John says that after the morsel had been given by the Lord, Satan entered into Judas. But Satan had assailed him before the morsel also; and him whom he had first entered that he might deceive him, he afterwards entered that he might more fully possess one now delivered over to him.

VERS. 7.-- *And the day came.* Although he was crucified on the following day, that is, on the fifteenth moon, yet on this night on which the Lamb was immolated he both delivered to his disciples the sacraments of flesh and blood to be celebrated, and, held and bound by the Jews, hallowed the beginning of that same immolation, that is, of his passion.

[0337B]VERS. 10.-- *Behold, as you go in.* As God, foreknowing all things, he speaks with his disciples of the things that are done elsewhere. And well does a man carrying a pitcher of water meet those who are to prepare the Pasch, that it may be shown that the mystery of this Pasch is to be celebrated for the washing of the world through the water of baptism, where for the worshippers of the true Pasch the typical heat is taken away from the threshold, and the baptism of the life-giving font is consecrated for the taking away of sins. The pitcher signifies the perfect measure of the fulfilled time in which it was fitting that these things be done. *A man a pitcher.* He is designated without a name, that he who is chosen by the Lord may be reckoned poor and ignoble. Whose holiness appears in this, that in his chamber the Lord with his disciples rests, with the delight of sublime virtues. *Follow him.* The names are of set purpose passed over, [0337C] both of the water-bearer and of the master of the house, that it may be signified that leave is to be given to all who wish to be steeped in the sacraments of Christ (which is to celebrate the Pasch), and who seek to receive Christ into the lodging of their own mind.

VERS. 12.-- *A large dining room.* Spiritual is the law, which, going forth out of the straits of the earth, receives the Saviour in a lofty place. He who remains in the letter keeps the Pasch in the innermost places; but he who follows the water-bearer, that is, the herald of grace, into the house of the Church, passing beyond the surface of the letter, prepares a refreshment for Christ on the throne of the mind, while he understands that all the sacraments of the Pasch, and the other decrees of the law, were written of him.

VERS. 15.-- *With desire I have desired,* etc. He desires first to eat the typical Pasch, and so to declare to the world the mysteries of his [0337D] passion, that he may both stand forth as the approver of the ancient Pasch, and, showing that this had belonged to the figure of his own dispensation, the shadow should now cease at the coming of the truth. In figure of which thing the manna failed after the children of Israel had eaten of the fruits of the land, nor did they use that food any more.

VERS. 16.-- *I will not eat.* I will no longer celebrate the Mosaic Pasch, until it be fulfilled, spiritually understood, in the Church, which is the kingdom of God. In this kingdom Christ eats to this day, when he exercises spiritually in his own members those things which Moses commanded the untaught people to observe carnally.

VERS. 17.-- *And having taken the chalice.* This chalice belongs to the old Pasch, to which he was setting an end. Having taken which, [0338A] he gives thanks, namely for this reason, because the old things had passed away and all new things were to come. *He gave thanks.* He shows what each man ought to do under the scourge of his own fault, since he himself bore with equanimity the scourges of another's.

VERS. 18.-- *For I say to you.* As the typical eating of the lamb, so also he denies that he will drink the typical drink of the Pasch, until, the glory of the resurrection being shown, the kingdom of God, that is, the faith of the world, shall come; that by the changing of these two sacraments he may teach that the other sacraments or commands of the law are to be transferred to a spiritual observance. Yet it can be taken simply, that from the hour of the supper until the time of the resurrection, at which he was to come in the kingdom of God, he was not to drink wine. For after the resurrection he ate and drank with his disciples.

[0338B]VERS. 19.-- *And having taken.* The solemnities of the old Pasch being finished, he passes over to the new, which he desires to be frequented in the Church in memory of his own redemption, that in place of the flesh and blood of the lamb he may substitute the sacrament of his own flesh and blood under the figure of bread and wine. He breaks it and reaches it out, to show that the breaking of his own body would not be without his own will. He gives thanks for the old things ended and for the new to be begun. *Bread,* etc. Because bread strengthens the body, wine produces blood.

VERS. 21.-- *But yet,* etc. As he foretold of the passion, so he foretold also of the betrayer, that, seeing he could not lie hid, he might repent of the deed. Nor does he mark him out by name, lest, being openly corrected, he should become the more shameless. He casts the fault therefore into the number, that he who is conscious of it may do penance [0338C] he foretold the penalty also, that whom shame had not convicted, the punishments denounced might restrain. *The hand of him that betrayeth me.* After the example of Judas he betrays the Son of man who presumes to violate that inviolable sacrament of the Lord's body. He sells him who, having neglected the fear and the love of him, is convicted of loving things fallen and criminal

VERS. 24.-- *And there was,* etc. [n: (BEDA.)] The cause of the strife is unknown to us, but it is not incredible, etc., *as far as* let us see what the spiritual master has commanded.

[n: (AMBR.)] Let us therefore beware exceedingly, etc., *as far as* that there be no boasting about preferment, but a contention about humility.

VERS. 25.-- *And he said to them.* The kindly master does not [0338D] accuse them of the strife begun, but describes the form of humility which they should follow; as if he said: You, after the manner of worldly men, wish to arrive at the possession of the kingdom through preferment; but the summit of the virtues is reached not by power, but by humility. But it is needful that the subject show correction, and mercy a mother, and discipline a father, or lest strictness be rigid, or mercy be remiss.

VERS. 27.-- *For which is greater,* etc. To the words of the exhortation to be fulfilled he sets forth an example from himself. Hence John writes [n: (Joan. XIII)]: *If I have washed your feet, I your Lord and master,* etc. And in the word of ministering there can also be all the things which the Lord did in the flesh, not only by preaching or by bringing miracles to bear, but [0339A] also by laying down the soul which he took for us, that we too may learn to lay down our lives for one another.

VERS. 28.-- *You have continued with me.* My weakness did not trouble you, but you persevered in the good you had begun. As therefore to me persevering unto death the Father gave *a name which is above every name [n: (Phil. III)]*, so I also will lead you persevering with me to the eternal kingdom. It is to be understood that Judas had already withdrawn, and that many others had already gone back, when the words of the incomprehensible sacrament had been heard, at the time when the Lord was speaking these things.

VERS. 30.-- *And may sit upon thrones.* This is the change of the right hand of the Most High: that they who, being humble, now rejoice to minister to their fellow servants, may then, being exalted, be fed at the table of the Lord with perpetual banquets; and that they who in temptations, [0339B] being unjustly judged, remain with the Lord, may there come with him as just judges over their own tempters. For by how much they are the more despised here, by so much shall they rise there to a greater height of power. *Judging the twelve.* By the twelve, not twelve persons, but the multitude of those judging is signified, on account of the two parts of the sevenfold number, by which universality is signified, that is, three and four, by which twelve is multiplied.

VERS. 31.-- *And the Lord said.* Lest the twelve should glory that they alone out of so many thousands were said to have continued with the Lord in temptations, he warns them that they could have been crushed by the same storm, had they not been protected by divine grace. But when Satan desires to tempt, and to shake them as one who cleanses wheat by winnowing, [0339C] we are taught that no one is tempted by the devil except by God's permitting it. *Behold, Satan,* etc. Satan asks for the good that he may sift them, when he pants with the heats of malice for their affliction. For wherein, envying, he seeks their temptation, therein, entreating, he asks as it were their proving.

VERS. 32.-- *But I have prayed.* Not that thou shouldst be tempted, but that thy faith fail not, that after the fall of denial thou mayest rise again by penance to thy former state. For it is profitable for the saints to be tried by temptations also, that either it may appear, when they are tempted, how strong they were, or that, their own weakness being known through temptation, they may learn to become stronger, and so, when they have been proved, may receive the crown of life.

VERS. 33.-- *Who said to him,* etc. Because the Lord had said that he had prayed for Peter, Peter, conscious of his present affection [0339D] and of his fervent faith, but ignorant of the fall to come, does not believe that he can fall away from him. But he who knows what is in man, lest anyone should incautiously trust in his own state, or the more incautiously distrust because of a fall, as God foretells the manner, the time and the number of his denial, and as merciful promises the help of his own defence.

VERS. 35.-- *When I sent you.* He does not instruct the disciples by the same rule of living in the time of persecution as in the time of peace. For when the disciples were sent to preach, he commanded that they should take nothing on the way, ordaining namely that he who *announces the Gospel should live by the Gospel.* But when the moment of death was at hand, and that whole nation was persecuting both the shepherd and the flock together, he decrees a rule suited to the time, [0340A] permitting the necessaries of living, until, the madness of the persecutors being lulled, the time of evangelizing should return.

VERS. 36.-- *But now,* etc. In this an example is given to us also, that sometimes, when a cause presses, we may without fault leave off certain things from the strictness of our purpose. For example: if we journey through inhospitable regions, it is lawful to carry more for provision than we should have at home. *And he that hath not.* He bids them either to take the sword they have, or, not having it, to buy it; that readers may know that the power of resisting was not wanting to the disciples, but that rather the love of suffering was in the master; where, even if there were no other cause for drawing the sword, this one can suffice, that, the servant's ear having been cut off, it should be healed by the Lord's touch. In which we are warned [0340B] that we, being struck, should not maintain anger, but rising again should take up faith.

VERS. 38.-- *Behold two swords.* Two swords are brought forth, one of the New, the other of the Old Testament, wherewith we are fortified against the snares of the devil. And it is said: *It is enough.* Two swords suffice for a testimony that the Saviour suffered willingly. The one, which should teach both that boldness to contend for the Lord was in the apostles, and that, the ear having been torn off by the stroke of the sword, mercy and the power of healing were in the Lord even when he was about to die. The other, which, being by no means drawn from the scabbard, should show that they were not permitted to do all that they could for his defence.

VERS. 39.-- *And going out.* The Lord, about to be betrayed by a disciple, goes to the accustomed place, where he could easily be found, lest he should be thought to have feared death. And well does he lead out [0340C] to the Mount of Olives the disciples steeped in the mysteries of his blood and of his body, that he might mark out all who are baptized in his death as to be confirmed by the highest charism of the Holy Spirit.



## CHAPTER XXII. (cont.)

VERS. 40.-- *Pray.* It is impossible for a man not to be tempted; nor does he say, that you be not tempted, but, *lest you enter into temptation. Pray,* that is, that temptation may not overcome you and hold you within its nets.

VERS. 41.-- *And he himself,* etc. The precept of praying having been given to the apostles, he himself also was drawn away from them. He alone prays for all who alone was about to suffer for all, intimating that his prayer stands as far from ours as his passion also does. *A stone's cast,* etc. In this he mystically intimates that they should direct the stone unto the selfsame point, that is, [0340D] that they should carry as far as him the intention of the law, which had been written on stone: for as far as him can that stone reach, since *the end of the law is Christ to everyone that believeth unto justice [n: (Rom. X)]*.

VERS. 42.-- *He prayed, saying.* He prays not so much from fear of suffering as from mercy toward the former people, that he might not drink from that people the cup proffered him. Whence he says also pointedly: *This one,* that is, the cup of the people of the Jews, who have no excuse of ignorance in the matter of my death, since I am daily announced to them in the law and the prophets. If therefore it can come to pass that the multitude of the Gentiles believe without the destruction of the Jews, I refuse the passion. But if they must be blinded that others may see, not my will but thine be done.

*Father, if thou wilt,* etc. [n: (AMBR.)] For me he grieved, when for [0341A] himself he had nothing to grieve over. For he took upon him my sadness that he might lavish upon me his own gladness. Confidently therefore do I name sadness, I who preach the cross.

*Nevertheless,* etc. As the Lord drew near to the passion, he took upon himself the voice and the fear of the weak, that he might draw them off: and again through obedience he showed the fortitude of the mind, so that when what we do not wish to be done presses upon us, we may so ask through weakness that it be not done, that yet through fortitude we may be ready to fulfil the Creator's will rather than our own.

VERS. 44.-- *And his sweat became.* With death drawing near he expressed in himself the struggle of our mind, we who deservedly suffer a certain force unto terror, when after a little while we are about to find what may last for ever. [0341B] *Of blood running down to the ground.* By the earth watered with blood it is declared that he was already obtaining the effect of his prayer: namely, that he might cleanse by his own blood the faith of the disciples, which earthly frailty was still convicting; and that whatever of scandal that faith had endured from his death, all this he might himself blot out by dying; and moreover might raise up to life the whole world, dead in its sins.

VERS. 47.-- *That he might kiss him.* The Lord receives the kiss, not that he may teach us to dissemble, but that he might not seem to shun even the betrayer, and might the more affect the betrayer, to whom he would not deny the offices of love. Whence it is said: *With them that hated peace I was peaceable [n: (Psal. CXIX)]*.

VERS. 49.-- *Lord, shall we strike with the sword.* [0341C] The order is this: when they had asked, saying, *Lord, shall we strike with the sword,* then he answered: *Suffer ye thus far.* That is, let not what is to come move you. They are to be permitted to go forward thus far, that they may seize me and the things written of me be fulfilled. But between the delays of the words of those asking and of him answering, Peter, stirred by a greater eagerness of defence, struck on the Lord's behalf. But things which could be done at the same time could not be said at the same time. It is not therefore said, Suffer ye thus far, as though the deed of Peter had pleased the Lord, since in Matthew he is read to have said: *Put back thy sword into its place,* which it appears Jesus said after the deed.

VERS. 50.-- *The servant,* etc. Mystically: the servant is the people of the Jews, made over, namely unduly, to their princes, [0341D] who in the Lord's passion lost the right ear, that is, spiritual understanding. Content with the left, that is, with the cheapness of the letter, Peter not taking it away, but the divine judgment disclosing that it had been taken away. Which ear is restored, by the Lord's mercy, in those who chose rather to believe.

VERS. 52.-- *They were come to him,* etc. According to the other evangelists the chief priests and the magistrates had not come to Jesus, but had waited in the hall of Caiphas. But because these men so dealt with the Lord's slaying that they might seem as it were guiltless, since he was betrayed by a disciple, seized by a tribune and by the crowds, condemned by the governor, crucified by the Romans, and Barabbas even chosen by the peoples, the evangelist, wishing them above all [0342A] to be the guilty, by whose counsel all these things were being carried on, says that they came to seize the Saviour, they who came by the power of their own commanding. *As to a robber,* etc. It is foolish to seek with swords and clubs one who of his own accord offers himself to their hands, and to hunt out by a betrayer, as one lurking by night and shunning your eyes, him who daily teaches in the temple. But for this reason do you gather against me in the darkness, because your power, with which you are armed against the light, is in darkness.

VERS. 54.-- *Of the high priest.* Of Caiphas, who had bought for himself the pontificate of a single year at a price from the Roman prince: yet first to Annas, as John says. *He followed afar off.* Deservedly afar off, he who close at hand was about to deny. It signifies that the Church would imitate [0342B] Christ's passion far differently, because the Church suffers for herself, Christ for the Church.

VERS. 55.-- *A fire in the midst.* The fire of cupidity, kindled at the prompting of the malignant spirit, was arming in the hall of Caiphas the tongues of the faithless to deny and to blaspheme God. For what the malignant synod was carrying on within, in the house of the high priest, this the fire kindled outside amid the colds of the night was typically showing. Peter, numbed for the hour by this cold, desired as it were to be warmed by the coals of the servants, because he sought the solace of temporal advantage in the fellowship of the faithless. But this faithlessness he left when the Lord looked upon him; and after the resurrection, at the catch of fishes, when he had seen the coals laid and the fish laid upon them, he set his heart aflame with the coals of love.

[0342C]VERS. 56.-- *A certain maidservant.* Why does a maidservant betray him first, when men could better have recognized him? Unless that this sex might be shown to have sinned in the Lord's slaying, being about to redeem through the passion. And therefore a woman first received the mystery of the resurrection, and keeps the commands, that she might do away the old error of the transgression.

VERS. 57.-- *But he denied him.* Not on the mountain, not in the temple, not in his own house does Peter deny, but in the praetorium of the Jews, in the house of the high priest, where there is no truth, where Christ is taken and bound. What else than that he should err, whom the doorkeeper of the Jews let in? Eve ill led Adam in; a woman ill brought Peter in.

VERS. 59, 60.-- *Man, I know not what thou sayest.* Not only does he deny Christ who says that he is not the Christ, [0342D] but also he who, though he be one, denies that he is a Christian; and so is fulfilled what the Lord says: *Thou shalt deny me. He is Galliaeus.* Men of Jerusalem and Galileans are alike Hebrews and of one tongue, but each several province has its own properties, and cannot change the homeborn sound of its speech. *And immediately.* After the third denial the cock's crow follows. The sacraments of things are designated by the state of the times. At midnight he denies, at the cock's crow he repents. After the resurrection, under the light, him whom he had thrice denied he thrice professed that he loved; because he who had erred in the darkness of forgetfulness corrected it by the remembrance of the light now hoped for, and by the presence of that same true light fully raised up whatever he had supposed.

[0343A]VERS. 61.-- *And the Lord turning.* By his look he provoked him to tears, as it were bringing back to his mind how often he had denied, and what he had foretold him; nor could he remain in darkness upon whom the light of the world looked.

VERS. 62.-- *And going forth without.* Shut off from the council of the impious he washes his guilt, who was permitted not within, and for this reason, to waver: that in the chief of the Church a remedy of penance might be founded, and that no one might dare to trust in his own virtue.

VERS. 63.-- *They mocked him,* etc. The prophecy was fulfilled: *With a rod shall the cheek of the judge of Israel be smitten.* And likewise: *I have given my cheek to blows.* [var: Vulg. Is 50:6 *genas meas vellentibus*, "my cheeks to them that plucked them"] But he who was then beaten by the buffets of the Jews is now beaten by the blasphemies of false Christians. They veiled his face, not that he might not see their crimes, but that they might hide from themselves (as they once did [0343B] to Moses) the grace of the knowledge of him: *For if they believed Moses, they would believe the Lord also [n: (Joan. VIII)]*. This veil remains upon their hearts until this day, but from those who believe it has been taken away: when he died the veil of the temple was rent.

VERS. 64.-- *Prophesy.* A contumely against him who called himself a prophet. But both heretics and Jews, who until this day deny Christ, and evil Christians who exasperate him by reprobate deeds, while they suppose that their thoughts and works of darkness are not seen by him, say as it were in mockery: *Prophesy: Who is it that struck thee?*

VERS. 66.-- *If thou be the Christ.* They do not desire the truth, but prepare a calumny. They understood that the Christ would be born of David a mere man. [0343C] According to this, therefore, they ask, so that if he should say, I am the Christ, they might calumniate him for arrogating royal power to himself.

VERS. 67.-- *If I shall tell you.* He had often said that he was the Christ. He had also asked them how they said that Christ was the son of David, when David calls him Lord, that by this he might provoke them to believe him God: but they were willing neither to believe him speaking, nor to answer him asking, nor to let him go who was guiltless.

VERS. 69.-- *And hereafter it shall be.* They who sought to calumniate the seed of David hear yet more: that through contempt he is to come to glory.

VERS. 70.-- *You say.* So does he temper his answer [0343D] that he both speaks the truth and gives no opening to calumny. He chose rather to prove himself Christ the Son of God than to say it, that the ground of condemning might be taken away from those who confess the very thing they lay against him.

VERS. 71.-- *For we ourselves have heard.* Where he said: *The Son of man shall be sitting at the right hand of the power of God.* By their own sentence do they condemn themselves who deliver to death him whom by the testimony of their mouth and of their work they acknowledge to be God.



## CHAPTER XXIII.

VERS. 1.-- *They led him away.* They handed him over to the Roman power, that in this manner the Jews might show themselves strangers to his killing, wherein not their innocence but their madness is shown.

VERS. 3.-- *And Pilate.* etc. Since two things had been objected [0344A] against him, namely that he forbade the assigned dues to be rendered, and that he called himself Christ, Pilate was able to hear what he had said about the tributes: *Render the things that are Caesar's to Caesar,* and thence, counting this as an open falsehood of no weight, he asked only about what he did not know.

*Thou sayest it.* With the same word with which he answered the chief priests he answers the governor also, that by their own sentence both may be condemned.

VERS. 4.-- *I find no cause.* The day before he suffered, the Lord says to the disciples: *The prince of this world cometh, and he hath not anything in me [n: (Luc. XX)]*. But since the prince, that is Pilate, absolves him, in whom he finds nothing worthy of condemnation, see what the Jews do, who search out the truth not from love of equity, but labour to condemn the just man at the goad of envy.

[0344B][sic: VERS. 2.]-- *He stirreth up the people.* They accuse not him, but themselves. For to have taught the people, and by teaching to have stirred them from the former sluggishness of the body, and by such an act to have passed through from Galilee as far as Judea, that is the whole land of promise, from end to end, is a judgment not of crime but of virtue. And so Pilate, thinking that the Saviour was not even to be questioned about this, but rather having got the occasion, desires to make himself free from judging him.

VERS. 7.-- *And when he knew it.* Lest he should be forced to give sentence against a guiltless man, and one whom he knew to have been handed over out of envy, he sends him to Herod to be heard, that he might be either absolved or condemned by his own tetrarch; but, lest any excuse should remain to the Jews, Herod too, who was a Jew by birth and by religion, was permitted to show what he thought [0344C] about Christ, so that both regions, both Judea, in which Christ was born, and Galilee, in which he had been reared, might be shown to have conspired in his slaying.

*But he answered him nothing.* He was silent and did nothing, because both Herod's unbelief did not deserve to see divine things, and the Lord was avoiding ostentation. And in Herod are signified all the impious, who do not believe the law and the prophets, and cannot see the works of Christ in the Gospel. Therefore, while the chief priests accuse him, he answers few things before Pilate, none before Herod, lest, washing away the charge and being dismissed by the prince, he should put off the profit of the cross.

[sic: VERS. 14.]-- *And he mocked him.* That he is despised and mocked in a white garment signifies that for the sins of the whole world [0344D] the lamb was offered up in chaste flesh, and suffered, and was buried: that he is mocked here clothed in a white garment, and, according to the other Evangelists, in a purple or scarlet one, marks the twofold kind of martyrdom with which the Church is adorned in her sufferings, so that she is white in action, red with blood. For he himself, blooming with the little flowers of his members, brings forth lilies in peace, roses in war. *And he sent him back to Pilate.* He is sent to Herod, he is sent back to Pilate; and although each of the two does not pronounce him guilty, yet each complies with the goads of another's iniquity.

VERS. 12.-- *And they were made friends.* The abominable compact which Herod and Pilate struck in the killing of Christ, their successors keep by hereditary right, while Jews and Gentiles, disagreeing in mind as they do in race and religion, nevertheless agree in persecuting Christians [0345A] and in destroying the faith of Christ. In the type of Herod and Pilate, who were made friends through Jesus, the figure of the Jews and of the Gentiles is preserved: so that through Christ's passion a concord of both peoples was to come about, yet in such wise that the Gentile should first receive the kingdom of God, and pass on to the Jew the devotion of his own faith, that they too might clothe with the glory of his majesty the body of Christ, whom before they had despised.

VERS. 14.-- *You have presented.* Mark how great an eagerness Pilate has to release Jesus. First, when they accuse him, he says that he has found no cause in him; then, when they persist in what they have begun, he sends him to Herod, to explore whether he can find any wickedness in him, or whether he decides that he should be released. Then, when Herod's will is known, and no crime found in Jesus, [0345B] he seeks the sentence of the people, to whom one must be released at the Pasch. Again also a third time, wishing to release him, he asks. But the more carefully he seeks to release him, the more criminal does he find them for his death.

VERS. 15.-- *No, nor Herod either.* To reproach the hardness of the Jews or of the Gentiles, Pilate confesses that neither he nor Herod found anything in killing and mocking Christ, but that he complied with the shouts of another's cruelty.

VERS. 16.-- *I will chastise him therefore,* etc. Pilate absolves Jesus, whom he proves guiltless; but that the Scriptures might be fulfilled, he absolves indeed by judgment him whom nevertheless he crucifies in a mystery.

VERS. 18.-- *Away with this man,* etc. Because in place of Jesus they chose a robber, in place of the Saviour a murderer, in place of the giver of life [0345C] a taker of it away, they deservedly lost salvation and life; and they gave themselves over to robberies and seditions so far that they lost both the homeland and the kingdom which they had preferred to Christ, and to this day have not received back the liberty of soul or of body which they had sold. *Barabbas,* etc. Barabbas, who is interpreted the son of their master or of their father, is himself Antichrist, whom they will prefer to Christ, they to whom it is said: *You are of your father the devil.* Pilate is interpreted the mouth of the hammerer, and signifies the devil, who is the hammer of all matter, through whom God permits the vessels to be tested, and at last crushes him, when he breaks him in the final damnation.

VERS. 21.-- *Crucify him, crucify him.* Great cruelty: they seek not only to kill but to crucify him, [0345D] so that with hands and feet fastened to the wood he might be slain by a lingering death, lest the pain should end too quickly, and that he might long be seen upon the cross.

VERS. 22.-- *I will chastise him,* etc. This chastisement he offered to the desires of those abominable men by way of mockery and scourging, that they might not rage as far as crucifying. This both the words of John the evangelist and the very column testify, to which the Lord was bound, which to this day shows sure signs of the Lord's blood to those who look upon it. But although Pilate does this, the insatiable fury of the sacrilegious does not cease, but presses the harder, because they had seen the accusation which they had brought against the Lord emptied out by Pilate's careful questioning. At length the shameless men turn themselves to entreaties alone, [0346A] that what they had not been able to do by accusing or as it were by reasoning, they now accomplish by demanding and by shouting and by accomplishing.

VERS. 25.-- *And he released,* etc. The author of sedition and of murders is released to the Jews, that is, the devil, who for his fault had been driven from the homeland of light and sent into the prison of darkness; and therefore they cannot have peace, because they did not choose the author of peace.

VERS. 26.-- *They laid hold of one,* etc. First the Lord carried the cross, but afterwards it was laid upon Simon, whom they met as they went out; and this fittingly: *For Christ suffered for us, that you should follow his steps [n: (I Petr. II)]*. But he who wishes to follow him, let him take up his cross and follow. But because Simon [0346B] was not an Israelite but a Cyrenean, by him the Gentiles are designated, formerly strangers and guests to the testaments, but now in the faith citizens and of the household of God and coheirs of Christ; whence Simon is interpreted obedient, heir of Cyrene. But Simon came from the country place, that is from the village (*pagus*), from which come the pagans, who, strangers to the city of God, are ignorant of city conversation. But Simon, going forth from the village, carries the cross after Jesus, when the peoples of the Gentiles, having forsaken pagan rites, obediently embrace the footsteps of the Lord's passion.

VERS. 27.-- *There followed him,* etc. But they do not follow the cross with the same mind: the wicked follow that they may look with gladness on him dying; the women, that they might weep for him whom they desired to live, as about to die, as dying, and as dead. Nor is the lamentation of the women alone brought in on this ground, [0346C] since many men also were grieving, but because the female sex, as more contemptible, could the more freely display before the chief priests who were present what it felt against them. But because *the Lord knoweth who are his [n: (II Tim. II)]*, passing by the crowd of the raging people, he turns his eyes and his face to the women who loved and lamented him. *Who bewailed him.* They were sorrowing for a mortal life, which was to be ended by death, not for the blind men who were killing the physician.

VERS. 28.-- *Daughters of Jerusalem.* Note, when he calls them sons of Jerusalem, that not only those women who had come with him from Galilee, but also many women, citizens of that same city, had attached themselves to him. *Weep not.* Do not lament me as about to die, whose resurrection can quickly loose death, whose death will destroy death and the very [0346D] author of death. Rather wash yourselves and your offspring with worthy fountains of tears, lest with the faithless you be condemned in vengeance for my cross.

VERS. 29.-- *Behold, the days shall come.* The days of the Roman siege and captivity. It is natural, when captivity threatens and the enemy's slaughter rages through the fields and the cities, that all who are able to escape seek out any high or hidden refuges in which they may hide themselves.

VERS. 30.-- *Fall upon us.* This can be understood, by way of surplus, of those who in either sex have made themselves eunuchs for the kingdom of heaven.

VERS. 31.-- *For if in the green wood.* The green wood is Christ and his elect, the dry the impious and sinners; whence he says: If then I, who have committed no sin, who [0347A] am deservedly called the tree of life and bear twelve fruits of grace in each several month, do not go out of this world without the fire of the passion, what torments await those who are empty of fruits, and moreover do not fear to give the tree of life to the flames? And *if now is the time that judgment should begin at the house of God, and all who wish to live godly in Christ suffer persecution,* what is the end of those who do not believe the Gospel? [n: (I Petr. IV; I Tim. III)].

VERS. 32.-- *And there were led also.* That is fulfilled which was said: *And he was reputed with the wicked [n: (Isa. LIII)]*. In death he is reputed with the wicked, that in the resurrection he might quicken the wicked, he who, made man for the sake of men, gave to men the power to become the sons of God.

VERS. 33.-- *The place of Calvary.* Outside the gate of Jerusalem [0347B] were places in which the heads of the condemned were struck off, and they were called places of Calvary, that is, of the beheaded. There he is crucified, that, there was the ground of the condemned, the standards of martyrdom might be raised up. *And the robbers,* etc. Execrable in the deed is the iniquity of the Jews, which crucifies the Redeemer of all as though a robber; yet in the mystery he is a good robber, who lay in wait for the devil, to carry off his vessels. By him who is on the right hand is signified the mortification of the flesh for heavenly glory; by him who is on the left, the mortification of the flesh for some cause of this world.

VERS. 34.-- *And Jesus,* etc. Because Luke set himself to write Christ's priesthood, rightly does the Lord intercede with God for his persecutors by the right of a priest, and open paradise to the robber who confesses him; nor does he pray in vain, [0347C] but obtains it for those who believed after his passion. He does not pray for those who through envy and pride understood him to be the Son of God, and deny and crucify him, but for those who, having a zeal of God, but not according to knowledge, knew not what they did.

*Dividing.* Of these John speaks more fully. The fourfold garment signifies the Church, which consists of four parts, spread through the whole world, distributed equally, that is, harmoniously, to all the parts. The tunic allotted signifies the unity of all the parts, which is held together by the bond of charity. *Lots.* In the lot the grace of God is commended. For thus it came in one to all, when the lot pleased all; because the grace of God also comes to all in unity, and when the lot is cast it is credited not to anyone's person or merits, but to the hidden [0347D] judgment of God.



## CHAPTER XXIII. (cont.)

VERS. 35.-- *He saved others.* By their own sentence they condemn themselves, who even against their will confess that he saved others. He could indeed have saved himself, but because he himself is the Christ, the elect of God, he was unwilling to come down from the cross. For he who came for this, that he might be crucified for us, for this reason neglected to save himself from the cross: because by dying he took care to save, along with the rest of sinners, even those who crucified him.

VERS. 36.-- *And the soldiers also mocked him,* etc. Note that the Jews deride the name of Christ and of the Son of God, handed down to them by the authority of Scripture. But the soldiers, ignorant of the Scriptures, insult not Christ the elect of God, but the king of the Jews. *And offering him vinegar.* Vinegar [0348A] were the Jews, degenerating from the wine of the patriarchs. With this vinegar the soldiers give the Lord to drink, whom at their prompting they deliver to death.

VERS. 38.-- *And there was also,* etc. These three tongues were pre-eminent above the rest: Hebrew, because of the Jews glorying in the law; Greek, because of the wisdom of the Gentiles; Latin, because of the Romans holding command. Therefore, will they or nill they, the Jews, every kingdom of the world, all worldly wisdom, and all the divine sacraments of the law bear witness that Jesus is the emperor of those who believe and confess him. *This is the king of the Jews.* The title which witnesses that Christ is king is set not below but above the cross, because although on the cross he was suffering in the weakness of a man, yet above the cross he was shining in the majesty of a king; and therefore, when he had been sacrificed, the title was set above him, that it might lie open to all who wished to read, [0348B] that is, to believe, that through the cross he did not lose his empire but strengthened it.

VERS. 40.-- *He rebuked him.* The rebuke itself reveals the coming scandal of the cross even among believers, of whom some are on the right hand, others on the left.

VERS. 42.-- *Lord, remember me.* Great grace shines out in this robber. He has no member free from the torment except his heart and his tongue; the whole of what he has free, he offers. With the heart he believes, with the mouth he confesses. He had faith, who believed that he would reign whom he saw dying beside him. He had hope, who asked for entrance. He held charity firmly, who both convicted the robber concerning his own iniquity and preached to him the life which he had come to know. Great is his virtue: he confesses him whom he sees dying in human weakness, whom the apostles were denying, [0348C] whom they had seen working miracles by divine power.

VERS. 43.-- *Amen I say to thee.* A most beautiful example of a conversion to be sought after, in that pardon is so quickly loosed to the robber, and the grace is more abundant than the petition. For the Lord always bestows more than he is asked. He was asking: *Remember me,* etc. The Lord says: *This day thou shalt be with me,* etc. Quickly does the Lord pardon, because that man is quickly converted.

VERS. 44.-- *And there was darkness,* etc. The sun drew back its rays, either that it might not see the Lord hanging, or that the impious blasphemers might not enjoy its light. And note that at the sixth hour, that is, when the sun was about to withdraw from the centre of the world, he is crucified; at daybreak, that is, when the sun rises, he rises again, because *he died for our sins, and rose again for our justification [n: (Rom. XVIII)]*. [var: Vulg. Rom 4:25 *qui traditus est propter delicta nostra*, "who was delivered up for our offences"] When Adam [0348D] had sinned after midday, that is, when the light of faith had declined, the voice of the Lord is heard; at the same moment of time, therefore, at which he had then shut paradise against the sinning Adam, the Lord now opens it to the penitent robber.

VERS. 45, 46.-- *The veil of the temple,* etc. This was done as the Lord breathed forth his spirit, but Luke anticipates, wishing to join the miracle of the sun to the miracle of the veil. And the veil is rent, that the ark of the testament and all the sacraments of the law which were covered might appear and pass over to the Gentiles. Josephus says that the angelic powers, once the guardians of the temple, then cried out together: *Let us flee from these seats. Father,* etc. Calling upon the Father, he declares himself the Son of God. Commending his spirit, he intimates not a failure of power, but confidence in the same [0349A] together with a share of power. For he loves to give glory to the Father, wherein he forms us, that we may give glory to the Creator.

VERS. 47.-- *And the centurion seeing.* Seeing that he thus had the power of sending forth his spirit, which none has but the Maker of souls, the very man who had crucified him, in the very scandal of the passion, confesses him the Son of God, whom the Jews after so many miracles refused to believe. Whence by the centurion is designated the faith of the Church, which, when the veil of the heavenly mysteries had been unlocked by the Lord's death, straightway affirms him just and the Son of God, while the Synagogue is silent.

VERS. 48.-- *Striking their breasts.* That they strike their breasts as a sign of penitence and of mourning can be understood in two ways. For either they were grieving that one whose life they had loved [0349B] had been unjustly slain; or, remembering that they had procured his death, they were trembling that he was the more glorified in that death. But whatever cause compelled them to beat the breast, let us see the distance between nation and nation. The Gentiles, fearing God as the Lord died, glorify him with the voice of open confession; the Jews, only striking their breasts, go home in silence.

VERS. 50.-- *Named Joseph.* Joseph is worthy, through the nobility of his worldly power, to receive the body; through the justice of his merits, to bury it.

VERS. 52.-- *This man went,* etc. Not the apostles, but Joseph, just and steadfast, and Nicodemus, in whom was no guile, bury him. Such too is the burial of Christ, which has in it no fraud or iniquity; and in this the shuffling of the Jews is blocked. For if [0349C] the apostles had buried him, they would say that he was not buried whom they said was carried off.

VERS. 53.-- *In a winding-sheet.* From the simple burial of the Lord the ambition of the rich is condemned, who cannot do without riches even when dead.

Mystically: he wraps Jesus in a clean winding-sheet who receives him with a pure mind. Hence also the custom of the Church holds that the sacrifice of the altar be celebrated not on silk, not on dyed cloth, but on pure linen.

*And laid him.* Well, then, is Christ laid in the tomb of a just man, that the Son of man may have where to lay his head, and may rest in the dwelling of justice. Well in a new one, that he who receives Christ may walk in newness of life. No one buries Christ save he who believes in Christ.

*In a monument that was hewn.* The Lord alone is enclosed in the tomb, [0349D] that his burial and resurrection might be shown unlike the frailty of our nature. He was buried, but for as long as he willed; he rose again, but when he willed. *Wherein not yet.* If he were laid in a monument where another had been laid, another would be feigned to have risen. Hewn in the rock, lest, had it been built of many stones, and the foundations of the tomb dug away, he should be said to have been carried off by theft.

VERS. 54.-- *And it was the day,* etc. On the sixth day man was made; on the sabbath God rested. Crucified therefore on the sixth day, he fulfils the secret of the preparation. Resting on the sabbath in the sepulchre, he awaits the event of the resurrection. So must we in the sixth age of the world suffer for the Lord. In the seventh, that is, when we die, the body remains in the tomb, [0350A] the soul with the Lord in rest after its labours, until in the eighth the bodies rise again.

VERS. 55.-- *The women.* When the other acquaintances of Jesus withdrew after the body was taken down, the women alone, who loved more closely, were looking upon the office of the burial, that at a fitting time they might offer the gift of their devotion, awaiting what Jesus had promised; and therefore they were the first to see him.

VERS. 56.-- *And returning.* Having looked upon the Lord's burial, they return and prepare spices and ointments: they who, having read, heard and called to mind the Lord's passion, turn themselves to the works of the virtues in which Christ delights, and on the sabbath keep silence. Being about to come to the Lord after the sabbath with their gifts, when the preparation-day of the present life is finished they await, rejoicing in blessed rest, the time [0350B] of the resurrection, at which they run to meet Christ with the fragrant spices of their deeds. *And on the sabbath,* etc. It had been commanded that the silence of the sabbath be kept from evening until evening. Therefore the women, when the Lord was buried, so long as it was lawful to work, are busied on the preparation-day in making ready the ointments until the setting of the sun; and, the sabbath being over at the going down of the sun, that coming in the morning they might anoint him, because on the evening of the sabbath, the moment of night already coming on, they could not go to the monument.



## CHAPTER XXIV.

VERS. 1.-- *And on the first day of the week,* etc. The first of the sabbath is the same as the first day of the sabbath, that is, the one day from the day of the sabbath or of the sabbaths, which is now called the Lord's day. In that the women came very early to the monument, [0350C] a great fervour of charity in seeking and finding the Lord is shown.

Mystically: an example is given, that with faith enlightened and the darkness of the vices shaken off, we should approach the sacred body of the Lord. For the sepulchre is a figure of the altar, on which the mystery of the Lord's body and blood is celebrated. The spices which they bring signify the odour of the virtues and the sweetness of prayers, with which we ought to draw near to the altar.

VERS. 2.-- *The stone rolled back.* The rolling back of the stone signifies the unlocking of the sacraments of Christ, which were covered by the veil of the letter. The law was written on stone; and when its covering is taken away, the body of the Lord is not found dead, but is proclaimed living: *because although we have known Christ according to the flesh, yet now we know him no more* [0350D] [n: (II Cor. V)]. And he was able to go forth from a closed sepulchre who went forth from a closed womb.

VERS. 4.-- *Cast down in mind.* They are cast down in mind, because they were both amazed that a stone of immense size had been rolled back, and grieved that so venerable a body was not found. As, when the Lord was tempted in the wilderness, angels ministered to him when the victory was accomplished, so now, after death had been conquered, angels came, who announce the glory of the triumphant one not only by word but also by their shining habit. And as they stood by the body in the sepulchre, so also they daily stand by the consecrated body upon the altar.

VERS. 5.-- *They bowed down their countenance.* Note that neither are the holy women read to have fallen to the earth, nor any [0351A] of the saints to have adored prostrate on the earth at the Lord's resurrection; whence the custom began in the Church, at paschal time, not to bend the knees in prayer, but to pray with the countenance bowed toward the earth.

VERS. 6.-- *Remember.* On the third day, as we have from this place, the Lord foretold among the disciples, and to the women also who were following him, that he would rise again. On the preparation-day, at the ninth hour, he expired; in the morning, on the first day of the sabbath, he rose again. And deservedly did he lie one day and two nights in the sepulchre, because he set the light of his own single death (which was in the flesh only) against the darkness of our double death, which was in the body and in the soul, and healed both of ours. For if he had taken both upon himself, he would have freed us from neither.

VERS. 8.-- *And they remembered.* As the women, standing at [0351B] the Lord's monument, remember the words which he had spoken beforehand concerning his dispensation, so we, amid the mysteries of the Lord's passion that are to be celebrated, ought to remember not only the passion but the resurrection also.

VERS. 9.-- *They told.* As in the beginning the woman was to the man the author of guilt, and the man was the executor of the error, so now she who had first tasted death saw the resurrection; and lest she should bear among men the reproach of a perpetual guilt, she who had poured guilt into the man poured grace also into him. And because the inferior sex, weaker for carrying a thing through, has not the constancy of preaching, the office of evangelizing is committed to men.

VERS. 11.-- *And they did not believe.* That the disciples are slow to believe was not so much their weakness as [0351C] our own firmness to come; for while they doubted, the resurrection is shown by many proofs, and while we, reading, come to know them, what else are we but made solid out of their doubting?

VERS. 12.-- *But Peter rising up.* John relates that both he and Peter ran together, and that, while Peter went inside into the monument and saw the linen cloths and the napkin, John himself, stooping down, saw the linen cloths and did not go in. But it is to be understood that Peter first, stooping down, saw what Luke relates and John passes over in silence; but that afterwards he entered, that he might discern the inner things more carefully, yet entered before John went in.

VERS. 13.-- *Named Emmaus.* Emmaus is itself the metropolis, a notable city of Palestine, which, after the storming [0351D] of Judea, was restored under the prince Marcus Aurelius Antonius, and changed its name along with its condition.

VERS. 15, 16.-- *While they talked together.* The Lord draws near and accompanies them as they speak of him, that he may both kindle in their minds the faith of the resurrection, and fulfil what he had promised he would do: *Where there are two or three gathered together in my name, there am I in the midst of them [n: (Matth. XXVIII)]*. *Drawing near,* etc. *Their eyes,* etc. He appeared indeed in his own form, but did not show the form which they might recognize. As they both loved him within and yet doubted, so he himself is present outside and at hand, and does not show who he is. To those who speak of him, then, he grants his presence; but from those who doubt of him he takes away the appearance by which he might be known.

[0352A]VERS. 18, 19, 20.-- *Art thou a stranger,* etc. *To whom,* etc. They think him a stranger whom they do not recognize. And truly he was a stranger to them, from whose frailty he now stood far off through the glory of the resurrection, and from whose faith, ignorant of the resurrection, he remained an alien. *A man, a prophet,* etc. *And how they delivered him up,* etc. They confess him a prophet and a great one; of the Son of God they are silent, either because they do not yet believe perfectly, or, if they believe, they fear to be delivered into the hands of the Jews, not knowing with whom they speak.

VERS. 21.-- *But we hoped,* etc. In a manner they refute themselves, in that they had hoped for redemption in him whom they had seen dead, nor did they believe that he would rise again; and they grieve most of all that he was slain without guilt, whom they had known to be innocent; and from this they were walking sorrowful [0352B].

VERS. 22.-- *They affrighted us,* etc. They are rightly said *teruisse* those to whom they added more sadness about the body not found than they bring gladness, by the resurrection announced, wherewith they might be refreshed.

VERS. 24.-- *Some of us went,* etc. Since above he said that Peter ran to the monument, and now Cleophas says: *Some of us went to the monument,* etc., it is to be understood that two went together. But he mentioned Peter alone first, because Mary announced it to him first.

VERS. 25.-- *O foolish and slow of heart.* Here a twofold necessity of being humbled presses upon us, who are neither taught in the Scriptures as much as we ought, nor as intent as is fitting on fulfilling the things we know. For if [0352C] Moses and the prophets spoke of Christ, and had foretold that he would enter through passion into glory, how does he glory that he is a Christian who neither searches out in the Scriptures how they pertain to Christ, nor desires to reach through sufferings the glory which he wishes to have with Christ?

VERS. 28, 29.-- *And he himself,* etc. *Stay with us,* etc. He showed himself such in body as he was among them in mind. They were to be tested, whether they would love at least as a stranger him whom they did not yet love as God. But because they cannot be strangers to the brightness, they with whom the Truth was walking call him to lodging, nay compel him. From which example it is gathered that strangers are not only to be invited to lodging, but even to be drawn in.

[0352D]VERS. 30.-- *He broke it,* etc. Him whom they did not know in the exposition of Scripture they knew in the breaking of bread; because not the hearers of the law but the doers shall be justified, and the truth is better understood by working than by hearing.

VERS. 31.-- *Their eyes were opened,* etc. The appearance of weakness is withdrawn from the eyes of the flesh, that the glory of the resurrection may begin to appear to their minds.

VERS. 32.-- *And they said,* etc. Mystically: for this reason another likeness was shown them in Christ, that they might not know him save in the breaking of bread; so that all may understand that they do not recognize Christ unless they become partakers of his body, that is, of the Church, whose unity the Apostle commends in the sacrament of the bread, saying: [0353A] *We being many are one bread, one body [n: (I Cor. X)]*. When therefore he reaches out the blessed bread, their eyes are opened that they may know him, and the impediment is removed which was upon their eyes from Satan, that Jesus might not be recognized. And this impediment the Lord allows to remain until the sacrament of the bread be come to. But when the unity of the body has been shared, the enemy's impediment is taken away, that Christ may be recognized.

*Burning. [n: (GREG.)]* When the word had been heard, the heart, before cold with the numbness of unbelief and of fear, was kindled with the fire of the Holy Spirit, so that it now burns with a heavenly desire and stretches itself out to believe the truth. By as many precepts as a man is instructed, by so many torches, as it were, is he set aflame.

VERS. 34.-- *The Lord is risen.* Already there was a report [0353B] that Jesus had risen, made by those women and by Simon Peter, to whom he had appeared; these two found them speaking of these things. But they themselves also had heard earlier that he had risen; yet they feared to say it on the way, when they said only that angels had been seen by the women. For, not knowing him with whom they were speaking, they could fear that they might fall into the hands of the Jews, if they should say anything anywhere about Christ's resurrection.

VERS. 36.-- *Jesus stood,* etc. This appearance is that of which John says [n: (Joan. XX)]: *When it was late that day,* etc., *and he said to them: Peace be to you.* But as to John's saying that Thomas was not with them, while Luke says that these two found the eleven gathered together, and those who were with them, it is to be understood that Thomas had gone out from there before the Lord [0353C] appeared to them as they spoke of these things.

VERS. 37, 38.-- *To see a spirit. And he said to them.* The disciples had known him to be true man, but after he died they do not believe that true flesh could rise from the sepulchre on the third day. They think therefore that they see the spirit which he sent forth in the passion; but the Lord takes away this error.

*Why are you troubled. [n: (AMBR.)]* The apostles believed, but nevertheless they are troubled, etc., *as far as* that the Lord, with the doors shut, poured himself in unforeseen with his body.

*Thoughts,* etc. False ones, not descending from above, but rising up in the heart like an evil weed. I require the faith which I planted in you; what has sprung from yourselves I condemn. If there is no truth of the resurrection, [0353D] the fruit of the passion is lost.

VERS. 39.-- *Handle and see.* By many proofs he persuades them of the resurrection, offering himself both to be seen by their eyes and to be handled by their hands; and while by their handling he shows bones and flesh, he marks the state of his own resurrection and of ours, in which our body will be both subtle through the effect of a spiritual power, and palpable through the truth of its nature. After the resurrection the Lord showed in his body two contrary things: both what is palpable, of the same nature, that he might form them to faith, and what is incorruptible, of another glory, that he might invite them to the reward.

VERS. 40.-- *He showed them his hands,* etc. That by showing the scar of his wounds he might heal the wound of unbelief; which [0354A] scars he both kept then and will keep in the judgment. First, to establish faith in the resurrection; then, that, entreating the Father for us, he may always show what kind of death he endured for man; thirdly, that he may intimate to those redeemed by his death how mercifully they have been helped, by setting before them the tokens of that same death. But lastly, that in the judgment he may declare how justly the impious are condemned, by showing the scar which he received from them. Not therefore from any inability to heal them did he keep the scars, but that he might carry about the perpetual triumph of his victory.

VERS. 41.-- *Have you here anything.* To convey the truth of the resurrection, he willed not only to be touched but to eat with them; not that either he or we after the resurrection ought to need food, but that he might make manifest that he was a body, [0354B] not a spirit; he ate by power, not by necessity. In one way the thirsting earth soaks up water, in another the hot ray of the sun: the former by need, the latter by power.

VERS. 42.-- *Of a broiled fish.* The broiled fish is the Mediator himself who suffered, taken in the waters of the human race by the snare of death, broiled at the time of the passion. He is also to us a honeycomb in the resurrection. The honeycomb in the wax, that is, the Godhead in the manhood. In the eating of this honey it is signified that he receives into his body, unto eternal rest, those who, when they feel tribulations for God, do not withdraw from the love of the inward sweetness. They who are broiled here shall there be filled with the true sweetness.



## CHAPTER XXIV. (cont.)

VERS. 43.-- *And when he had eaten.* He takes away all evasions [0354C]: he was seen, he was touched, he ate; and lest in anything he should seem to have deluded the human senses, he puts his hands to the Scriptures, so that even if it should seem, as the pagans say, that by magic arts he was able to do what he willed, they may at least see that he could not have prophesied before he was born.

VERS. 45.-- *Then he opened.* After the sight, after the touch, after the law recalled to mind, he opened their understanding, that they might understand what they see and read; then, after the truth of his own body had been commended, he commends the unity of the Church: *And that there should be preached in his name.*

VERS. 46.-- *It behoved,* etc. It behoved that the ministers who were to preach penance and remission [0354D] unto all nations should begin from Jerusalem. Not only because to them were entrusted the oracles of God, and because theirs is the adoption of sons, and the glory the testaments, and the law-giving; but for this reason also, that the Gentiles, entangled in various errors and crimes, might by this token above all of the divine loving-kindness be roused to the hope of pardon: that he grants pardon to those who crucified the Son of God.

VERS. 49.-- *But stay you.* After he had perfectly instructed the disciples about the power of preaching, although he could of a sudden, if he willed, have strengthened them, he put it off, saying: *But stay you.* That he might give an example to those who follow, lest the imperfect presume to preach before the time. He sits in the city who binds himself within the cloisters of the mind, lest by speaking he wander abroad outside; so that when he has [0355A] been perfectly clothed with the divine power, then he may go forth as it were out of himself, by instructing others.

VERS. 50.-- *And he led them out.* Passing over all that was done through the forty days, he silently joins to the first day of the resurrection the last, on which he ascended into heaven. And beautifully, being about to bless the disciples, he leads them out to Bethany, which is called the house of obedience: because he who came down on account of the disobedience of the perverse, ascended on account of the obedience of the converted.

VERS. 53.-- *In the temple.* That in the place of prayer, amid the devotions of praise, with hearts made ready in all things, they might await the promised coming of the Holy Spirit. So [0356A] let us, after the solemnities of the passion and the resurrection have been celebrated in Jerusalem, that is, in the vision of peace, seek Bethany with the Lord for our leader, that with a quiet mind we may be steeped in the sacraments of his body and blood, and may take care to be a house of obedience.

*Praising and blessing.* Luke, who took upon himself to set forth Christ's priesthood more fully than the rest, and therefore began his Gospel from the ministry of the temple through the priesthood of Zachary, beautifully completed it in the devotion of the temple, when he concludes with the apostles there, ministers, namely, of the new priesthood to come, not in the blood of victims, but in the praise of God and in blessing.
